                        The Rule of 40 as a Price Line:
           Efficient Frontiers, Economic Profit and the Limits of AI
                                  Automation
                                      Akshaya Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy*

                                                       October 2026



                                                           Abstract
        The Rule of 40 (revenue growth plus profit margin of at least 40%) is a widely used benchmark for
        software firms. We model the set of attainable growth-margin pairs. Its efficient frontier is concave, with
        an intercept equal to the margin a firm earns when it grows with its market, and Rule-of-40 and Rule-
        of-X lines are price lines whose tangency with it gives the maximal score: the intercept plus market
        growth plus a growth surplus that vanishes as market share saturates and draws on a finite budget of
        share headroom. In value terms the Rule of X is a rule of economic profit, and the weight a value-
        maximizing firm puts on growth is the marginal value of revenue, not the screening weights of two to
        three. Using SEC filings as first reported for 293 software firms from 2010 to 2025, we find that the
        top of the distribution of reported scores fell by 12 to 15 points between 2016-19 and 2023-25 as fast
        growers became scarcer, and that on the organic frontier, which removes acquired growth, a point of
        growth has come with about half a point less margin among the best performers and growth spending
        implies a weight on growth of 7 to 13. Charging the full costs of running AI, complete automation at
        fixed prices keeps a mature asset-light firm’s cash-basis score below 100 unless its market grows faster
        than about 42% a year if compute absorbs 20% of revenue (26 to 72% over the credible range of that
        share), and a Rule of 1000 can be sustained only briefly. How fast a firm approaches its limit is set
        by its capacity to execute risky transitions: under our scenario assumptions an exceptional firm embeds
        about 23% of its automation program by 2036 under central execution and all of it by 2033 under fast
        execution. We also show how acquired growth and interest distort the score. Where entry is free, the
        limit is set by what stays scarce, not by what is automated.


1     Introduction
The Rule of 40 holds that a software company’s revenue growth rate plus its profit margin should reach
40%. It is a widely used benchmark in software investing (Feld, 2015; Depeyrot and Heap, 2018), and it has
spawned variants: a “Rule of 60” for AI-enabled software (Vista Equity Partners, 2026), growth-weighted
versions such as the “Rule of X” (Deeter and Bondy, 2024), and the suggestion that firms reinvesting in
    * Independent researcher. Correspondence: bs.akshay@gmail.com. This paper was produced in a documented collaboration

Commercial use of either requires the author’s prior written permission.


                                                               1
AI may for a time have to settle for a “Rule of 30” (Lipman et al., 2026). Reported scores at the top have
reached far above 40: Palantir reported a score of 155 for the second quarter of 2026 on its adjusted margin
(Palantir Technologies Inc., 2026). The practitioner discussion does not say which combinations of growth
and margin a firm can attain, what the frontier of attainable combinations looks like, whether 40 is a point
on that frontier or a line that crosses it, or how high the score could go if artificial intelligence automates
most of the work a software company does.
     This paper asks four questions. What is the efficient frontier of growth and profitability, and how does
the Rule of 40 relate to it? What is the theoretical limit of the score: is it a Rule of 100, a Rule of 1000, or
something else? How might that limit evolve over the next two, five and ten years as AI automates more of
the firm? And what conditions must a firm meet to approach it?
     We answer with a model, an empirical frontier and a calibration. In the model (Section 3), a firm grows
faster than its market only by spending, and the returns to spending diminish with the amount spent and with
the share of the market the firm already holds. The efficient frontier of attainable (growth, margin) pairs is
then decreasing and concave, with an intercept equal to the margin the firm earns when it simply grows with
its market. The Rule of 40 and the Rule of X are iso-value lines, not frontiers, and the maximal score is
reached where one of them is tangent to the frontier. In closed form it is the margin intercept, plus market
growth, plus a growth surplus that vanishes as the firm’s share approaches its cap and is paid for out of a
finite budget of share headroom, so extreme scores are necessarily transient. Capital intensity lowers the
cash-basis score in proportion to optimal growth. The model separates two weights on growth that practice
treats as one: the weight investors use to screen firms, which reflects the duration of the revenue stream and
is consistent with the weights of two to three in use, and the weight a value-maximizing firm acts on, the
marginal value of a dollar of revenue, which is typically far larger. In value terms the Rule of X is a rule
of economic profit plus growth at the net value of growth, so the frontier applies to any firm whose growth
is bought with expensed spend at diminishing returns, and the cash Rule of 40 penalizes capital-intensive
growth whatever its return (Proposition 13). Fast growers gain most from automating the growth engine and
retention, mature firms from automating overhead; under free entry the long-run score is pinned down by
market growth, the cost of capital and the rent on whatever input rivals cannot replicate; and the frontier
estimated across firms is drawn through the points firms chose, so it does not identify the trade-off any one
firm faces.
     Two extensions bring the costs and the pace of automation into the frontier. First, full automation
replaces labor with compute, fees, an accountability floor and the recurring costs of maintaining, securing
and supervising AI. We give each floor a closed form and show that the accountability floor contains an
expected loss from misstatements that control layers sharing one AI model jointly miss, which neither
cheaper inference nor scale removes (Proposition 10). Second, a firm reaches its full-cost frontier only
through transitions that fail, roll back and draw on limited capacities to absorb change, decide and review
deployments, and that new leaders, strategies and reorganizations can disrupt. In the frontier attainable by
a given date, risk acts like a cut in capacity: it delays the approach to the static frontier without lowering it,
while partial realization of benefits lowers the level to which the firm converges (Propositions 11 and 12).
     The empirical analysis (Sections 4 and 5) uses SEC XBRL filings, taken as first reported, for 293
prepackaged-software firms over fiscal years 2010 to 2025, including firms later acquired or delisted; a
broader sample of 541 software and internet firms serves as a robustness check. Because both growth and
margin are desirable, we use partial-frontier statistics whose meaning does not depend on sample size, and
we report two estimands: observed scores for statements about reported scores, and the organic frontier,
which removes acquired growth, for statements that confront the model, whose growth is bought with ex-


                                                        2
pensed spend within one market rather than with capital across many. Six findings stand out. First, on the
organic frontier, among the best performers a point of growth has come with about half a point less margin
(upper-decile slopes of −0.59 on the GAAP basis and −0.43 after stock-based compensation), far short
of the one-for-one trade the Rule of 40 line suggests; in observed scores the GAAP slope is significantly
flatter (−0.46, with −0.37 after stock-based compensation), because acquired growth flattens it. Within
firms, margin and growth are nearly unrelated. Second, the level of growth spending among organic grow-
ers implies that firms weight growth at roughly 7 to 13, well above screening weights and consistent with
the marginal value of revenue; the data do not identify how fast the returns to spending diminish. Third,
the upper tail of observed cash-basis scores declines with firm size, by about 6 points per tenfold increase
in revenue, but the gradient disappears once acquired growth is removed, so it is not evidence of saturation.
Fourth, since 2023 the upper tail of the cash-basis score has been 11 points lower for capital-intensive firms
on the organic frontier (15 in observed scores), with no comparable gap on the GAAP basis. Fifth, between
2016–19 and 2023–25 the 95th percentile of reported scores fell by 12 to 15 points (12 on both bases of the
organic frontier) while the number of fast growers fell, and the best margins attainable at a given growth rate
showed no detectable change, except at the thinly populated 40% threshold of the organic frontier. Sixth, on
the organic frontier firms near the frontier earn about 33 points more operating margin than typical firms at
the same growth rate; among firms growing 40 to 60% a year, 28 points of that band’s 37-point gap come
from lower selling and marketing expense, while among slow growers the difference lies mostly in gross
margin and overhead. Observed scores give the same picture.
     Combining model and estimates (Section 7), we find that automation alone does not set the limit of the
rule. Inverting the model firm by firm on the organic frontier shows that the median top score of 2023–25 was
at its durable level if all selling and marketing expense buys growth, and that up to 39% of it was a transient
growth surplus if none does; the best organic scores were partly transient only if part of that spending does
not buy growth (in observed scores the transient share is 12 to 45%). Under full automation, and holding
prices fixed, the cash-basis score of a mature firm is bounded by its after-tax margin net of compute costs,
fees, an accountability floor and the recurring costs of maintaining, securing and supervising AI, plus market
growth net of capital needs. If compute absorbs 20% of revenue at full automation, a scenario value, that
bound is 63 to 88 points for market growth of 5 to 30%, so that an asset-light firm exceeds 100 only if its
market grows faster than about 42% a year (32% with favorable parameters, 59% if compute stays as costly
as projected for AI products in 2027). Every threshold is conditional on the scenario compute share: over
its credible range of 1 to 55% of revenue the central threshold runs from 26 to 72%. A Rule of 1000 is not
a steady state: it requires 7 to 21 times the demand pull that the surplus of today’s best firms implies, and
the share-headroom budget limits how long any such surplus can last. The pace of approach is limited by
execution. Under central execution an exceptional firm embeds about 23% of its automation program by
2036, and under fast execution all of it by 2033; while it is transforming, the costs of transitions in flight
lower its cash score by about 3.0 to 3.9 points. Under our scenario assumptions, exceptional asset-light
firms would reach maximal scores of roughly 40 to 156 in 2028, 35 to 123 in 2031 and 31 to 122 in 2036,
of which about 28 to 77, 27 to 84 and 27 to 98 points would be durable; these are scenario bounds, not
forecasts. Where entry is free, the score falls toward market growth plus the return on capital, 7.5 points
in our example, and reaching 100 requires a rent of 92.5 points of revenue on a scarce input. The levers
that move the limit are therefore a non-replicable input, exceptional demand pull, capital that earns its keep,
pricing that captures falling unit costs, small accountability and full-cost floors and a fast-growing market;
a sustained Rule of 100 requires them jointly to push the durable score above 100.
     Measurement matters as much as economics at the top of the distribution. Adjusted margins that exclude


                                                      3
stock-based compensation, capitalized computing capacity, gross revenue recognition and run-rate revenue
can each push a reported score above the ceiling without any change in economics. Growth that is bought
rather than built is the largest such distortion: acquired revenue enters the growth term, while its price, paid
in investing cash flow, never touches a cash score, and acquisition debt moves interest below the operating
margin. Charged properly, acquired growth enters the score at the net weight X − kacq , where kacq is the price
paid per dollar of acquired revenue, and each acquisition buys a fresh budget of share headroom (Section 6).
For Bending Spoons, a serial acquirer that listed in 2026, the Rule-of-40 score in the second quarter of 2026
was about 160 as reported and about 37 on the company’s own measure of organic growth. Its 2025 GAAP
score, recomputed from its Form F-1 filed on 8 June 2026, is 115.9, 105.0 after interest and 84.4 to 92.8
when its investing outflows are charged as an annuity over a scenario life, with a lower bound of −24.8 when
they are charged in full in the year paid. With net revenue retention of 91% to 95%, its existing subscription
base shrinks by 5.1 to 9.4% a year, so that, absent new customers, standing still requires acquiring revenue
at that rate. Acquired growth is exactly why the organic frontier, not the observed score, is the estimand that
confronts the model.
     The paper builds on several literatures (Section 2). The tangency between a growth–profit transforma-
tion curve and a valuation line goes back to Marris (1964), Solow (1971) and Odagiri (1981); our growth
technology resembles the advertising models of Vidale and Wolfe (1957) and Nerlove and Arrow (1962),
and recent evidence that firms grow by acquiring customers at roughly constant markups (Fitzgerald et al.,
2024; Argente et al., 2025) supports it. The only peer-reviewed academic study of the rule we are aware of
evaluates it as an investment screen (Lee, 2024). Empirically, we adapt partial-frontier estimators (Cazals
et al., 2002; Aragon et al., 2005) to a frontier on which both outputs are desirable, and we map the effects
of AI automation (Aghion et al., 2019; Acemoglu, 2025; Babina et al., 2024) onto the parameters that de-
termine a firm’s score. A companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026) derives those parameters
from the tasks a firm automates; this paper does not depend on it.
     The paper was written by its human author in collaboration with AI research agents under a documented
protocol. Appendix E records which agents contributed, the review rounds, the decisions the author made
and the errors the safeguards caught; we offer it as one concrete template for disclosing such collaboration.
     Section 2 reviews related work. Section 3 presents the model. Section 4 describes the data and Section 5
the empirical frontier. Section 6 shows how to measure the score when growth is bought. Section 7 calibrates
the ceiling and the limits by horizon, Section 8 discusses the weights on growth, measurement and the
accountability floor, and Section 9 concludes.


2     Related Literature
Our analysis connects six bodies of work: theory and evidence on the trade-off between firm growth and
profitability; the valuation of growth, its duration and the practitioner rules that summarize it; frontier esti-
mation; the economics of AI and automation and its effects on firms; markups, rents and concentration; and
recent work on AI-assisted research. For each, we keep the canonical statement and add the work of 2018
to 2026 that bears on our results; working papers and preprints are identified as such.

2.1   Growth and profitability: theory and evidence
The idea that growth is costly at the margin is old. Penrose (1959) argued that expansion is bounded by
the managerial services available to plan and absorb it, so faster growth raises unit costs. Uzawa (1969)


                                                       4
formalized this “Penrose effect” as a concave relation between investment and the growth it buys, and the
adjustment-cost models of Lucas (1967) and Hayashi (1982) gave it a neoclassical form in which marginal
q governs investment and equals average q under linear homogeneity. The closest antecedents of our model
are Marris (1963, 1964), Solow (1971) and Odagiri (1981), in which the firm’s objective (value, growth, or
a managerial preference over both) selects a point on a frontier trading growth against profitability or valu-
ation. They remain the canonical treatments. Our feasible set is the same kind of object: a transformation
curve between growth and margin, with each Rule-of-X line acting as a price line, so the score-maximizing
point is a tangency. Mueller (1969, 1972) extended the managerial view to conglomerate mergers and to
a life-cycle account in which growth opportunities decline as firms mature. On the financial side, Higgins
(1977) showed that sustainable growth is pinned down by margin, asset turnover, leverage and payout, a link
that our capital-intensity correction and our restatement in terms of economic profit generalize.
     The growth technology also has antecedents in marketing science. Growth bought by spending and
limited by the unserved market resembles the sales-response model of Vidale and Wolfe (1957) and the
goodwill model of Nerlove and Arrow (1962), and our optimal-spend condition, under which spend equals
the spend elasticity times the net score value of the growth it buys, is a Dorfman and Steiner (1954) condition.
Modern firm-level evidence supplies the structure behind that technology. Exporters grow after entry by
building a customer base through marketing and advertising, with flat markups and a customer base that
responds little to past sales, rather than through low introductory prices (Fitzgerald et al., 2024); successful
entrants build market share by reaching more customers, with no markup differences between new and
established markets (Argente et al., 2025); and in a working paper, a firm’s market share is tied mainly
to its number of customers and its markup only to its sales per customer (Afrouzi et al., 2023). Growth
bought with spend at roughly constant margins per customer, separate from the margin intercept, is what
Assumption 2 posits. Empirically, the link between growth and profit, in either direction, is weak and varies
across samples (Coad, 2007; Davidsson et al., 2009; Federico and Capelleras, 2015; Lee, 2014), and firm-
level effects explain more of the variance in both than industry effects do (Goddard et al., 2009), which
cautions against reading industry averages as frontier positions.

2.2   Valuing growth: duration and the practitioner rules
In the dividend-discount model of Gordon (1959, 1962) and the valuation framework of Miller and
Modigliani (1961), growth adds value only when the return on new investment exceeds the cost of capi-
tal, so any linear rule that adds growth to margin embeds an implicit exchange rate between the two; our
Proposition 13 makes that rate explicit in terms of economic profit. Schwartz and Moon (2000) showed
that high valuations of loss-making internet firms can be rationalized by uncertain, mean-reverting revenue
growth, and McCarthy et al. (2017) valued subscription businesses from disclosed customer metrics. The
sensitivity of value to growth in these models is a duration effect in the sense of Macaulay (1938); our
derivation of the screening weight as a duration connects to the implied equity duration of Dechow et al.
(2004). Cash flows of longer duration earn lower expected returns: long-duration stocks earn about 1.1%
a month less than short-duration stocks (Weber, 2018), and the value, profitability and investment premia
reflect a premium on near-term cash flows (Gormsen and Lazarus, 2023). If the discount rate falls with
duration, the firm’s own weight on growth is higher than our constant-rate calculation gives, which widens
its gap with screening weights. Short-termism points the same way: firms that just meet analyst forecasts
cut research spending, and in an estimated growth model short-termism slows growth (Terry, 2023), as a
firm acting on a screening weight below its marginal value of revenue would. The saturation term in our


                                                       5
model resembles Bass-type diffusion (Bass, 1969).
    The Rule of 40 itself is a practitioner construct. Feld (2015) popularized it after hearing it from a late-
stage investor: revenue growth plus profit margin should reach 40%. Consulting studies find that it is rarely
met and is associated with valuation premia. Depeyrot and Heap (2018) report that only 16% of 86 public
software firms beat it in every year from 2013 to 2017 and that firms above the line traded at roughly double
the EV/revenue multiple; Roche and Tandon (2021) reach similar conclusions for more than 200 software
firms over 2011 to 2021. Later work raised or reweighted the threshold. Bosche et al. (2022) asked whether
60 is the new 40; Deeter and Bondy (2024) proposed the Rule of X and recommend multiplying growth by
about 2 for private firms and 2 to 3 for public firms before adding free cash flow margin; and Vista Equity
Partners (2026) describe a Rule of 60 as an emerging aspiration for AI-enabled software firms. In the other
direction, Lipman et al. (2026) argue that inference adds variable cost to software margins and that firms
reinvesting in AI may need to accept a Rule of 30 for a time. The only peer-reviewed academic study of
the rule we located is Lee (2024), who evaluates it as an investment screen on 1,756 SaaS firms over 2003
to 2022 and proposes a “Rule of 65.” We found no modern peer-reviewed study of the growth-profitability
frontier of software firms beyond it.
    Scores also depend on which margin is used. Bradshaw and Sloan (2002) document the widening gap
between GAAP and Street earnings, Barth et al. (2012) study why pro forma and Street earnings continued
to exclude stock-based compensation after SFAS 123R, and Mohanram et al. (2020) find that analysts who
exclude it issue optimistically biased price targets. This motivates our explicit treatment of stock-based
compensation in the margin measure. The split of selling, general and administrative expense into growth
spend and maintenance has a market-based counterpart: exit prices imply the long-lived share of SG&A and
the depreciation of R&D, giving intangible stocks about 15% smaller than standard parameters (Ewens et al.,
2025), and an intangible-adjusted value factor prices assets at least as well as the traditional one (Eisfeldt
et al., 2022).

2.3   Frontier estimation
Our empirical frontier draws on two traditions. Stochastic frontier analysis, introduced by Aigner et al.
(1977) and Meeusen and van den Broeck (1977) and surveyed by Kumbhakar and Lovell (2000), separates
noise from one-sided inefficiency under parametric assumptions. Deterministic envelopment, beginning
with the data envelopment analysis of Charnes et al. (1978), avoids functional-form assumptions but is
sensitive to outliers. Partial frontiers address that sensitivity: the order-m frontier of Cazals et al. (2002)
and the conditional-quantile frontier of Aragon et al. (2005), both treated in Daraio and Simar (2007), are
robust to extreme observations. Upper-quantile regression (Koenker and Bassett, 1978) offers a parametric
counterpart. We have not found prior applications of these estimators to the growth-margin plane of software
firms.

2.4   AI, automation and firms
Our reduced-form mapping of AI effects is informed by task-based models of automation. Aghion et al.
(2019) show that automation can coexist with balanced growth because, as in Baumol (1967), sectors that
resist automation come to dominate expenditure and constrain aggregate growth. Trammell and Korinek
(2023), Davidson (2021) and Erdil and Besiroglu (2023) examine conditions under which AI could produce
much faster growth, and Erdil et al. (2025) build an integrated assessment model of AI automation. Ace-
moglu (2025) argues from task exposure and cost-savings estimates that aggregate productivity effects over


                                                      6
the next decade are likely to be modest. Technical exposure also overstates what is worth automating: in a
working paper on computer vision, only 23% of the wages paid for exposed tasks would be economical to
automate at current costs (Svanberg et al., 2024).
    Firm-level evidence bears on the channels in Table 1. Brynjolfsson et al. (2025) document productiv-
ity gains from a generative AI assistant for customer-support agents, and Eloundou et al. (2024) measure
occupational exposure to large language models. Firms that invest in AI grow faster in sales, employment
and value, mainly through product innovation (Babina et al., 2024), which supports the growth channels (λ
and G) alongside the cost channel (a). Markets capitalized labor substitution at once: firms whose work-
forces were most exposed to generative AI outperformed by 5% in the two weeks after ChatGPT’s release
(Eisfeldt et al., 2023), and task-level exposure lowers labor demand while adopting firms grow (Hampole
et al., 2025); both are working papers. Adoption inside firms is slow. Generative AI assisted only 1 to 5%
of U.S. work hours in late 2024 (Bick et al., 2026); firm use rose from 3.7% of firms in September 2023
(Bonney et al., 2024) to 18% by early 2026, most adopters use AI in three or fewer business functions,
and performance rises with the breadth of integration (Bonney et al., 2026). This is the slow approach that
our attainable frontier formalizes; optimal adoption of a technology whose risks are learned over time is
gradual (Acemoglu and Lensman, 2024), and a preprint obtains staged commitment from pilots that build
firm-specific capability (Tewari, 2026). On costs, the price of reaching fixed benchmark performance fell
between roughly 9 and 900 times per year depending on the task (Cottier et al., 2025), or about 5 to 10
times per year at fixed performance (Gundlach et al., 2025), while short-run price elasticities of demand
for inference just above one (Demirer et al., 2025, 2026) mean that spending on inference, and the com-
pute share of our ceiling, need not fall. Post-quantum cryptography adds a dated cost: NIST deprecates
quantum-vulnerable public-key algorithms after 2030 and disallows them after 2035 (Moody et al., 2026),
and the only quantified migration cost is a rough order of magnitude for U.S. federal civilian systems (Office
of Management and Budget, 2024).

2.5   Markups, rents and concentration
Work on markups and intangibles explains why high scores are possible at all: De Loecker et al. (2020) doc-
ument rising markups, Haskel and Westlake (2017) emphasize the scalability of intangible assets, Crouzet
et al. (2022) and Corrado et al. (2022) set out how non-rival, partly excludable intangibles scale and how
to measure them, and Peters and Taylor (2017) and Crouzet and Eberly (2023) show how intangible capi-
tal and rents alter the relation between q and investment. Our free-entry result formalizes the implication
that, where entry is free, AI-driven cost reductions raise scores durably only if they accrue to scarce inputs.
Which inputs stay scarce is itself a question of modern theory. Data-driven markets tend to tip when more
user data lower the cost of quality (Prüfer and Schottmüller, 2021), and the largest firms are valued mainly
for their data (Farboodi and Veldkamp, 2023); a data rent is durable when the market tips and fades when
it does not. At the level of the AI supply chain, the amortized cost of training the most compute-intensive
models has grown about 2.4 times a year since 2016 (Cottier et al., 2024), a preprint: a rising fixed cost
of the kind that concentrates supply, and with it the exposure to a single provider that our common-mode
failure term prices.

2.6   AI-assisted research
Automated research systems are advancing quickly (Lu et al., 2024; Si et al., 2025; Gottweis et al., 2026),
and Korinek (2023) catalogs uses of generative AI in economics. arXiv requires authors to report significant


                                                      7
use of generative AI, states that such tools should not be listed as authors, and holds authors responsible for
all content (arXiv, 2026); Appendix E documents our compliance.

2.7   Our contribution
Relative to this literature, we read the Rule of 40 and the Rule of X as price lines on a frontier of attainable
growth and margin, distinguish the firm’s own weight on growth from the investor’s screening weight, bound
how long extreme scores can last, show that the cross-section does not identify any firm’s frontier, restate
the rule as a rule of economic profit, charge the full costs of running AI against its ceiling, including an
accountability floor whose common-mode part does not vanish under automation, and define the frontier
attainable by a given date when transitions are risky and capacity is limited. Empirically, we estimate
frontier statistics whose meaning does not depend on sample size, for observed scores and for the organic
frontier. Unlike Lee (2024), who evaluates the rule as an investment screen, we model the frontier it is
applied to, and its limits.


3     A Model of the Growth–Profitability Frontier
This section builds a model in which a firm spends part of its revenue on growth, so that faster revenue
growth costs current free cash flow margin. The model gives an efficient frontier between growth and
margin and a closed form for the best attainable Rule-of-X score. It identifies three limits on that score:
market saturation, the finite headroom of market share, and competitive replication. It shows that a value-
maximizing firm maximizes a Rule-of-X score at every date, with a weight equal to the marginal value
of a dollar of revenue, and it explains why that weight differs from the weights investors use to screen
firms. We then extend the model to heterogeneous firms, which relates it to the cross-sectional evidence
of Section 5, and map the effects of AI automation onto its parameters. Finally, we charge the full cost of
running AI against the ceiling of the score, derive the frontier a firm can reach by a given horizon when
transitions are risky and organizational capacity is limited, and restate the objective in terms of economic
profit for industries other than software. Supporting results are collected in Appendix A, and all proofs
are in Appendix B. The script code/verify_theory.py checks every closed form, first-order condition
and envelope derivative symbolically, and checks the concavity, limit and inequality claims numerically on
random parameter draws.

3.1   Setup
We describe a firm at a point in time. Revenue is S, the addressable market is M, and market share is s = S/M.
Growth rates are instantaneous (continuously compounded) rates, so revenue growth equals market growth
plus share growth. The static results can equally be read in period rates; Lemma 2 and Proposition 4(b)
treat the places where the distinction matters. Margins and spending are expressed as fractions of current
revenue.

Assumption 1 (Market and share). The market grows at a constant nominal rate G > −1. The firm’s share
satisfies s ∈ (0, s̄), where the cap s̄ ∈ (0, 1) reflects antitrust limits and customer heterogeneity. Revenue
growth g and share growth are linked by g = G + ṡ/s.




                                                       8
Assumption 2 (Growth technology). The firm chooses discretionary growth spend σ ∈ [0, σ̄ ] (sales and
marketing plus expansion R&D, as a fraction of revenue). Here σ̄ ∈ (0, ∞] is a financing cap, and σ̄ = ∞
means that spend can be financed externally without limit. Revenue grows at
                                                             s
                        g = G + λ σ α h(s),        h(s) = 1 − ,        α ∈ (0, 1), λ > 0.                       (1)
                                                             s̄
We write ℓ := λ h(s) > 0 for the firm’s effective growth opportunity and ∆ := g − G ≥ 0 for excess growth.

    The parameter λ measures demand pull (the growth that spend buys when the share is small), h(s)
measures remaining headroom, and α < 1 imposes diminishing returns to spend. The technology resembles
the sales-response model of Vidale and Wolfe (1957), in which spend converts the unsaturated part of the
market, and the goodwill model of Nerlove and Arrow (1962); under a constant spend rate it makes the share
follow a logistic path.

Assumption 3 (Costs). Gross margin is γ = 1 − cv . Variable cost is cv = ch + cc + f , made up of human
service cost ch ≥ 0, compute (inference) cost cc ≥ 0 and third-party fees f ≥ 0 (payments, marketplaces).
Non-discretionary operating cost a covers general and administrative cost, maintenance R&D, and an ac-
countability floor, and satisfies a ≥ amin ≥ 0. The operating margin is mop = γ − a − σ , and m0 := γ − a is
the margin intercept.

Assumption 4 (Capital). Serving revenue requires a capital stock K = kS with k ≥ 0 (for example, owned
compute), installed before the revenue it serves. Growth capital does not depreciate, and maintenance capital
expenditure is included in a. The free cash flow (FCF) margin is

                                        m = mop − k g = m0 − σ − k g.                                           (2)

    Equation (2) is exact in continuous time, because investment is K̇ = kṠ = kgS, and in discrete time when
capital is installed one period ahead; Lemma 2 in Appendix A shows that the alternative, contemporaneous
convention makes the feasible set non-convex when α < 1/2. Reported scores pair growth over the past
year, gt , with FCFt , which is the contemporaneous convention; for firms whose capital expenditure leads
revenue, gt+1 + mt is the pairing consistent with (2). Throughout, “margin” means the FCF margin m unless
stated otherwise. Remark 4 in Appendix A discusses fixed costs, firm-specific zero-spend growth, negative
capital intensity, depreciation and network effects.

Definition 1 (Feasible set, dominance, frontier, score). Fix s. The feasible set F is the set of pairs (g, m)
such that, for some σ ∈ [0, σ̄ ], g = G + ℓσ α and m ≤ m0 − σ − kg (margin can always be wasted). A pair
(g′ , m′ ) dominates (g, m) if g′ ≥ g and m′ ≥ m, with at least one inequality strict. The efficient frontier is the
set of undominated points of F . For a weight X ≥ 0, the Rule-of-X score is RX (g, m) = Xg + m. The Rule
of 40 is the test R1 ≥ 0.40, and Bessemer’s Rule of X uses weights X of about 2 to 3.

    Table 12 in Appendix A collects the notation of the paper. Four derived constants are used throughout:

              1
       β :=     > 1,       κ := ℓ−β = (λ h)−β ,        y := X − k,      Cα := (1 − α) α α/(1−α) ∈ (0, 1).       (3)
              α
The quantity y is the net value of a unit of growth in the score: the weight X less the capital k that each unit
of growth requires.



                                                         9
Calibration of α. The data do not identify the spend elasticity α (Section 5): under optimization spend
and excess growth move in proportion (Proposition 2), so a log-log regression of one on the other has slope
one whatever α is. We therefore use α ∈ [0.25, 0.45], equivalently β = 1/α ∈ [2.2, 4], as a calibration range,
report sensitivity to it, and use α = 0.3 in every illustrative example. Two features of this range recur. First,
since α < 1/2, the curvature of the frontier vanishes at g = G (Proposition 1(c)): with k ≈ 0 the frontier is
nearly flat for modest excess growth and steepens sharply as ∆ approaches ℓ, the excess growth bought by
spending 100% of revenue. Second, α < 1/2 is exactly the range in which the timing convention for growth
capital matters (Lemma 2).

3.2   The efficient frontier
Proposition 1 (Efficient frontier). Under Assumptions 1–4, fix s and let ḡ := G + ℓσ̄ α (with ḡ = ∞ if σ̄ = ∞).
Write I := [G, ḡ] ∩ R.
  (a) The efficient frontier is the graph of

                                    m∗ (g) = m0 − k g − κ (g − G)β ,        g ∈ I.                           (4)

      The feasible set F = {(g, m) : g ∈ I, m ≤ m∗ (g)} is convex. A feasible point is dominated if and only
      if m < m∗ (g). Each frontier point is produced by the unique spend level σ = κ(g − G)β .
  (b) At g = G the frontier takes the value m∗ (G) = m0 − kG, and its right derivative there is −k.
  (c) m∗ is strictly decreasing and strictly concave on I. For g > G, m∗′ (g) = −k − β κ(g − G)β −1 and
      m∗′′ (g) = −β (β − 1)κ(g − G)β −2 < 0. As g ↓ G, m∗′′ (g) tends to 0 if α < 1/2, equals −2κ if α = 1/2,
      and tends to −∞ if α > 1/2.
  (d) If σ̄ = ∞, then as g → ∞, m∗ (g) → −∞, m∗′ (g) → −∞ and m∗ (g)/g → −∞.

Intuition. Growth is bought with spend, and because returns to spend diminish, each extra point of growth
costs more margin than the one before. The frontier therefore bends down. Its slope at the market growth
rate is only −k: the first unit of excess growth costs almost no spend, only the capital needed to serve it. The
rate of steepening is governed by β = 1/α. Points strictly below the frontier waste margin.

3.3   The Rule of X as a supporting line
Geometrically, the frontier is a transformation curve between growth and margin, and a Rule-of-X score is a
price line with slope −X. The tangency between the two has antecedents in the growth-valuation frontier of
the managerial firm (Marris, 1964; Solow, 1971; Odagiri, 1981), and recent firm-level evidence that firms
grow by acquiring customers through marketing and distribution at roughly constant markups supports the
growth technology behind it (Fitzgerald et al., 2024; Argente et al., 2025; Afrouzi et al., 2023).

Proposition 2 (Maximal Rule-of-X score). Fix X ≥ 0 and s. In parts (b) and (e), assume that the financing
cap does not bind, that is, σ ∗ ≤ σ̄ with σ ∗ given in (6); this always holds if σ̄ = ∞.
  (a) Iso-score sets {(g, m) : Xg + m = c} are straight lines of slope −X. A maximizer of RX on F exists,
      and every maximizer lies on the frontier.

                                   30%                                                        Rule-of-2 optimum (X = 2)

  (b) If X > k, the maximizer is unique and interior (g∗ > G), and there the frontier is tangent to the iso-
      score line: m∗′ (g∗ ) = −X. In closed form,

             ∆∗ = g∗ − G = (λ h)1/(1−α) α(X − k)
                                               α/(1−α)
                                                         ,                                                                    (5)
                                1/(1−α)
             σ ∗ = αλ h (X − k)          = α(X − k) ∆∗ ,
                              
                                                                                                                              (6)
                                                                                      1/(1−α)
             R∗X = m0 + (X − k) G + (1 − α)(X − k) ∆∗ = m0 + (X − k) G +Cα λ h (X − k)
                                                                          
                                                                                               ,                              (7)

      and the margin at the optimum is m∗ (g∗ ) = m0 − kg∗ − α(X − k)∆∗ .
  (c) If X ≤ k, the unique maximizer is the corner g∗ = G with σ ∗ = 0, and R∗X = m0 + (X − k)G. This part
      does not need the cap condition.
  (d) (Duality.) Each frontier point with G < g < ḡ is the unique maximizer of RX for exactly one weight,
      X(g) = −m∗′ (g) = k + β κ(g − G)β −1 . The point g = G is a maximizer if and only if X ≤ k. If σ̄ = ∞,
      the optimal growth rate g∗ (X) is continuous and nondecreasing in X, and strictly increasing for X > k.
  (e) The growth surplus Π := (1 − α)(X − k)∆∗ (zero if X ≤ k) satisfies Π = maxσ ≥0 {(X − k) ℓ σ α − σ },
      so that R∗X = m0 + (X − k)G + Π.

    When the dependence matters we write ∆∗ (ℓ, X) and σ ∗ (ℓ, X) for (5) and (6) with λ h replaced by ℓ and
X − k by (X − k)+ .
Intuition. Because the feasible set is convex, maximizing a linear score is a supporting-line problem: push a
line of slope −X outward until it last touches the frontier. The maximal score has three parts. The intercept
m0 is what the firm earns without outgrowing its market. The term (X − k)G is the value of the free growth
the market supplies, net of the capital it requires. The growth surplus Π is the profit from running the growth

                                                                            11
technology at a shadow price of X − k per unit of growth. The spend rule σ ∗ = α(X − k)∆∗ is a Dorfman
and Steiner (1954) condition: spend equals the spend elasticity times the value of the growth it buys, so the
fraction α of that value is spent and the fraction 1 − α is kept as surplus. When the weight on growth does
not cover the capital each unit of growth needs (X ≤ k), the firm should buy no growth. Part (d) says the
weight X indexes the frontier: choosing a rule is the same as choosing a point.
    Remarks 5 and 6 in Appendix A treat a binding financing cap and show that the Rule of 40, read as a
pass mark, accepts an interval of frontier points and dominated points below them, whereas the Rule of X
selects one.

3.4   Limits: saturation, small base and the score budget
Write R∗X (s) for the maximal score at share s when σ̄ = ∞, let y+ := max{X − k, 0}, and define the saturated
score
                                            R∞ := m0 + (X − k) G.                                         (8)
Proposition 3 (Saturation and the small-base bound). Fix X ≥ 0 and let σ̄ = ∞.
                                     1/(1−α)
  (a) R∗X (s) = R∞ + Cα λ h(s) y+             . If X > k, this is strictly decreasing in s, with dR∗X /ds = −(X −
                         
           ∗
      k)∆ /(s̄ − s). If X ≤ k, it is constant.
  (b) (Saturation limit.) lims→s̄ R∗X (s) = R∞ . For the Rule of 40 with k = 0, the limit is m0 + G. Since
      m0 = 1 − ch − cc − f − a ≤ 1 − cc − f − amin ≤ 1,

                 R∞ ≤ 1 − cc − f − amin + (X − k) G,          and for X = 1 :     R∞ ≤ 1 + (1 − k) G.          (9)

  (c) (Small-base bound.) sups∈(0,s̄) R∗X (s) = lims→0 R∗X (s) = R0 := R∞ + Cα (λ y+ )1/(1−α) . If X > k, the
      supremum is not attained.
  (d) (Demand-pull threshold.) Let X > k and R̄ > R∞ , and define
                                                                   1−α
                                                       1    R̄ − R∞
                                            Λ(R̄) :=                     .                                    (10)
                                                     X −k      Cα

      Then R∗X (s) ≥ R̄ if and only if λ h(s) ≥ Λ(R̄), and a score of R̄ is attainable at some share if and only if
      λ > Λ(R̄). A firm exactly at the threshold has ∆∗ = (R̄ − R∞ )/[(1 − α)(X − k)], σ ∗ = α(R̄ − R∞ )/(1 −
      α) and m = m0 − kg∗ − σ ∗ .
Intuition. Rising share uses up growth opportunity. As s → s̄, the growth surplus vanishes and the best
score falls to the margin intercept plus the value of free market growth. Because the margin intercept cannot
exceed 100% of revenue, a saturated firm with negligible capital cannot sustain a Rule-of-40 score above
100(1 + G) points. With G between 0.05 and 0.30, this is the formal content of a “Rule of about 100”.
Scores far above that level need demand pull above the threshold (10), and even then only at small share,
with very large spend and deeply negative margins. With α = 0.3, X = 1, k = 0, m0 = 0.6 and G = 0.2
(R∞ = 0.8), a “Rule of 1000” requires λ h ≥ Λ ≈ 8.71; at the threshold the firm grows about 1,314 points
faster than its market, spends about 394% of revenue on growth and runs an FCF margin of about −334%.
Proposition 15 in Appendix A adds that, if the firm spends optimally at each date, its excess over R∞ decays
at least as fast as t −1/α .
     The next result makes the transience of extreme scores precise. It needs neither the functional form (1)
nor optimal behavior, only the margin bound m ≤ m0 − kg and share accounting.

Proposition 4 (Score budget). Hold m0 , G, k and X > k fixed, and let R∞ = m0 + (X − k)G.
  (a) (Continuous time.) Take any measurable spend path σ : [0, T ] → [0, σ̄ ] with 0T σ α dt < ∞, any mea-

                                                                        Z T                  +                           s(T )              s̄
                                                                               RX (t) − R∞        dt ≤ (X − k) ln               < (X − k) ln .                  (11)
                                                                          0                                                s0               s0

  (b) (Discrete time.) Suppose instead that growth and margins are period rates: st+1 = st (1 + gt )/(1 + G),
      gt ≥ G, mt ≤ m0 − kgt , and st ≤ s̄ for all t. Then
                                                                                 T −1       (RX,t − R∞ )+       sT      s̄
                                                                                 ∑   ln 1 +                  ≤ ln    ≤ ln .                                     (12)
                                                                                 t=0        (X − k)(1 + G)        s0     s0

Intuition. Every point of score above the saturated level must be paid for with excess growth, and excess
growth is share growth. Share can grow only from s0 to s̄, so the log headroom ln(s̄/s0 ) is a finite budget that
every growth strategy draws on. A firm can spend the budget quickly, on a high but brief score, or slowly,
on a modest but long-lived one; Proposition 16 in Appendix A shows that the bound (11) is sharp. A firm
cannot spend the budget twice.

Corollary 1 (Extreme scores are transient). Let X > k and R̄ > R∞ . In the setting of Proposition 4(b), the
number T (R̄) of periods in which a firm starting at share s0 scores at least R̄ satisfies

                                                                                                         ln(s̄/s0 )
                                                                              T (R̄) ≤                                       .                                 (13)
                                                                                           ln 1 + (R̄ − R∞ )/[(X − k)(1 + G)]

For example, let X = 1, k = 0, m0 = 0.6 and G = 0.2 (so R∞ = 0.8), with s̄ = 0.5 and annual periods. From
s0 = 1% the firm can score at least 1,000 points (R1 ≥ 10) in at most one year (the bound is 1.8) and at least
140 points in at most nine years (bound 9.6). From s0 = 10−5 it can score at least 1,000 points in at most


                                                                                                         13
five years (bound 5.0).

3.5   Comparative statics
Proposition 5 (Envelope results). Let σ̄ = ∞ and X ≥ 0. The maximal score R∗X is continuously differentiable
in (m0 , G, k, X, λ , s), with

                      ∂ R∗X              ∂ R∗X              ∂ R∗X                ∂ R∗X
                            = 1,               = X − k,           = −g∗ ,              = g∗ ,
                      ∂ m0               ∂G                  ∂k                  ∂X
                                                                                                            (14)
                                   ∂ R∗X      (X − k)∆∗
                                          =             = (X − k) h (σ ∗ )α ≥ 0,
                                    ∂λ            λ
where the last inequality is strict if and only if X > k. Through m0 = γ −a and γ = 1−ch −cc − f , ∂ R∗X /∂ γ =
1 and ∂ R∗X /∂ a = ∂ R∗X /∂ cc = −1. Moreover R∗X is convex in k and in λ , and ∂ 2 R∗X /∂ k ∂ λ = −∆∗ /[(1 −
α)λ ] ≤ 0.

Intuition. By the envelope theorem, a parameter change can be valued at the optimal point without re-
optimizing. Capital intensity is charged on every unit of growth, including the free market growth, so
its marginal cost is the growth rate itself. The negative cross-partial says that firms with more growth
opportunity are hurt more by capital intensity.

Corollary 2 (Capital-for-labor substitution). Let σ̄ = ∞, and suppose automation lowers a by da > 0 and
raises k by dk > 0, with other parameters fixed. Then the change in the maximal score satisfies

                                da − g∗ (k) dk ≤ ∆R∗X ≤ da − g∗ (k + dk) dk.                                (15)

Hence the score rises if da > g∗ (k) dk and does not rise if da ≤ g∗ (k + dk) dk. To first order, it rises if and
only if da > g∗ dk.

Intuition. Replacing recurring human cost with owned capital raises the FCF score only if the opex saved per
dollar of revenue exceeds the growth rate times the added capital per dollar of revenue, because the capital
must be bought again for every new dollar of revenue. Without depreciation, raising k by 0.10 must cut
opex by more than 5 points of revenue for a firm growing at 50%, but by only 1 point for a firm growing at
10%. Compute depreciates quickly, however: with depreciation at rate δ the threshold becomes (g∗ + δ ) dk
(Remark 4(c)), so at δ = 0.25 the two figures rise to 7.5 and 3.5 points. The bracket (15) holds exactly, not
only to first order, because R∗X is convex in k.

Corollary 3 (Automation priorities). Let σ̄ = ∞ and X > k, so that the Rule-of-X optimum is interior, and
evaluate each improvement at the optimum, with spend re-optimized. To first order:
  (i) (Non-discretionary cost.) Removing non-discretionary cost da (a share of revenue) raises R∗X by da
      and the saturated score R∞ by da, so the gain survives saturation.
  (ii) (Growth engine.) Raising demand pull λ by a proportion xλ raises R∗X by xλ (X − k)∆∗ = xλ σ ∗ /α;
       exactly, by Π [(1 + xλ )1/(1−α) − 1]. The first-order gain equals that from cutting a non-discretionary
       cost line of size σ ∗ /α by the same proportion, so a percentage gain in the productivity of growth
       spend is worth 1/α (2.2 to 4) times the same percentage cut in an equally large cost line. The gain
       does not raise R∞ , and it vanishes as s → s̄.



                                                        14
 (iii) (Retention.) Raising zero-spend growth Gi (net revenue retention, Remark 4(b)) by dG raises R∗X by
       (X − k) dG, and R∞ by the same amount.
 (iv) (Cost of revenue.) A cut du < 0 in unit cost of revenue raises the gross margin, and hence R∗X , by
      (1 − ϕ)|du|/p at given demand, where ϕ is the pass-through elasticity of Lemma 3. Under free entry
      without a scarce input it leaves the long-run markup at zero (Proposition 8(b)); the long-run score
      then changes only through the return on capital, (r − G) ku /ĉ, because lower prices raise capital per
      dollar of revenue, and not at all if ku = 0.
  (v) (Capital for labor.) Automation that adds capital dk while removing cost da raises R∗X if and only if
      da > g∗ dk (Corollary 2); since g∗ ≥ G, the hurdle is lowest for the slowest growers.

Intuition. A fast-growing firm acting on a high weight gets the most from automating its growth engine and
its retention (parts (ii) and (iii)); a saturated firm gets the most from removing non-discretionary cost (part
(i)); savings in cost of revenue count only to the extent the firm keeps them (part (iv)); and capital-heavy
automation suits slow growers (part (v)). These are effects that shift the frontier itself, distinct from closing
a firm’s distance to its frontier.

3.6   Which weight on growth? The firm’s and the investor’s
The score results take the weight X as given. Which weight should a firm use, and how does it relate to the
weights of 2 to 3 in investors’ screens? We answer the first question with the firm’s dynamic problem and
the second with a valuation identity. The two weights turn out to be different objects.
    Let r > G be the discount rate. A spend policy is a measurable σ : [0, ∞) → [0, ∞) with 0t σ α dτ < ∞
                                                                                                  R

for all t. Under Assumptions 1–4 it induces, from s(0) = s0 , the share path ṡ = s ℓ(s) σ α with ℓ(s) = λ h(s),
revenue St = st Mt and FCF margin mt = m0 − σt − kgt . Firm value is
                                                                 Z T
                                   V (S0 , M0 ) := sup lim sup         e−rt mt St dt,                             (16)
                                                    σ    T →∞     0

where each integral is well defined in [−∞, ∞) because mt ≤ m0 − kG.

Proposition 6 (Dynamic optimality: the firm’s own growth weight is the marginal q of revenue). Let σ̄ = ∞,
r > G and m0 − kG > 0.
  (a) (Homogeneity.) V (S, M) = M v(S/M), where v(s) := V (s, 1) satisfies (m0 − kG) s/(r − G) ≤ v(s) ≤
      (m0 − kG) s̄/(r − G). Hence ∂V /∂ S = v′ (s), the marginal value of a dollar of revenue, and V /S =
      v(s)/s, the revenue multiple, wherever v is differentiable.
  (b) (Verification.) Let v̂ ∈ C1 ((0, s̄)) be bounded and satisfy the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equa-
      tion
                  (r − G) v̂(s) = s max m0 − σ − kG + v̂′ (s) − k ℓ(s) σ α
                                                                    
                                                                                  for all s ∈ (0, s̄). (17)
                                     σ ≥0

      Then v̂ ≥ v. If, in addition, from every s0 ∈ (0, s̄) the feedback σ̂ (s) = σ ∗ (ℓ(s), v̂′ (s)) generates a share
      path in (0, s̄), then v̂ = v and the feedback is optimal.
  (c) (The Rule of X at every date.) The maximand in (17) equals RX (g, m) − XG with X = v̂′ (s). Under
      the conditions of (b), a firm that follows the optimal feedback therefore maximizes, at every date, the



                                                         15
      Rule-of-X score at its current share, with weight

                                                                     ∂V
                                              X dyn (s) = v′ (s) =      ,                                   (18)
                                                                     ∂S

      the marginal q of revenue; and (17) can be written R∗X dyn (s) (s) = (r − G) v(s)/s + G X dyn (s).
  (d) (Small share.) Let v0 be the smallest positive number that satisfies
                                                                     1/(1−α)
                                 (r − G) v0 = m0 − kG +Cα λ (v0 − k)+          ,                            (19)

      assumed to exist and to be a simple root, and let ∆◦ = ∆∗ (λ , v0 ), σ ◦ = σ ∗ (λ , v0 ), g◦ = G + ∆◦ and
      m◦ = m0 − kg◦ − σ ◦ . Then g◦ < r and v0 = m◦ /(r − g◦ ); v(s) ≤ v0 s for all s; and lims→0 v(s)/s = v0 .
      If a function v̂ as in (b) exists, then also X dyn (s) = v̂′ (s) → v0 as s → 0.

Intuition. A point of excess growth bought today is a permanent addition to the revenue base, worth v′ (s)
per dollar of revenue, while the spend that buys it is paid once. The HJB equation therefore splits the firm’s
problem into a sequence of static Rule-of-X problems, one per date, with a weight equal to the marginal
value of revenue. The Rule of X is exactly the right instantaneous criterion, but its weight is a valuation
multiple, not 2 or 3. Hayashi (1982) shows that marginal and average q coincide when value is linearly
homogeneous in the stock. Here value is homogeneous of degree one in revenue and market size jointly, not
in revenue alone, so saturation drives marginal q of revenue, v′ (s), apart from the revenue multiple v(s)/s.
The two coincide in the small-share limit of part (d), where the firm is a constant-growth firm whose revenue
multiple v0 = m◦ /(r − g◦ ) is the Gordon multiple.
Worked example. Take r = 0.10, G = 0.04, k = 0, α = 0.3 and m0 = 0.35, so that a firm that never spends on
growth is worth m0 /(r − G) ≈ 5.8 times revenue. Demand pull λ ≈ 0.070 makes the small-share multiple
v0 = 10: the firm grows at 7.6%, spends 10.7% of revenue on growth and earns an FCF margin of 24.3%,
and 0.243/(0.10 − 0.076) = 10. Demand pull λ ≈ 0.081 gives v0 = 15, with growth of 9.2%, spend of
23.6% and a margin of 11.4%. With the share cap s̄ = 0.5 and v0 = 10, a numerical solution of (17) gives
a growth weight v′ (s) of 9.1, 7.2 and 5.4 at 1%, 10% and 50% of the cap, against revenue multiples v(s)/s
of 9.4, 8.1 and 6.5. For revenue multiples of 5 to 15, the firm’s own growth weight is therefore far above
the 2 to 3 of investors’ screens. Equity cash flows of longer duration earn lower expected returns (Weber,
2018; Gormsen and Lazarus, 2023); if the discount rate falls with duration, the marginal q of revenue of a
fast grower is higher than this constant-rate calculation gives, which widens the gap.
Implication. Because σ ∗ = α(X − k)∆∗ , the ratio of growth spend to excess growth reveals α(X − k).
Section 5 finds a median ratio of the previous year’s selling and marketing share to excess growth of 3.3
among above-median organic growers. With k near zero and α ∈ [0.25, 0.45], this implies a weight on
growth of roughly 7 to 13, although the proxy has biases of uncertain net sign. That is consistent with the
marginal q of revenue at plausible revenue multiples, not with screening weights of 2 to 3.
    The weights in investors’ screens answer a different question. They compare a change in the growth rate
that persists for several years with a change in the margin, which is what regressions of revenue multiples on
growth and margin estimate. Let η := (1 + g)/(1 + r), NT (η) := ∑t=1  T
                                                                          η t , and let M∞ := (1 + G∞ )/(r − G∞ )
be a Gordon multiple for growth G∞ ∈ (−1, r) after year T .

Lemma 1 (The investor’s screening weight). Let V = S0 [ m NT (η) + mLR η T M∞ ]: revenue grows at g
for T years and at G∞ thereafter, the current margin m holds for T years, and the long-run margin is
mLR = θ m + (1 − θ )m̄, where θ ∈ [0, 1] measures the persistence of the current margin and m̄ is the margin

                                                       16
the business earns at maturity. Define X scr := (∂V /∂ g)/(∂V /∂ m).
  (a) In general,
                                                      T

                                        X scr =                               .                             (20)
                                                  (1 + g) NT (η) + θ η T M∞
                                                         


  (b) (Persistent margin.) If θ = 1 and M∞ = 0, then
                                                                      T
                                                    mD               ∑t=1 t ηt
                                          X scr =       ,     D :=     T       ,                             (21)
                                                    1+g              ∑t=1  ηt

      where D is the present-value-weighted average date (Macaulay duration) of the revenue stream. D is
      strictly increasing in T ; for T ≥ 2 it is strictly increasing in g and strictly decreasing in r; and D = 1
      for T = 1. If θ = 1 and M∞ > 0, the same formula holds with D replaced by DTV := [∑t=1             T

      T η T M∞ ]/[NT (η) + η T M∞ ].
  (c) (Transitory margin.) If θ < 1 and m̄ > 0, then X scr > 0 even when m = 0. If 0 ≤ m ≤ m̄ and m̄ > 0,
      then X scr is strictly decreasing in θ .
  (d) (Gordon limit.) If θ = 1, M∞ = 0 and g < r, then as T → ∞, X scr → m(1 + r)/[(1 + g)(r − g)] =
      (V /S0 ) (1 + r)/(1 + g)2 .

Discussion. The screening weight is a duration, as in the implied equity duration of Dechow et al. (2004):
it rises with how long growth persists and with how much of the value lies in the future, and it falls with the
persistence of the current margin. For example, m = 0.25, D = 10 and g = 0.25 give X scr = 0.25 · 10/1.25 =
2, inside Bessemer’s range; with r = 0.10, a duration of 10 years at g = 0.25 requires about 15 years of 25%
growth (D = 10.25 at T = 15, but only D = 6.5 at T = 10). Part (b) makes X scr proportional to the current
margin, so a firm that breaks even because it spends heavily on growth would get no credit for growth; part
(c) removes this artifact. With r = 0.10, g = 0.25, G∞ = 0.03, m̄ = 0.25, T = 10 and θ = 1/2, (20) gives
X scr ≈ 1.8 for m = 0.10 and 2.8 for m = 0.25; at θ = 0 these rise to 5.4 and 6.2.
     The two weights are different objects. The firm’s weight X dyn of Proposition 6 prices a one-period trade
of margin for growth whose revenue gain is permanent, so it is a revenue multiple. The screening weight
X scr prices a change in the growth rate that persists for T years against a margin change with persistence
θ , so it is a duration-scaled margin. They coincide approximately only when the current growth rate and
margin both persist forever (part (d)); with growth that reverts within a few years, X scr is of the order of 1 to
3, while X dyn is of the order of the revenue multiple. A firm that maximized a score with a screening weight
would buy too little growth, because ∆∗ is increasing in X (Proposition 2(d)). The screening weight also
has the usual limits of a local approximation: it varies with r, T , θ and the margin level, and it ignores risk,
dilution from stock-based compensation and the gap between GAAP and FCF margins.

3.7   Heterogeneous firms: the industry frontier as an upper envelope
Empirical frontiers are drawn across firms. Section 5 estimates the slope of the upper decile of margin on
growth, for g ∈ [0, 0.6], at −0.46 (standard error 0.14) on the GAAP basis and −0.37 (0.11) after stock-
based compensation, and finds a within-firm slope near zero. This subsection shows what each fact can
and cannot reveal. The cross-sectional slope is consistent with two different structures. One is a common
frontier with high opportunity: with k = 0, the frontier m0 − (∆/ℓ)β falls by about 24 points over ∆ ∈ [0, 0.6],
an average slope of about −0.4, when ℓ ≈ 0.86 (α = 0.25) or ℓ ≈ 1.14 (α = 0.45). The other is selection by


                                                        17
quality, in which firms with more growth opportunity also have higher margin intercepts (part (ii) below).
The cross-section cannot tell these apart (part (iv)). Read literally, the first structure places firms at different
points of one frontier, which requires them to act on different weights, none above the frontier’s steepest
slope on that range, about 0.9 to 1.6; the spend-based weight of roughly 7 to 13 (Proposition 6) favors the
second. The within-firm slope requires more (part (v)): anticipated shocks to opportunity would make it
strongly negative, so unanticipated demand shocks or costs committed in advance must offset them.

Assumption 5 (Heterogeneous firms). A set I of firms shares G ≥ 0, α, X ≥ 0 and a finite financing cap
σ̄ < ∞. Firm i has margin intercept m0i , growth opportunity ℓi = λi h(si ) > 0 and capital intensity ki ≥ 0,
with supi m0i < ∞. Each firm chooses a point of its feasible set that maximizes RX .

Definition 2 (Possibility envelope and observed frontier). Firm i’s feasible growth set is Gi = [G, G +
∆max
   i ], with ∆max
               i  = ℓi σ̄ α , and its frontier m∗i is given by (4). The industry possibility envelope is Φ(g) :=
sup{m∗i (g) : i ∈ I , g ∈ Gi } on G := i Gi . Firm i’s chosen point is (gci , mci ), with score Rci = Xgci + mci ,
                                           S

and R∗i is its maximal score. The observed frontier is Ψ(g) := sup{mci : gci ≥ g} for G ≤ g ≤ supi gci . For
a population distributed over I , the χ-quantile frontier Ψχ (g) is the χ-quantile of mci among firms with
gci ≥ g.

Proposition 7 (Industry frontier as an upper envelope). Under Assumption 5:
  (i) (Possibility envelope.) Φ is nonincreasing on G . If g1 < g2 and the supremum defining Φ(g2 ) is
      attained (for instance, if I is finite), then Φ(g1 ) > Φ(g2 ). A flat or rising cross-sectional frontier
      therefore cannot be the possibility envelope of finitely many firms.
  (ii) (Selection by quality.) Suppose firms are indexed by quality ω in a set Ω ⊂ R with a largest element
       ω̄. Suppose also that k is common, X > k, ℓω is strictly increasing in ω, and caps are slack. Then
       gcω = G + ∆∗ (ℓω , X) is strictly increasing in ω, and for ω < ω ′

                              mcω ′ − mcω = (m0ω ′ − m0ω ) − k + α(X − k) ∆∗ω ′ − ∆∗ω .
                                                                                   
                                                                                                               (22)

      If m0ω is nondecreasing, then Φ = m∗ω̄ , which is strictly decreasing. If, in addition, the quality condi-
      tion
                         m0ω ′ − m0ω ≥ k + α(X − k) ∆∗ω ′ − ∆∗ω            for all ω < ω ′
                                                                   
                                                                                                            (23)
      holds, then the locus of chosen points is nondecreasing in g and the observed frontier is flat: Ψ(g) =
      mcω̄ on its whole domain. Finally, suppose Ω = [ω, ω̄] carries a continuous, strictly increasing distri-
      bution function, ℓω and m0ω are continuous, and (23) holds strictly. Then every quantile frontier Ψχ ,
      χ ∈ (0, 1), is strictly increasing on [gcω , gcω̄ ).
 (iii) (Scores.) supg∈G [Xg + Φ(g)] = supi R∗i = supg [Xg + Ψ(g)]. Under the assumptions of (ii), including
       (23), the supremum over Ψ is attained only at the upper edge of the growth support, g = gcω̄ , where Ψ
       is flat. The observed frontier score is thus the best firm’s own tangency score, not a tangency on the
       observed frontier.
 (iv) (Non-identification.) The slope of the locus of chosen points can take any value, and, subject to the
      financing cap, the same locus is generated by firms with any spend elasticity α ′ ∈ (0, 1) that maximize
      RX ′ for any X ′ > k; the cross-section therefore identifies neither the slope nor the curvature of any
      firm’s frontier (Proposition 17(iv)).



                                                        18
  (v) (Within-firm co-movement.) If spend responds to anticipated shocks to opportunity while costs are
      committed before growth surprises arrive, then for small shocks the within-firm slope of margin on
      growth is a variance-weighted average of the expansion-path slope −[k + α(X − k)] < 0 and an
      operating-leverage term, plus a term from shocks that raise opportunity and the margin intercept to-
      gether; anticipated opportunity shocks alone give the strongly negative slope −[k + α(X − k)] (Propo-
      sition 17(v)).

Intuition. Every firm can grow at the market rate at its highest margin, so the envelope of what is possible
slopes down (part (i)). Observed points, however, are choices. When firms with more growth opportunity
also have higher margin intercepts (a quality factor), they grow fastest and earn the most, and the cloud of
chosen points slopes down much less than any firm’s own frontier. The quality condition (23) is explicit: the
margin intercept must rise by at least k + α(X − k) per unit of excess growth. With α = 0.3 and k = 0 this
is 0.3 margin points per growth point for a firm that maximizes the Rule of 40 (X = 1), and 2.1 points for a
firm that acts on X = 7. For example, a low type with (ℓ, m0 ) = (0.3, 0.30) and a high type with (0.8, 0.50),
with α = 0.3, X = 1, k = 0 and G = 0.1, choose (g, m) ≈ (0.21, 0.27) and (0.53, 0.37): the cross-sectional
slope is about +0.31, while both firms’ frontiers have slope exactly −1 at their chosen points. The binding
constraint on the best attainable score is therefore the supply of growth opportunity λ h together with the
margin intercept m0 , as (7) shows, not any willingness to trade margin for growth. Part (iii) explains why
the maximal observed-frontier score sits at the edge of the growth support (Section 5): it is the best firm’s
own R∗i , which falls with share by Proposition 3. Part (v) explains why a near-zero within-firm slope is
informative: with X = 7, α = 0.3 and k = 0, anticipated opportunity shocks alone would give a slope of
−2.1, so a slope near zero requires growth surprises against committed costs, or demand shocks that also
raise the margin intercept, to offset them.

3.8   Replication and the competitive limit
The frontier takes the margin intercept m0 as given. Under competition it is not given: if rivals can replicate
the product, entry competes margins down. We formalize this with Cournot competition and free entry in a
growing market.

Assumption 6 (Replication market). Inverse demand at date t is P = A − BQe−Gt with A, B > 0 and G ≥ 0,
so that at a constant price the quantity demanded grows at rate G. Identical firms can enter by paying a
sunk replication cost F > 0. Entry is possible only at date 0; later entry, which a growing market would
attract, is ruled out. A firm produces at unit variable cost u ≥ 0 (human service, compute and fees). It
needs ku ≥ 0 units of non-depreciating capital per unit of output flow, financed at cost of capital r > G. Let
ĉ := u + rku ∈ (0, A) and F̂ := (r − G)F. After entry, firms choose quantities à la Cournot at each date.
Capital may be owned (investment ku ẋ) or rented at rku per unit, and the two have equal present value.

Proposition 8 (Free entry and replication). Under Assumption 6:
  (a) With n firms, the price is Pn = (A + nĉ)/(n + 1), each firm’s flow profit is πn eGt with πn = (A −
      ĉ)2 /[B(n + 1)2 ], and an entrant’s net present value is πn /(r − G) − F. The Lerner index over full cost
      is Ln = (Pn − ĉ)/Pn = (A − ĉ)/(A + nĉ). A firm that owns its capital has kn = ku /Pn , operating margin
      mop = r kn + Ln , FCF margin m = (r − G)kn + Ln and score

                                            RX = X G + (r − G) kn + Ln .                                   (24)



                                                      19
                                 √
  (b) Assume NF := (A − ĉ)/ BF̂ ≥ 2. With free entry, n∗ = ⌊NF ⌋ − 1 firms enter, and 0 < Ln∗ < (A −
      ĉ)/[ĉ(NF − 1)]. As F → 0: n∗ → ∞, Ln∗ → 0, P → ĉ, and RX → XG + (r − G) ku /ĉ. For X = 1 the
      limit is G(1 − k̂) + rk̂, where k̂ = ku /ĉ.
  (c) (Scarce input.) Suppose in addition that each unit of output requires one unit of a non-replicable input
      in fixed supply ZeGt , with 0 < Z < (A − ĉ)/(2B), traded competitively at price w. Then for every n ≥ 1
      the input binds, wn = A − ĉ − BZ(n + 1)/n > 0, and P = A − BZ. Let k = ku /P. A firm that owns its
      input requirement has mop = rk + ρn + Lnnet , where ρn = wn /P is the rentqper dollar of revenue and
      Lnnet = BZ/(nP). A firm that buys the input has mop = rk + Lnnet . Assume Z B/F̂ ≥ 1. Free entry then
                   q
              ∗
      gives n = ⌊Z B/F̂⌋, and as F → 0, n∗ → ∞ and ρn∗ → ρ := 1 − ĉ/(A − BZ), so that

            RX → X G + (r − G) k + ρ (input owners),           RX → X G + (r − G) k (other firms).          (25)

Intuition. In a zero-economic-profit steady state, the operating margin covers the cost of capital, rk, plus a
markup L that is the growth-adjusted return on the sunk replication cost. As automation drives the replication
cost to zero, the markup vanishes and the price falls to full unit cost, so automation’s cost savings pass to
customers. What remains is (r − G)k, a return on capital that is large only for capital-heavy firms in slow
markets, plus the rent ρ on whatever input stays scarce (proprietary data, distribution, regulatory standing,
frontier compute). Under free entry and replication, then, the limit of the Rule of 40 is set by rents on what
stays scarce, not by how much can be automated. At given output prices the conclusion is the opposite:
the saturated score rises by 1 − τ points for every point of human cost or overhead removed, up to the full-
automation ceiling of Corollary 4. With G = 0.05, r = 0.10 and k = 0.5, a competitive firm without a scarce
input scores XG + 0.025, for example 7.5 points for X = 1. Two simplifications matter: entry happens only
at date 0, and the scarce input’s supply grows at exactly the market rate, a knife-edge case that keeps its price
constant (with slower supply growth the rent would rise over time). Remark 8 in Appendix A discusses other
modes of competition.

3.9   AI automation: mapping and frontier shifts
Table 1 maps the main effects of AI onto the model’s parameters, together with the first-order effect of each
on the maximal score (Proposition 5). Price effects need care: a cost reduction passed through to customers
can raise or lower the gross margin, and price deflation changes nominal market growth. Lemma 3 in
Appendix A shows that a cost reduction raises the gross margin if and only if the pass-through elasticity
of prices to unit cost is below one, and that price deflation lowers nominal market growth when demand is
inelastic.

Proposition 9 (AI and the maximal score). Let the parameters (m0 , k, λ , G) be continuously differentiable
functions of an AI adoption index ζ , with s and α fixed and σ̄ = ∞. Then

                            dR∗X                                             λ′
                                 = γ ′ − a′ + (X − k) G′ − g∗ k′ + (X − k) ∆∗ ,
                                           
                                                                                                            (26)
                            dζ                                               λ

so R∗X is strictly increasing on a neighborhood of any ζ at which the right side is positive, and strictly
decreasing on a neighborhood of any ζ at which it is negative. If price changes are driven by unit cost (with
f fixed), then γ ′ = −(1 − ϕ)u′ /p, where ϕ is the pass-through elasticity of Lemma 3.
