Intuition. AI moves several parameters at once, and not all in the same direction. Lower opex, cheaper ac-
quisition and faster software-market growth push the frontier up and out; owned compute steepens it, which
helps slow growers and hurts fast growers (Proposition 18 in Appendix A). Competitive price deflation can
erode the gross margin when pass-through is high, and it slows nominal market growth when demand is
inelastic. Equation (26) decides the net effect: the maximal score rises if and only if the margin gains, the
net value of faster market growth and the value of cheaper acquisition together exceed the growth rate times
the added capital intensity.
Remark 1 (Taxes). Let profits be taxed linearly at rate τ ∈ [0, 1) with full loss offset. Growth spend is
expensed, and the present value of tax deductions per dollar of growth capital expenditure is ψ ∈ [0, 1],
where ψ = 1 is full expensing and ψ = 0 means no deduction within the horizon. Under current U.S. law,
domestic research and experimental expenditure, which includes software development, is deducted when
incurred in tax years beginning after 2024 (United States Congress, 2025b), and equipment acquired after
19 January 2025 qualifies for a 100% first-year allowance (Internal Revenue Service, 2026), so ψ = 1 for
both. Deductions taken ratably over T years from the middle of the first tax year are worth

                                           1 + (1 + r)−T 1 − (1 + r)−(T −1)
                                    ψT =                +                                                            (27)
                                                2T              rT
per dollar at an annual discount rate r. Development abroad is amortized over 15 years (United States
Congress, 2025a), so ψ = ψ15 = 0.53 at r = 0.10; under the five-year amortization of 2022 to 2024, do-
mestic development had ψ5 = 0.80; and working capital, which is never deducted, has ψ = 0. The after-tax
FCF margin is m = (1 − τ)(m0 − σ ) − (1 − ψτ)kg. The frontier keeps the form (4) after the substitutions
m0 7→ (1 − τ)m0 , k 7→ (1 − ψτ)k, λ 7→ (1 − τ)−α λ and σ̄ 7→ (1 − τ)σ̄ . The results of Sections 3.2–3.5 and
Propositions 7 and 9 therefore hold after these substitutions, with σ read as after-tax spend (1 − τ)σ ; a dollar
of deductible growth spend costs only 1 − τ. The calibrations of Section 7 ignore this adjustment to demand
pull, which would raise λ by 5% to 7% for τ ∈ [0.15, 0.21] and α = 0.3.

Definition 3 (Full-cost floors). Under full automation, automation itself creates recurring costs. As shares
of revenue they are:
  (i) maintenance and revalidation, cmnt : upkeep of the automated builds, their revalidation at each model
      upgrade, and technical debt;
  (ii) security, csec : optimal security spending plus the residual expected incident loss, including losses from
       injected instructions; the migration to post-quantum cryptography, amortized over the window to the

                                                         21
      deprecation dates of Moody et al. (2026); and AI-specific compliance;
 (iii) AI failure, cerr : the expected loss after optimal controls from errors, hallucination and misbehavior
       that are independent across activities, plus the expected cost of common-mode failures that processes
       share through a model provider;
 (iv) other recurring costs, canc : management of AI spend, incident-response capacity and legal review of
      AI vendors.

    Lemma 4 in Appendix A gives each floor a closed form under stated assumptions: maintenance as an
annual equivalent of upkeep and revalidation, security as the minimized sum of spending and residual loss,
which grows only with the logarithm of exposure, AI failure as a review-proof error loss plus a capped
heavy-tailed misbehavior loss, and common-mode failure as a cost that rises with the concentration of the
firm’s exposure on one provider. The companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026) derives the
same floors activity by activity. Each cost is counted once. The accountability floor amin keeps its meaning
(officer certification, internal control over financial reporting and its integrated audit, directors’ and officers’
insurance, legal and liability costs, and model-risk validation), and it also holds whatever human review
and sign-off regulation or the firm’s risk appetite requires. By Proposition 10 below it further holds the
control testing and IT general controls that AI-performed financial controls add, and the expected loss from
misstatements the control design does not catch, whose common-mode part does not vanish as layers are
added; the upkeep and revalidation of the builds themselves stay in cmnt . Operational losses that survive
review are in cerr , and neither it nor its common-mode term counts misstatements; incidents caused by
attackers, including injected instructions and exploited shared vulnerabilities, are in csec . One-time costs
(builds, parallel running, severance, training) are transition costs and enter Section 3.10, not the ceiling. The
compute share cc is inference bought as a service plus the running cost of owned compute. The capital of
owned compute and of other automated work is part of k and is replaced at the rate δK . With product lines l of
revenue Sl , the compute share and fees are revenue-weighted blends, cc = ∑l (Sl /S)cc,l and f = ∑l (Sl /S) fl ,
so the gross margin is the blend γ = ∑l (Sl /S)γl , and a growing AI product line with a lower gross margin
moves it over time.

Assumption 7 (Layered financial controls). A financial-reporting process offers Nmm opportunities a year
for a material misstatement, each costing Lmm if no control layer catches it. A design uses n j ≥ 0 layers of
each type j (people, or AI on a given model provider). All layers of type j fail together with probability
pcm
  j ∈ (0, 1) (common mode: management override or a shared error for people; a regression after a model
update, drift or an injected instruction for a model), and otherwise each fails independently with probability
p j ∈ (0, 1); failures of different types are independent. A layer of type j costs c j a year. Each AI applica-
tion that performs or supports an in-scope control carries IT general controls (access, change management

including a retest at each model upgrade, operations and interface controls) costing Cg a year for applica-
tion g, and the cost of accountability Cacct (certifications, audit committee, integrated audit, directors’ and
officers’ insurance) is common to all designs.

Intuition. Control layers that share a model share its failures, so stacking them buys little against the failures
that matter most, and the expected loss from misstatements they jointly miss is a floor that neither cheaper
inference nor larger scale removes. Accountability is therefore not a headcount that automation shrinks to
zero: part of it is fixed in dollars and falls with scale, and part of it is an expected loss set by the diversity of
the control design. The companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026) embeds the proposition in a
model of internal control over financial reporting, including the scope of AI applications and the timing of
cutovers.

Corollary 4 (Upper bound under full automation, at full cost). Under the tax treatment of Remark 1, let
replacement capital spending δK k be part of a, as in Assumption 4, so that a ≥ amin + cfull + δK k with
cfull := cmnt + csec + cerr + canc . Then the saturated score satisfies
                                                                    
                 R∞ = (1 − τ) 1 − ch − cc − f − a + X − (1 − ψτ)k G
                                                                                    
                    ≤ Rmax := (1 − τ) 1 − cc − f − amin − cfull − δK k + X − (1 − ψτ)k G,

with equality if and only if automation is complete (ch = 0 and a = amin + cfull + δK k). For the Rule of 40
(X = 1) with no deduction for growth capital (ψ = 0) and no replacement (δK = 0),
                                                                               
                   Rmax = (1 − τ) 1 − cc − f − amin − cmnt − csec − cerr − canc + (1 − k) G,                    (29)

which reaches R̄ at k = 0 if and only if G ≥ R̄−(1−τ)(1−cc − f −amin −cfull ). Full expensing (ψ = 1) adds
τkG, and replacement at rate δK subtracts (1 − τ)δK k. At any share, R∗X (s) − R∞ equals the after-tax growth
surplus, which vanishes as s → s̄, and the time integral of any excess over R∞ is bounded as in Proposition 4.

Intuition. Full automation removes human cost and pushes the cost floor to its accountability minimum, but
compute, fees, taxes and the cost of running AI safely remain, and so does market growth net of the capital
it requires. The ceiling is a saturated score: any excess over it is a growth surplus, which is transient by
Proposition 4. Observed scores above Rmax must therefore reflect one of three things: a growth surplus;
rents on scarce inputs, as in Proposition 8; or a margin concept more generous than after-tax free cash flow.
The ceiling also holds output prices fixed. Under free replication, prices fall toward full cost and the score
falls toward XG + (r − G)k + ρ (Proposition 8), far below Rmax . Section 7.2 grounds the parameters and
evaluates the ceiling in Table 10.




                                                         23
3.10    The attainable frontier over time
The frontier of Proposition 1 and the ceiling are static: they describe where a firm can be once a transforma-
tion is complete. Getting there takes time. Transitions run in parallel with legacy systems, fail and roll back,
and draw on limited organizational capacities: how many affected employees the firm can absorb, how many
initiatives its leaders can decide and govern at once, how many deployments its security team can review,
and how deep a score dip its owners tolerate. Leaders, strategies and structures also change before the work
is done. This subsection takes these capacities and hazards as primitives and turns them into a frontier that
is attainable by a given horizon. The companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026) derives each
capacity, the success probability of a transition and the path of the dip from a task-level model of the firm;
nothing below depends on those derivations.

Assumption 8 (Transformation program). At date 0 the firm has after-tax parameters ϑ0 = (m0 , k, ln λ , G)
and can migrate a finite set P of N processes. Migrating process p with realized benefit share b ∈ [0, 1] adds
b δ ϑ p to the parameters, where δ ϑ p is the process’s increment at full cost, net of the floors of Definition 3
and including its capital. Write ϑZ := ϑ0 + ∑ p∈Z δ ϑ p . Migrations run in waves whose length is the parallel
run Tpar : wave w occupies [(w − 1)Tpar , wTpar ), and a process started in it is embedded, so that its savings are
durable, at (w − 1)Tpar + Temb , with Temb ≥ Tpar . The processes started in one wave use at most Γi of each
resource i, with uses cip ≥ 0: affected employees against the change capacity of the wave; decision units
against the load beyond which further initiatives lower value; deployments against security-review capacity;
and parallel-run premia, as shares of revenue, against the dip the owner tolerates.

   Let Zn be the family of sets of processes that can be split into n waves within capacity, with Z0 = {0},
                                                                                                         /
and let n(t) := ⌊(t − Temb )/Tpar ⌋ + 1 for t ≥ Temb and n(t) := 0 before: the number of waves embedded by t.

Definition 4 (Attainable and expected frontiers). (a) The set attainable by t is At := Z ∈Zn(t) F (ϑZ ),
                                                                                               S

      where F (ϑ ) is the feasible set of Definition 1 at ϑ . Its undominated points form the attainable
                     X (t) := supAt RX . The static full-cost set is A∞ := Z ⊆P F (ϑZ ).
      frontier, and Ratt
                                                                          S

  (b) (Risk.) Each attempt to migrate p works with probability ϖ p , realizes a share b p with mean b̄ p , and is
      disrupted before it embeds with probability 1 − e−νD Temb , where νD := ν L (1 − pkeep ) + ν V is the rate
      of leadership changes whose new leaders do not keep the program (leaders change at rate ν L and keep
      it with probability pkeep ) plus the rate ν V of changes of strategy. Outcomes are revealed by the end
      of the wave, or at the disruption, and all draws are independent. A failed or disrupted attempt reverts
      the process, which may be attempted again in a later wave, and the firm may revert any process at any
      time. Reorganizations arrive at rate ν R and freeze the program for Tfrz each, which slows its clock by
      the factor 1 + ν R Tfrz , so tR := t/(1 + ν R Tfrz ) is program time. A plan chooses the attempts in each
      wave, within capacity, as a function of what it has observed, and ϑt is ϑ0 plus b p δ ϑ p for each process
      embedded by t. The expected frontier of a plan is m̄t (g) := E m∗ (g; ϑt ), and the expected attainable
      score is R̄X (t) := supplans E R∗X (ϑt ).
  (c) Process p is X-improving if ∇R∗X · δ ϑ p = δ m0p − g∗ δ k p + (X − k)(∆∗ δ ln λ p + δ G p ) ≥ 0 at every
      ϑ0 + ∑q xq δ ϑq with x ∈ [0, 1]N (Proposition 5). A cost saving qualifies, and so does a capital-for-labor
      switch that meets Corollary 2 at the largest optimal growth rate.

Proposition 11 (The attainable frontier). Under Assumption 8:




                                                        24
  (a) (Monotonicity.) At ⊆ At ′ for t ≤ t ′ , and At grows weakly with each capacity Γi and shrinks weakly
      with each use cip and with Temb . For every X ≥ 0, Ratt                       ∗
                                                               X (t) = maxZ ∈Zn(t) RX (ϑZ ) has the same mono-
      tonicity, equals R∗X (ϑ0 ) for t < Temb , and is at most Ratt                  ∗
                                                                X (∞) := maxZ ⊆P RX (ϑZ ).

  (b) (Convergence.) Let n̄ be the least number of waves within capacity into which P can be split. Then
      At = A∞ for all t ≥ Temb + (n̄ − 1)Tpar . If every process fits a wave on its own, n̄ ≤ N; if Γi ≥ ∑ p cip
      for every i, n̄ = 1. As capacities grow, the attainable set therefore reaches the static one at Temb .
  (c) (Speed limit.) Every Z ∈ Zn(t) uses at most n(t)Γi of each resource. With identical uses ci , at most
      n(t) ncap processes are embedded by t, where ncap := mini ⌊Γi /ci ⌋, and embedding the whole program
      takes at least Temb + (⌈N/ncap ⌉ − 1)Tpar ≥ maxi Nci Tpar /Γi : no program moves faster than its scarcest
      capacity allows.
  (d) (Diminishing returns.) If each δ ϑ p changes only m0 and G, with X ≥ k, then R∗X (ϑZ ) = R∗X (ϑ0 ) +
                                                                                       ∗
      ∑ p∈Z ∆R p with ∆R p := δ m0p +(X −k)δ G p . For each resource i, Ratt  X (t) ≤ RX (ϑ0 )+Vi (n(t)Γi ), where
      Vi (C) := max{∑ p ∆R p x p : ∑ p cip x p ≤ C, x ∈ [0, 1]N } is nondecreasing, concave and piecewise linear
      and takes processes in decreasing order of ∆R p /cip .

    The realized score lies below the attainable frontier while transitions are in flight. At any date the
margin is the frontier margin at the embedded parameters plus the net flow of the processes in flight, which
is negative while parallel-run premia and one-time charges exceed the savings already ramped up: the J-
curve of Brynjolfsson et al. (2021) at the level of a program. If the dip the owner tolerates loosens as
savings accumulate, the dip capacity depends on what has been embedded; the monotonicity in (a) still
holds, because every plan that is feasible with less capacity remains feasible with more.

Proposition 12 (The expected frontier). Under Assumption 8, with the risks of Definition 4(b):
  (a) (Bounds.) R∗X (ϑ0 ) ≤ R̄X (t), and if every process is X-improving, R̄X (t) ≤ Ratt
                                                                                     X (tR ).

  (b) (Monotonicity.) R̄X (t) is nondecreasing in t, in each Γi and in each ϖ p , and nonincreasing in each
      cip , in νD (and so in ν L and ν V ; it is nondecreasing in pkeep ) and in ν R Tfrz . If every process is X-
      improving, it is also nonincreasing in Temb and nondecreasing in each b p in the sense of first-order
      stochastic dominance. A leadership change that also lowers the success probability of transitions, for
      example by removing their sponsor, acts in the same direction through ϖ p .
  (c) (Risk consumes capacity.) Let processes be identical, with per-attempt embedding probability pemb :=
      ϖe−νD Temb . Under every plan the expected number of processes embedded by t is at most N and at
      most pemb ncap n(tR ), and the second bound holds with equality for the plan that fills every wave if
      N ≥ ncap n(tR ). If each δ ϑ p changes only m0 and G, by ∆R ≥ 0 in score, then E R∗X (ϑt ) ≤ R∗X (ϑ0 ) +
      ∆R min{N, b̄ pemb ncap n(tR )}.
  (d) (Limits.) If every process fits a wave and has pembp := ϖ p e−νD Temb > 0, the plan that retries reverted
      processes embeds every process in finite time almost surely, and E R∗X (ϑt ) → E R∗X (ϑ0 + ∑ p b p δ ϑ p )
      as t → ∞. If every process is X-improving, this limit is at most R∗X (ϑP ), with shortfall (1 − b̄)N∆R
      in the setting of (c); and as capacities grow, ϖ p → 1, b p → 1 in probability and νD and ν R Tfrz fall to
      zero, R̄X (t) → Ratt
                        X (∞) for every t > Temb .

Intuition. The attainable frontier moves out in steps, one wave at a time, and at a decreasing rate, because
the best processes go first. It reaches the static frontier only once the binding capacity has had time to work
through the whole program. Risk changes this picture in two ways. Failures, rollbacks and disruptions by

                                                       25
new leaders or new strategies waste capacity: each embedded process costs 1/pemb attempts on average,
so these risks act like a cut in capacity by the factor pemb /(1 + ν R Tfrz ). They delay the approach to the
static frontier without lowering it. Partial realization of benefits, by contrast, lowers the level to which the
firm converges. Leadership and strategy hazards matter only while work is in flight, so they bear most on
programs that take long to embed. Lemma 5 in Appendix A adds that, when processes shift only m0 and k,
the expected frontier is the frontier at the expected parameters, and that it understates the expected maximal
score by the value of choosing growth after outcomes are known.
Remark 2 (Ownership regimes). An owner sets the weight X, the discount rate, the horizon and the tolerated
dip, and its decision speed scales the decision capacity; the companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy,
2026) derives these for public, private, private-equity and other owners. On a common technology, three
consequences follow. (i) Owners with different weights choose different points of the same attainable set:
a higher weight picks a point with weakly more growth, and, when the lower weight is positive and growth
is strictly higher, less margin. (ii) An owner who admits only the transitions that pay within its rate and
horizon admits a subset P ′ ⊆ P, whose attainable and static sets are contained in those of an owner who
admits all of P with the same capacities. (iii) Pace differs: a tighter dip budget, or slower decisions that
lower decision capacity, shrink At and lower R̄X (t) at every horizon (Propositions 11(a) and 12(b)), while
the static frontier of a given program is unchanged.

3.11   Beyond software: economic profit and ROIC
The Rule of 40 is a software convention; the model is not. It needs only growth bought with expensed
spend at diminishing returns, capital proportional to revenue and a margin bound. This subsection restates
the objective in value terms that apply to any industry. For a point (g, m) of a firm with capital intensity k
and cost of capital r, define the economic margin e := m + k(g − r): operating profit less the charge for the
invested capital kS, per dollar of revenue, so that eS is economic profit, ROIC net of the cost of capital times
invested capital. For k > 0, define the return on invested capital ROIC := (m + kg)/k.

Proposition 13 (Value-based objectives). Under Assumptions 1–4:
  (a) (One frontier, three coordinates.) The economic-margin frontier is e∗ (g) = m∗ (g) + k(g − r) = m0 −
      rk − κ(g − G)β . It is concave, has slope zero at G and is strictly decreasing beyond. For k > 0 the
      ROIC frontier is [m0 − κ(g − G)β ]/k. The same spend levels are efficient in all three coordinates.
  (b) (The Rule of X is a rule of economic profit.) For every (g, m), RX (g, m) = (X − k)g + e + rk =
      k [ROIC + (X/k − 1)g]. Maximizing the Rule of X is therefore the same as maximizing the economic
      margin plus growth at the weight X − k, the net value of growth, or ROIC plus growth at the weight
      qK − 1, where qK := X/k is the marginal value of a dollar of invested capital. Growth is bought if and
      only if qK > 1, as in Proposition 2(b) and (c).
  (c) (Value.) At a constant growth rate g < r and constant margins, V /S = m/(r − g) = k + e/(r − g)
      and V /(kS) = (ROIC − g)/(r − g) = 1 + (ROIC − r)/(r − g), and the FCF yield plus growth equals
      r. Iso-value lines in (g, e) pass through (r, 0). If v0 > k, then at the small-share point (g◦ , m◦ ) of
      Proposition 6(d) the one of slope −(v0 − k) is tangent to the economic-margin frontier: −e∗′ (g◦ ) =
      v0 − k and e∗ (g◦ ) = (v0 − k)(r − g◦ ).
  (d) (The Rule of 40 as a special case.) With k = 0, e = m, so the Rule of X in (g, m) is the economic-
      profit criterion at the firm’s own weight. With k > 0, the cash Rule of 40 is R1 = (1 − k)g + e + rk. It


                                                      26
      weights growth at 1 − k, not X − k, so for k ≥ 1 it penalizes growth whatever its return, and at a given
      economic margin and growth rate it changes with capital intensity at the rate r − g.

     Every result in this section is proved from Assumptions 1–4, and from Assumptions 5 and 6 where they
are stated. Each therefore holds for any firm that satisfies them, with k its capital per dollar of revenue
including working capital, and by (b) each statement about RX translates into one about economic profit,
ROIC and value. Outside software three conditions need checking. Growth must be bought largely with
expensed spend at diminishing returns, as the evidence on customer acquisition suggests for firms in general
(Fitzgerald et al., 2024; Argente et al., 2025). Capital must scale with revenue, which fails where capacity
is lumpy (Remark 4(a) treats fixed costs). Headroom must shrink with share, which fails under network
effects (Remark 4(d)). What is specific to software is the conventions and the numbers: the pass mark of 40
and the screening weights of 2 to 3; the empirical results of Section 5 and the calibrated range of α; the cost
structure behind Table 10 (compute, fees and the accountability floor), and with it the ceiling thresholds; and
the horizon ranges of Section 7. For a capital-intensive firm the right screen is the economic margin plus
growth at the net weight X − k. At k = 1.5 and 15% growth with r = 0.10, for example, the cash Rule of
40 scores a firm 7.5 points below an asset-light firm with the same economic margin and growth, and each
further point of growth lowers its score by half a point.


4     Data and Measurement
4.1   Sample
We build a firm-year panel from the XBRL financial data that companies file with the U.S. Securities and
Exchange Commission (U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, 2026b). The universe is every EDGAR
registrant whose standard industrial classification (SIC) code is 7370 to 7374 (computer programming, data
processing and related services). The EDGAR company index by SIC code lists inactive as well as active
registrants, so firms later acquired or delisted remain in the universe and the sample does not condition on
survival. For the 662 registrants in this universe that ever report at least $50 million of revenue, we retrieve
every fact they filed in annual reports (forms 10-K, 20-F and 40-F, including amendments) for 35 US-GAAP
elements.
    Every item for a fiscal year is taken as first reported: from the original annual filing for that year, the
earliest-filed annual report that contains a revenue fact for the fiscal year. Growth is computed within that
same filing, which reports the prior year alongside the current one. This matters. Later filings restate prior
years, most often when a divested business is reclassified as discontinued operations, so mixing vintages
produces spurious growth; for example, the restated continuing-operations revenue of AppLovin for 2023
is 35% below its originally reported 2022 total, while growth as first reported was +17%. Revenue is the
largest revenue element reported for the year in that filing, which prevents the selection of a component.
Items that the original filing does not report are taken from the earliest later filing that does; operating
income, operating cash flow, capital expenditure and stock-based compensation come from the original filing
in 96.5 to 99.8% of the core firm-years that report them. Fiscal years follow the Compustat convention: a
year ending in January to May is assigned to the previous calendar year. We drop seven duplicate registrants
(for example, a holding company and its operating subsidiary filing identical financial statements) and the
overlapping years of three predecessor–successor pairs.
    Our core sample is prepackaged software (SIC 7372), the industry to which the Rule of 40 is usually
applied. The broader SIC 7370–7374 universe, which adds internet platforms, information-technology ser-

                                                      27
vices, payments and data processing, serves as a robustness check. The analysis sample keeps firm-years
in fiscal years 2010 to 2025 with prior-year revenue of at least $100 million and growth between −90%
and 500%; margins outside −300% to 100% are set to missing. The core sample contains 1,929 firm-years
for 293 firms: 517 in 2010–15, 458 in 2016–19, 496 in 2020–22 and 458 in 2023–25. The broad sample
contains 3,626 firm-years for 541 firms. In the core sample, 88% of firm-years belong to registrants with a
U.S. business address; the rest are mostly foreign private issuers reporting under US GAAP, whose dollar
growth includes currency movements.

4.2     Growth and margin definitions
Revenue growth is git = Sit /Si,t−1 − 1. We study four margin definitions, each scaled by revenue:
   1. GAAP operating margin, mop : operating income.
   2. Adjusted operating margin, madj : operating income plus SBC, close to the adjusted margins behind
      company-reported scores such as Palantir’s (Palantir Technologies Inc., 2026).
   3. Free-cash-flow margin, mfcf : operating cash flow (from continuing operations where reported) minus
      capital expenditure and capitalized software. It is missing when capital expenditure is not reported
      (2.2% of firm-years) rather than set to zero.
   4. Free-cash-flow margin after SBC, mfcf−sbc : free cash flow minus SBC, which treats equity com-
      pensation as the economic cost it is and corresponds to the free-cash-flow margin of the model. A
      robustness version also deducts additions to finance-lease right-of-use assets, so that lease-financed
      capacity counts as capital spending.
The Rule-of-40 score is R = g + m under each definition. Capital intensity is capital expenditure plus
capitalized software over revenue. Following the model, we treat selling and marketing expense as the
observable part of discretionary growth spend.

4.3     Acquisitions and discontinued operations
Purchased growth is not the growth the model describes. We flag a firm-year as acquisition-affected when
cash paid for acquisitions or the increase in goodwill over the fiscal year is at least 25% of prior-year
revenue, or when reported pro forma revenue exceeds actual revenue by at least 10%; because acquired
revenue arrives mostly in the following year, we flag the following year as well. The flag covers 24% of core
firm-years and 37% of those with growth above 50%. Six percent of core firm-years report discontinued
operations.

4.4     Two estimands
We report two explicitly defined estimands, because a reported score and the frontier of the model answer
different questions.
      • E1, observed scores. All firm-years of the sample, with growth and margins as first reported. E1 is
        primary for descriptive statistics about reported scores: their distribution, pass rates, the trend in their
        upper quantiles and the comparison of measurement bases.




                                                         28
      • E2, the organic frontier. Growth net of acquired growth: we remove acquired revenue with the pro
        forma adjustment where the filing discloses it, and otherwise exclude the acquisition-affected firm-
        years. E2 is primary for the findings that confront the model, whose growth is bought with expensed
        spend within one market: the upper-tail slope of margin on growth, the growth weights implied by
        spending, the size and capital-intensity gradients and the gap between firms near the frontier and
        typical firms. The calibration of Section 7 already uses an organic sample.
The organic frontier contains 1,516 firm-years for 283 firms, of which 59 acquisition-affected firm-years are
kept with pro forma organic growth; the other acquisition-affected firm-years are excluded. Where both are
shown, we report each finding with its estimand; conclusions are stated under both. Robustness checks also
exclude firm-years with discontinued operations.

4.5     Why the measurement basis matters
The four definitions produce materially different scores for the same firm-year. In the core sample, 14.8% of
firm-years clear 40 on the GAAP basis and 16.9% after deducting SBC from free cash flow, against 26.7%
on the adjusted basis and 30.9% on free cash flow before SBC. Capital spending can change a score by more
than 100 points. CoreWeave, which builds and operates AI computing capacity, grew 168% in fiscal 2025 at
a GAAP operating margin of about zero, for a GAAP score of 167; its free-cash-flow margin after SBC was
−154%, for a cash-basis score of 14. Company-reported scores such as Palantir’s, which combine quarterly
year-over-year growth with an adjusted quarterly margin, are not comparable with any of the four annual
measures. We report the GAAP and free-cash-flow-after-SBC bases throughout and treat the latter as the
economically relevant one.


5     The Empirical Frontier, 2010–2025
This section measures the frontier that the model describes in the core software sample and confronts five
of its implications: the shape of the frontier, the growth technology, the decline of the attainable score with
scale, the cost of capital intensity and the change since 2016–19; it then asks where the distance to the
frontier sits. Each finding is labeled with its estimand (Section 4.4): observed scores (E1) for descriptive
statistics about reported scores, and the organic frontier (E2) for the findings that confront the model. Table 2
reports every model-facing statistic under both estimands, with paired bootstrap intervals for their difference.

5.1     Statistics
Because both growth and margin are desirable, the relevant object is a Pareto (output–output) frontier of
the points firms chose, which Definition 2 calls the observed frontier Ψ. For a growth level g, let S (g)
be the set of firm-years that grew at least that fast, and let φ (g) be the 95th percentile of margins in S (g),
a quantile-type partial frontier in the spirit of Aragon et al. (2005), adapted to two desirable outputs. A
natural summary is the best score attainable on this frontier, maxg [g + φ (g)]. We do not use it as a headline
statistic. The maximum sits at the edge of the growth support, where a handful of firm-years determine
it, and its value falls by 26 to 50 points as the minimum number of firm-years required at each growth
threshold rises from 15 to 100 (Table 15); the firm-cluster bootstrap is also not valid for such an extreme
statistic. We therefore report four statistics whose meaning does not depend on sample size: upper quantiles
of the score R = g + m; frontier points at fixed growth thresholds, T (g0 ) = g0 + φ (g0 ); the 90th percentile

5.2    The top of the distribution fell as fast growers became scarcer
This subsection describes observed scores (E1). Table 3 and Figure 3 show the distribution over time. The
median GAAP score fell from 23.6 in 2010–15 to 11.9 in 2023–25, and the share of firm-years clearing 40
fell from 18.6% to 9.9%. After deducting SBC from free cash flow the median fell from 24.0 to 15.2 and
the pass rate from 18.3% to 9.4%. Median growth fell from 20.6% in 2020–22 to 12.9% in 2023–25, while
the median GAAP margin recovered from −6.5% to −1.6%.
     The top of the distribution fell at least as much as the middle (Table 4). Between 2016–19 and 2023–25
the 95th percentile of the GAAP score fell by 12 points, from 56 to 44 (90% interval −21 to −7), and the
90th percentile by 8 points; after SBC the declines were 15 and 10 points. The decline does not depend on
any single firm: dropping each firm in turn leaves the change in the 95th percentile between −11 and −12
points on the GAAP basis and between −13 and −16 after SBC. It does not depend on acquired growth
either: on the organic frontier (E2) the 95th percentile fell by 12 points on the GAAP basis and 12 after
SBC.
     Frontier points at fixed growth thresholds, also computed on observed scores (E1), show no detectable
change (Table 5). The 95th-percentile GAAP margin among all firm-years, T (0), changed by +2 points
between 2016–19 and 2023–25 (90% interval −4 to +5), and after SBC by −3 points (−5 to +1). At
growth thresholds of 20% and 40% the changes range from −9 to +13 points on the two bases, and every

interval includes zero. The intervals are wide, so the evidence is an absence of detectable change rather than
proof of stability, and the 40% point for 2023–25 rests on only 18 firm-years, so it is close to the sample
maximum. On the organic frontier (E2) the 40% point rose, by 23 points on the GAAP basis (interval −9
to +40) and 24 after SBC (+3 to +38), but it rests on only 12 firm-years in 2023–25, and we do not read it
as evidence that the best margins at high growth improved. What changed clearly is how many firms grew
fast: only 18 firm-years grew at least 40% in 2023–25, against 57 to 98 in each earlier period, and 132 grew
at least 20%, against 175 to 255. The decline at the top of the score distribution coincided with a fall in the
supply of growth, with no detectable change in the margins available at a given growth rate except at the
thinly populated 40% threshold of the organic frontier.

5.3    At the top, a point of growth costs well under a point of margin
The upper-tail slope confronts the model, so its primary estimand is the organic frontier (E2). Figure 4 plots
all firm-years with the 90th percentile of margin in each growth band, and Table 6 reports the values for
observed scores (E1): the upper-decile GAAP margin falls from 29 points for firms growing 0–10% to 17
points at 30–40% and 12 to 13 points above 40%. On the organic frontier, the 0.90-quantile slope of margin
on growth between 0 and 60% is −0.59 (standard error 0.16) on the GAAP basis and −0.43 (0.11) after
deducting SBC from free cash flow. For observed scores it is −0.46 (0.14) and −0.37 (0.11), and −0.60
and −0.43 with one observation per firm and period (Table 14). Acquired growth flattens the GAAP slope:
the paired 90% interval for the difference between the estimands, 0.02 to 0.33, excludes zero, while on
the cash basis it does not (−0.01 to 0.22; Table 2). On the GAAP basis the slope was steeper in 2020–25

than in 2010–19 under both estimands (−0.77 against −0.31 for observed scores, −0.79 against −0.45 on
the organic frontier). After SBC the observed slope steepened from −0.25 to −0.40, but the organic slope
did not (−0.48 and −0.43), so the steepening of the cash-basis slope in observed scores reflects acquired
growth. On the organic frontier, then, a point of growth has come with about half a point less margin, and
never with a full point; in observed scores, with a third to a half of a point.
    The cross-sectional slope is far flatter than the slope of any firm’s own frontier at its chosen point, which
by Proposition 2 equals −X, and X is well above one (see below). Proposition 7 explains why the two
can differ: every firm faces a downward-sloping frontier, but the estimated frontier is drawn through the
points firms chose, not through their possibilities, and when firms with more growth opportunity also have
higher margin intercepts the chosen points slope down much less than any individual frontier. The cross-
section does not identify any firm’s marginal cost of growth (Proposition 7(iv)); a common frontier with
high opportunity can produce a similar slope. Within firms (E1), margin and growth are nearly unrelated:
with firm and year fixed effects the coefficient of GAAP margin on growth is +0.08 (standard error 0.07),
and after SBC −0.02 (0.04), against pooled coefficients of −0.21 and −0.24. Proposition 7(v) shows
that anticipated changes in opportunity would produce a strongly negative within-firm slope, so the near-


                                                                                 33
zero slope means that growth surprises against costs already committed, or demand shocks that also raise
margins, offset the cost of buying growth.

5.4   What the data do and do not say about the growth technology
The model’s growth technology has two parameters, demand pull λ h and the spend elasticity α, and the data
do not identify them separately. Under Proposition 2, an optimizing firm spends σ ∗ = α(X − k)∆∗ , so when
firms differ mainly in their growth opportunities, their spend and their excess growth move in proportion,
and a regression of log excess growth on log spend returns a slope of one whatever α is; it returns α only
when firms differ in the weight they put on growth. Slopes below one, such as those we obtain using
selling and marketing expense as the measure of spend, therefore mix these two sources of variation with
measurement error in the proxy and selection on growth. The level of spend is more informative, and its
estimand is the organic frontier (E2), because acquired growth adds to excess growth without any selling
spend. Among organic firm-years growing faster than the median, the median ratio of the previous year’s
selling-and-marketing share to growth in excess of the median is 3.3 (interquartile range 1.9 to 7.3); the
median selling-and-marketing share in this group is 41% of revenue and median excess growth 11.5 points.
Read through the first-order condition, the median ratio implies α(X − k) ≈ 3.3, so for α between 0.25
and 0.45 the weight on growth that firms act on is roughly 7 to 13 (7.2 to 13.0), well above the weights
of two to three used in investors’ screens. For observed scores (E1) the ratio is 2.9 and the implied weight
6.5 to 11.6: the conclusion is the same under both estimands. The proxy has biases of uncertain net sign:
part of selling and marketing expense maintains existing revenue rather than buying growth, which biases
the ratio upward, while growth spend in the model also includes development work aimed at expansion,
which the proxy omits. Proposition 6 explains why a weight of this size is natural: a value-maximizing firm
weights growth by the marginal value of a dollar of revenue, a valuation multiple. We therefore treat α as
a calibration parameter in [0.25, 0.45] and anchor the calibration of Section 7 on observed firms rather than
on a regression.

5.5   Scale
Proposition 3 predicts that the maximal score falls as market share rises. In observed scores (E1) the evidence
is weak (Table 7 and Figure 5). After SBC, the 95th percentile of the score is 64 for firms with $0.1–0.5
billion of prior revenue, 56 and 54 in the next two classes and 48 above $10 billion, and a 0.95-quantile
regression gives a slope of −2.5 points per log unit of revenue (standard error 1.2), about 5.7 points per
tenfold increase (2.8). On the GAAP basis there is no gradient (slope +0.7, standard error 1.3). Median
growth falls from 20.0% in the smallest class to about 10% in the largest two. The gradient does not survive
on the organic frontier (E2), the estimand that confronts the model: after SBC it is −2.7 points per tenfold
increase (standard error 3.4), and on the GAAP basis +3.4 (2.6). The paired 90% interval for the difference
between the estimands on the cash basis, −8.1 to −0.4 points, excludes zero, so the cash-basis gradient in
observed scores reflects acquired growth rather than saturation. Size is in any case a noisy proxy for market
share, and larger firms enjoy operating leverage that the model omits, so the data do not test saturation.

Proposition 5 implies that on a cash basis capital intensity lowers the maximal score in proportion to growth,
while operating margins are affected only through depreciation. We split each period at its median ratio of

confronts the model, the cash-basis gap is smaller but still significant: 11 points (37 against 48; interval −20
to −3), against 1 point on the GAAP basis (interval −8 to +5). A gap on the cash basis without one on
the GAAP basis is the pattern the capital channel predicts, but it is partly mechanical, because the sorting
variable is deducted from the cash margin, and it is not driven by builders of AI computing capacity: the
capital-intensive half of 2023–25 starts at only 2% of revenue, and the firms near its top are mostly ordinary
software and services firms. CoreWeave, described in Section 4, illustrates the mechanism at the extreme.

5.7    The distance to the frontier widened, mainly through new entrants
For observed scores (E1), the stochastic frontier estimates measure how far the typical firm sits below the
frontier. The mean shortfall implied by the half-normal inefficiency term on the GAAP basis rose from 18
margin points in 2010–15 to 25, 32 and 29 in the next three periods, and after SBC from 14 to 20, 27 and 24.
The rise comes mainly from firms that entered the sample in 2019 or later: in 2023–25 their mean shortfall
is 31 points on the GAAP basis and 28 after SBC, against 22 and 15 for firms already in the sample before
2019.

5.8      Where the distance to the frontier sits
The gap between firms near the frontier and typical firms confronts the model, so its primary estimand is
the organic frontier (E2); Table 8 reports it for observed scores (E1), and the two agree. Because operating
margin is gross margin minus selling and marketing, research and development, general and administrative
and other operating expense, the margin gap between firms near the frontier and typical firms at the same
growth rate splits exactly into gaps in each line. Table 8 compares, within each growth band, firm-years in
the top decile of GAAP operating margin with those between the 40th and 60th percentiles, for the 76% of
firm-years with growth of 0 to 60% that report all four lines. On the organic frontier, the top decile earns
on average 32.7 points more margin across bands (90% interval 27 to 36), of which selling and marketing
accounts for 15.7; among firms growing less than 10% a year gross margin accounts for 16.5 of 38.6 points,
and among firms growing 40 to 60% selling and marketing for 27.7 of 37.1. Observed scores give the same
picture. On average across bands the top decile earns 34.6 points more margin (90% interval 28.9 to 37.9).
Selling and marketing accounts for 16.0 points of the gap (12.5 to 19.9), research and development for 7.6,
gross margin for 5.5 and general and administrative expense for 5.1. The composition changes with growth.
Among firms growing less than 10% a year, gross margin is the largest component (14.9 of 37.3 points) and
selling and marketing one of the smallest (4.8). Among firms growing 40 to 60%, selling and marketing
accounts for 28.2 of 39.5 points and research and development for another 13.2: the best fast growers buy the
same growth with far less selling and marketing expense, which in the model is higher demand pull λ h. The
decomposition is descriptive. Firms near the frontier may spend less on selling because their products pull
demand, not because they sell more efficiently, so the table shows where margins differ, not which changes
would close the gap. It agrees, however, with the ordering of Corollary 3: the growth engine matters most
for fast growers and overhead for slow growers, while the corollary values savings in cost of revenue only
to the extent pricing lets the firm keep them.

5.9      Robustness
Table 2 sets the two estimands side by side and repeats the organic frontier with acquisition flags at 10%,
25% and 50% of prior-year revenue. Table 14 in Appendix D, on observed scores (E1), repeats the two


                                                                       36
central statistics, the decline in the 95th percentile of the score and the upper-decile slope, using one obser-
vation per firm and period; restricting the sample to filers with a U.S. address; excluding acquisition-affected
firm-years and firm-years with growth of at least 100% for which neither acquisition payments nor goodwill
are reported; excluding firm-years with discontinued operations; deducting finance-lease additions from free
cash flow; and using the broad SIC 7370–7374 sample. In every variant the 95th percentile falls by 10 to 26
points between 2016–19 and 2023–25 (computed before rounding), and the upper-decile slope lies between
−0.24 and −0.60. In the broad sample, which adds internet platforms such as Meta and AppLovin, the
slope is flatter (−0.31 on the GAAP basis), because several of these platforms combined fast growth with
very high margins. Table 15 documents the sensitivity of the edge statistic that we do not use.

5.10    Summary
Six facts carry into the rest of the paper; each is stated for its primary estimand, and for both where they
differ. (i) On the organic frontier (E2), among the best-performing software firms a point of growth has
come with about half a point less margin (−0.59 on the GAAP basis, −0.43 after SBC), well short of the
one-for-one trade the Rule of 40 line suggests; in observed scores (E1) the slope is flatter on the GAAP
basis, a third to a half of a point, because acquired growth flattens it. Because the cross-section records
choices, neither slope measures any firm’s own trade-off. (ii) The data do not identify the curvature of the
growth technology, but on the organic frontier the level of growth spending implies that firms act on a weight
on growth of roughly 7 to 13 (6.5 to 11.6 in observed scores), well above screening weights and consistent
with valuing growth at the marginal value of revenue. (iii) In observed scores the upper tail of the cash-basis
score declines modestly with scale, but the gradient disappears once acquired growth is removed, so it is
not evidence of saturation. (iv) Since 2023, the upper tail of the cash-basis score has been 11 points lower
for capital-intensive firms on the organic frontier (15 in observed scores), with no comparable gap on the
GAAP basis. (v) In observed scores, between 2016–19 and 2023–25 the top of the score distribution fell
by 12 to 15 points (12 and 12 on the organic frontier) as the number of fast growers fell, with no detectable
change in the best margins attainable at a given growth rate. (vi) On the organic frontier, firms near the
frontier earn about 33 points more operating margin than typical firms at the same growth rate (about 35
in observed scores); among slow growers the difference lies mostly in gross margin and overhead, among
fast growers mostly in selling and marketing expense. For a sense of scale, Palantir, whose second-quarter
2026 score was 155 on its adjusted margin and 140 on its GAAP margin (Palantir Technologies Inc., 2026),
scores 88 on the annual GAAP basis for fiscal 2025 (56% growth and a 32% margin), the third-highest value
in 2023–25 and the highest among firms with more than $1 billion of revenue other than CoreWeave, whose
GAAP score does not reflect its capital spending.


6      Measuring the Score When Growth Is Bought
The frontier of Proposition 1 prices growth bought with expensed spend at diminishing returns within one
market, and the score budget of Proposition 4 limits how long a firm can score above its saturated level. A
firm that grows by acquiring other firms buys growth with capital instead, across many markets. This section
shows how the Rule of X should be measured for such a firm and brackets the score of a serial acquirer that
listed in 2026. Section 4 flags acquisition-affected firm-years and Table 14 repeats the main results without
them; Proposition 14 says why such firm-years are not points on the frontier, and Remark 3 how to score
them when they are kept. A companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026) treats the acquirer’s


                                                      37
transformation economics; nothing below depends on it. Proofs are in Appendix C.

Definition 5 (Organic and acquired growth). Revenue grows organically at gorg , the growth of the businesses
the firm already owns, and by acquisition at gacq , the revenue run-rate acquired per unit of time as a share of
revenue, so that g = gorg + gacq ; annually, gorg = Stcn /St−1 − 1 and gacq = Stac /St−1 , where Scn is the revenue
of businesses owned throughout the year and Sac that of businesses acquired in it. Acquisitions cost kacq per
dollar of acquired revenue, so that the cash paid for them, Cacq , is kacq gacq of revenue. The organic score is
  org
RX := Xgorg + m and the score that charges acquisitions as growth capital is RA                       acq acq
                                                                                     X := Xg + m − k g . With
interest cfin per dollar of revenue, the score after interest is Xg + mop − cfin .

Proposition 14 (The score when growth is bought). Under Assumptions 1–4 and Definition 5:
                             org
  (a) (Net weight.) RA                  acq acq
                      X = RX + (X − k )g . Acquired growth raises the score if and only if k

      raises the Rule of 40 if and only if acquired revenue costs less than a dollar per dollar. A score that
      counts acquired growth without its price, Xg + m, overstates RA       acq acq
                                                                      X by k g .

  (b) (Bang-bang acquisition.) If revenue can be acquired at the constant price kacq up to the rate ḡacq and
      acquisitions leave the growth technology of the businesses already owned unchanged, the maximum
      of RA
          X over spend σ and g
                                acq ∈ [0, ḡacq ] is


                                                   ∗         acq + acq
                                             RA∗
                                              X = RX + (X − k ) ḡ ,                                          (30)

      with R∗X of (7): the firm buys all the revenue on offer if X > kacq and none if X < kacq . Unlike spend,
      acquisitions meet no diminishing returns until the supply ḡacq binds.
  (c) (Financing.) The margins of Assumptions 3 and 4 exclude interest. Debt-financed acquisitions leave
      the operating-margin score unchanged and lower the score after interest by cfin .
  (d) (The existing base.) With net revenue retention NRR on subscription revenue Ssub , the existing base
      contributes (Ssub /S)(NRR − 1) to annual organic growth. Its zero-spend growth is Gi = ln NRR, so
      by Remark 4(b) its saturated score is R∞ = m0 + (X − k) ln NRR, below m0 when NRR < 1. New-
      customer revenue, and so gorg , is not identified by NRR.
  (e) (Share headroom is bought.) Proposition 4, extended by Remark 4(b), applies business by business:
      a business bought at share sb in its own market can lift its organic score above its saturated level for
      a total of at most (X − k)[ln(s̄b /sb ) + (G − Gi,b )T ] over T years. The firm’s budget is the sum over
      the businesses it owns, so each acquisition buys a fresh budget at kacq per dollar of revenue: the finite
      headroom of one market becomes a limit on capital and on the supply of targets.
  (f) (Treadmill.) Holding growth at g requires gacq = g − gorg , so RA                 acq      org
                                                                        X = Xg + m − k (g − g ), which
                acq
      falls by k for each unit by which the businesses owned shrink. If NRR < 1 and new customers do
      not offset the decline, gorg < 0 and acquisitions must supply more growth than the firm reports.

Intuition. Growth bought with capital is the model’s growth capital (Assumption 4) at the price paid for
revenue rather than at k. It enters the score at the net weight X − kacq , exactly as the net value of a unit
of organic growth is X − k (equation (3)). A Rule-of-40 screen that adds acquired growth to an operating
margin therefore credits every acquisition, whatever it cost; for X = 1 any purchase above one times revenue
lowers the properly charged score. Because acquisitions do not draw down the headroom of the markets the
firm is already in, the transience result of Corollary 1 binds each business, not the firm, and a high score
sustained by acquisition is evidence of access to targets and capital, not of a frontier above the ceiling of

                                                        38
Corollary 4. Part (f) is why that access matters: a firm whose base shrinks must keep buying to stand still.
Acquirer returns are persistent across deals (Golubov et al., 2015), but bidders that buy often gain on private
and subsidiary targets and lose on public ones (Fuller et al., 2002), and a high and uneven acquisition rate
lowers performance (Laamanen and Keil, 2008), so the supply ḡacq at a given kacq need not last.
Remark 3 (Bounds on the charged score). Let mcf be a cash margin that excludes the cash paid for acqui-
                                                                     acq
sitions (operating cash flow over revenue, for instance), and let Cv be the cash paid in year v. With the
                                                     life
capital recovery factor CRF(r, T life ) = r/(1 − e−rT ), the level annual charge that repays a dollar at rate r
over T life years,
                                   acq                                       acq
                               C                                       Cv
                    gt + mtcf − t ≤ gt + mtcf − CRF(r, T life ) ∑             ≤ gt + mtcf ,                  (31)

                                                               t−T <v≤t
                                                                        S t     | {z }
                    |      {z      } |               {z                     }   uncharged

                                                         annuity

where the first inequality holds whenever the annuity on outlays still within their life is below the current
outlay, as it is when outlays grow quickly. Organic growth is bought with spend that is expensed, so its cost
lowers the margin term in the year it is spent (σ in Assumption 3). Acquired growth is paid for in investing
cash flow: it raises the growth term, and on a cash basis its price never touches the score. On the GAAP
basis it touches the score only through the amortization of acquired finite-lived intangibles, since goodwill
is not amortized (Deloitte, 2026; IFRS Foundation, 2022). The uncharged score thus omits the term kacq gacq
of part (a). The fully charged score charges each outlay in the year it is paid, as the cash basis charges capital
spending kg. But a deal closed during year t adds only part of a year’s revenue to gt and the rest to gt+1 ,
which is why Section 4 also flags the following year, so the full price is set against part of the growth it
bought: the fully charged score is a lower bound, not an estimate. The annuity score spreads each outlay
over the life of the revenue it buys, as depreciation spreads capital spending.
The company’s own split. Bending Spoons S.p.A., which acquires and transforms software businesses and
listed on Nasdaq in July 2026 (Dealroom News, 2026), gives the cleanest illustration. In the second quarter
of 2026 its revenue grew 126% on a year earlier, and it put its organic growth at 3% on its own definition,
which compares revenue with that of the same businesses a year earlier, using estimated pre-acquisition
revenue for businesses bought since (Bending Spoons S.p.A., 2026c). At a GAAP operating margin of 34%,
                                                                     org
the reported Rule-of-40 score is about 160 and the organic score R1 about 37. By part (a), the gap of 123
points is acquired growth credited at the weight X = 1 with no charge for its price. These are the company’s
measure for one quarter, not our estimate.
Worked numbers. The company reports its 2024 and 2025 accounts in its Form F-1, filed on 8 June 2026
(Bending Spoons S.p.A., 2026a); every figure we use is unchanged in the final prospectus (Bending Spoons

margin, 87.5 and 105.0 after interest by part (c), and 103.9 and 116.9 on operating cash flow. On the GAAP
basis acquisitions reached the score through amortization and impairment of acquired intangibles, 12.8% and
11.5% of revenue. The company’s adjusted operating margin adds that charge back, with transaction expense
(6.5% of revenue in 2025) and reorganization expense (6.0%), and a score on it would be 141.6 in 2025:
its add-backs are the costs of buying and restructuring businesses, so it lies further from RA

     The measure of spend cuts both ways. The net investing outflow includes capital spending and other
items that our extract does not separate, which makes both charged scores too low. The company’s own
measure of capital deployed in acquisitions exceeded the net investing outflow in 2023 and in the first
quarter of 2026, which cuts the other way. If operating cash flow is stated after interest paid, the annuity
at the cost of capital charges financing twice. Leaving out outlays before 2023, which the extract does not
report, makes the annuity charge too small. Vimeo shows the timing mismatch: it closed on 24 November
2025 for $1,359 million, so its price was paid in 2025, while 327 days of its 2025 revenue, $376.9 million,
preceded the close and are not in the firm’s 2025 revenue. The pro forma statements for 2025, which add
AOL, Eventbrite and Vimeo for the full year, show revenue of $2,608.5 million, 2.0 times actual, and the
three purchase prices were 1.7 to 3.2 times annual revenue (Bending Spoons S.p.A., 2026b). At those prices
kacq > X = 1, so by part (a) each point of growth they buy lowers the fully charged Rule-of-40 score by 0.7 to
2.2 points; charged as an annuity it costs 0.19 to 0.47 points and raises the score. The charging convention,
not the data, decides the sign. With NRR of 95%, the existing subscription base shrank by about 5% in
2025 (Gi = −5.1%; −9.4% at 2024’s 91%). By part (d) its saturated score therefore lies below its margin
intercept, and by part (f) net growth needs new customers or acquisitions. The split between the two for
2024 and 2025, and with it gorg , is not in the filings we read; we do not infer it.


                                                              40
The acquisition flag. Section 4 flags a firm-year when cash paid for acquisitions or the increase in goodwill
reaches 25% of prior-year revenue, or pro forma revenue exceeds actual revenue by 10%, and it flags the
year after as well. The flag covers 24% of core firm-years and 37% of those growing faster than 50%. Its
cash threshold is a statement about the charge in (31): a firm-year flagged on cash paid and growing at g has
a fully charged correction of at least 25%/(1 + g) of revenue, 17 points at 50% growth. That is of the order
of the 10 to 26 point fall in the 95th percentile of the score that Table 14 reports, so uncharged firm-years of
this kind could move the upper tail by as much as the change being measured; the table repeats the results
without them and finds the fall within that range. The firm above would carry the flag in every year shown.
Its net investing outflows were 2.3 and 2.8 times prior-year revenue in 2024 and 2025, so it would escape the
flag only if less than 11% and 9% of them paid for acquisitions. The capital it deployed in acquisitions in
the first quarter of 2026 alone was 1.5 times its 2025 revenue, and its pro forma revenue for 2025 exceeded
actual revenue by 100%, against the pro forma threshold of 10%. Table 14 removes such firm-years from
the frontier estimates. When they are kept, (31) is how to score them.
What the numbers can and cannot show. The bounds are wide, from −24.8 to 116.9 in 2025, because the
extract splits neither growth nor spend. The final prospectus gives prices and full-year revenue for the three
businesses in its pro forma statements, and the company reports organic growth for one quarter; a split of
2024 and 2025 growth and spend by business is not in the filings we read. What the bounds do show is that
the GAAP score of 115.9 rests on growth whose price the score leaves out. Under the scenario life, charging
that price as an annuity puts the 2025 score between 84.4 and 92.8. A score this high is not evidence that
the frontier of Proposition 1 can exceed the ceiling of Corollary 4: by part (e) it is consistent with ordinary
frontiers in each acquired business plus capital spent on buying new budgets. The case is one firm, selected
because it reached a listing, and its organic growth is measured only by the company, for one quarter.


7     How High Can the Score Go? Limits by Horizon
We now combine the model with the estimates to bound the score under full automation and in 2028, 2031
and 2036. Throughout we use the free-cash-flow-after-SBC basis and X = 1 (the Rule of 40 itself), and we
vary the spend elasticity over α ∈ [0.25, 0.45] because the data do not identify it (Section 5). The question
whether, with everything that can be automated automated, the limit is a Rule of 100 or a Rule of 1000 has
a precise answer in the model: the limit depends on the margin intercept, market growth, capital intensity,
demand pull and the spend elasticity, and automation moves only some of them. How fast a firm approaches
that limit depends on its capacity to execute transitions that are risky (Section 3.10). Every number in this
section is conditional on the scenario values it states; the replication package records the grounding of each
parameter in an assumptions register, with four classes: sourced, grounded by precedent, calibrated from
our own data, and scenario values.

7.1   How AI enters the parameters
Proposition 9 maps the effects of AI onto the model’s parameters; the companion paper (Bangalore Sreeni-
vasa Murthy, 2026) derives the mapping from the tasks a firm automates. Automation of general, adminis-
trative and maintenance work lowers the non-discretionary cost a and raises the margin intercept m0 = γ − a,
but it adds the recurring costs of running AI (Definition 3). Inference costs enter gross margin γ through
the compute share cc . Survey evidence puts gross margins on AI products near 45% in 2025 and projects
53% in 2026 and 59% in 2027 (ICONIQ, 2026), below the median gross margin of 74% in our core sample


                                                      41
in 2023–25, so automation shifts cost from operating expense into cost of revenue rather than removing
it. The price of reaching a fixed level of benchmark performance has fallen by between 9 and 900 times
a year depending on the threshold (Cottier et al., 2025), but competition may pass much of that decline to
customers, so its effect on γ depends on pricing power (Lemma 3); and with a short-run price elasticity of
demand for inference just above one (Demirer et al., 2025), spending on inference stays roughly constant as
its price falls, so the compute share need not decline. AI can raise demand pull λ h for firms whose products
substitute for labor and market growth G where spending on wages converts into spending on software, and
firms that own computing capacity raise k. At given prices, automation raises the saturated score by 1 − τ
points for every point of cost it removes net of the costs it adds, up to the ceiling of Corollary 4; where entry
is free and replication cheap, it does not, because the margin it creates is competed away (Proposition 8).

7.2   The ceiling under full automation
Corollary 4 gives the score of a firm that has automated everything it can, spends nothing on growth and
holds a saturated share. Its parameters are brackets, not estimates.
     Accountability floor. The floor amin collects the costs of being accountable that automation does not
remove. They are largely fixed in dollars, so as a share of revenue they shrink with scale (Remark 4(a)). We
use a band from 0.5% to 4.6% of revenue and the values 1.5%, 3% and 4.6% in the favorable, central and
conservative cases. The upper end is the fifth-percentile general and administrative share of firms with $1 to
10 billion of revenue in our panel, and the central 3% equals the tenth-percentile share of firms with more
than $10 billion, so it assumes that none of that expense is automated. Proposition 10 grounds the lower
end from below. In the illustrative example of Remark 10, a firm with $1 billion of revenue has a bottom-up
floor of about 0.24% of revenue: direct audit and Section 404 costs of about 0.14% plus 0.10% for the best
control design, one human layer over two AI layers, against 0.20% for the best all-human design and 0.17%
for the best all-AI design, of which 0.12% is a common-mode loss that no further AI layer removes and that
does not fall with scale. Each further AI application in the scope of internal control over financial reporting
adds a line of IT general controls of about 0.035% of revenue at $1 billion, or about 0.05% if it also needs a
manual fallback for the close, so the floor reaches the band’s lower end at about 9 such applications per $1
billion of revenue, or 6 with fallbacks (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026). The lower end of the band is
thus conditional on how many in-scope AI applications a firm runs, and the three cases stand.
     Taxes, fees and compute. The cash tax rate τ of 15 to 21% runs from the 15% corporate alternative
minimum tax to the 21% federal rate of 26 U.S.C. 11(b), and excludes state taxes. Average effective rates
of profitable large corporations were lower still, 9 to 16% in 2014–18 according to GAO report GAO-23-
105384. Fees f of 2 to 3% of revenue are those of card processing. The compute share cc has no measured
analog, so it is a scenario parameter. Its credible bounds run from 1% (labor replaced at today’s observed
inference cost) to 55% (the 2025 cost of revenue of AI products, ICONIQ, 2026). We use 15 to 25%, and
a high-compute case of 40%: roughly the cost of revenue implied by the gross margins projected for AI
products in 2027, or the blend of a firm whose revenue comes entirely from AI products. For comparison,
the median core software firm’s entire cost of revenue was 26% of revenue in 2023–25.
     Full-cost floors. Appendix Table 13 evaluates Lemma 4. Security starts from today’s security spending
of 0.69% of revenue, reported in a survey of chief information security officers, and scales it to full automa-
tion; maintenance applies sourced upkeep rates and observed model-retirement intervals to a scenario stock
of builds; and failure losses combine measured omission, hallucination, catch and provider-incident rates
with scenario loss severities. Together the floors come to cfull = 1.6, 4.1 and 11.0 points in the favorable,

Table 10: Full-automation ceiling Rmax at full cost for the Rule of 40 (equation (29), ψ = 0, δK = 0), in

central and conservative cases, and 4.7 points in the high-compute case.
     The ceiling. Table 10 evaluates (29), holding output prices fixed. If compute absorbs 20% of revenue at
full automation, a scenario value, the central margin term is 57.7 points. An asset-light firm’s ceiling is then
63, 73 and 88 points for market growth of 5, 15 and 30%, and Rmax reaches 100 only if the market grows
faster than about 42% a year. Favorable parameters raise the ceiling to 73, 83 and 98 and lower the threshold
to about 32%; conservative ones give 50, 60 and 75 and 55%; and in the high-compute case, which keeps
the cost of revenue near that projected for AI products in 2027, the margin term falls to 41 points, the ceiling
to 46, 56 and 71 and the threshold rises to 59%. Counting the costs of accountability alone and none of
the costs of running AI, the four thresholds would be 31, 39, 47 and 55%. The sensitivities of the central
threshold rank the parameters:
    • Compute share. Over its credible bounds of 1 to 55% of revenue, the threshold runs from 26 to 72%
      (23 to 68% with accountability costs alone). This is the dominant uncertainty.
    • AI failure. From its favorable to its conservative value, the failure term moves the threshold from 41
      to 44%; with today’s highest measured error rates and losses equal to the in-house cost of the work
      (cerr = 7.8 points), it would reach 48%.
    • Taxes and the accountability floor. Over tax rates of 15 to 21% the threshold runs from 40 to 44%,
      and at a 9% effective rate it would be 36% (32% with accountability costs alone). Over the band of
      amin it runs from 40 to 44%.
    • Capital. A capital intensity of 1.5 lowers the ceiling at 30% market growth by 45 points if the capital
      is not deductible, by 36 to 38 points if it is deducted when incurred, as domestic software development
      and equipment that qualifies for the 100% allowance now are, and by 40 to 41 points for development
      abroad (Remark 1). Owning the compute behind cc instead of buying it raises the cash ceiling only if
      the vendor’s markup exceeds 20% at 5% market growth and 60% at 15% (Remark 11).
     Three conclusions follow. First, even complete automation does not carry a mature firm past a cash-
basis Rule of 100 unless its capital needs are negligible and its market grows faster than about 42% a year,
if compute absorbs 20% of revenue; every threshold is conditional on that scenario share, and the range
over its credible bounds, 26 to 72%, is wide. The thresholds hold prices fixed while computing costs fall;
if falling costs are passed through to customers, they rise. Second, a Rule of 1000 is not a steady state. By

                                                                  43
Proposition 3(d), even with a 50-point margin intercept and 20% market growth, it requires demand pull λ h
between 6.8 and 9.3 for α between 0.45 and 0.25. The upper-quartile growth surplus of the 2023–25 top tail
(Section 7.3) corresponds to λ h of 0.45 to 0.98 over the same range of α and both bounds on growth spend,
so a Rule of 1000 needs roughly 7 to 21 times the demand pull of the best public software firms today.
Such pull is necessarily transient. Proposition 4 shows that every point of score above the saturated level is
paid for out of a finite budget of log share headroom, and Corollary 1 turns the budget into a bound on the
number of years a firm can sustain a given score: with a margin intercept of 60 points, 20% market growth
and a share cap of 50%, a firm that starts at 1% of its market can score 1,000 in at most 1 year, and one that
starts at a thousandth of a percent in at most 5. Third, where entry is free, the margin intercept is not set by
automation. With free entry and replication cost approaching zero, Proposition 8 gives R = G + (r − G)k + ρ
for the Rule of 40. With market growth of 5%, a cost of capital of 10% and k = 0.5, a firm without a scarce
input scores 7.5. Reaching 100 would require a rent ρ of 92.5 points of revenue on something rivals cannot
copy: in a competitive market, essentially all of a high score must be rent.

7.3   Calibrated ranges by horizon
Table 11 reports the maximal score from Proposition 2 for an exceptional firm under scenario assumptions
that bracket each horizon. The calibration is on an after-tax cash basis, so m0 is the after-tax margin intercept
of Remark 1.
     Anchor. The data identify neither demand pull nor the spend elasticity, and a single intercept across
firms is not usable either: an intercept that fits typical firms lies below the observed margins of many of
the best ones. We therefore invert the model firm by firm for the top tail of the organic frontier (E2), the
estimand that confronts the model: the 20 firm-years at or above the 95th percentile of its 2023–25 cash-
basis score (44 points), with market growth set at the E2 median growth of 12.0%. 7 of them had cash
margins above the 95th-percentile margin of all firms. On the after-tax cash basis m = m0 − (1 − τ)σ − kg,
so each firm’s intercept is m0 = m + (1 − τ)σ + kg, with τ = 0.18 and measured capital spending for kg,
treated as not deductible (ψ = 0); with the deductions of current law each intercept would be lower by τkg,
at most 0.3 points at the quartiles. Growth spend σ is not observed, but it lies between zero and the selling-
and-marketing share; one firm-year that does not report selling and marketing expense is treated as having
none. We split each score into a durable part R∞ = m0 + (1 − k)G and a transient growth surplus Π = R − R∞
under both bounds, and we report the same inversion for observed scores (E1), which uses the 15 firm-years
of the observed top tail not affected by acquisitions, as a comparison.
     How much of the best organic scores of 2023–25 was transient depends on how much selling and mar-
keting expense buys growth. If all of it does, the median top-tail firm-year had a margin intercept of 43
points (interquartile range 36 to 52), a durable score of 55 (47 to 63) and a growth surplus, in points, of
−1 (−17 to 16): the median organic top-tail firm-year was at its durable level, and only 45% of the anchor
firm-years scored above it. A negative surplus is inconsistent with the model at X = 1, because the surplus
is a maximum over spend that includes zero spend; it means either that the firm acts on a weight on growth
above one or that its selling-and-marketing share overstates its growth spend. If none of it buys growth, the
median intercept is 25 points, the durable score 36 and the surplus 20 (12 to 28). The median score itself is
51, so between −2 and 39% of it was transient. That the best organic scores of 2023–25 were partly transient
therefore holds only if part of selling and marketing expense does not buy growth. In observed scores (E1)
the transient share is 12 to 45% of a median score of 54, with a median surplus of 7 points at the upper
bound. The difference comes from the lower organic cut-off, not from the firm-years both anchors share:

R∗ = R∞ + Π with R∞ = m0 + (1 − k)G (Proposition 2); a firm still transforming at the horizon scores lower by the dip of the
transitions in flight (Section 3.10; the path of the dip is derived in Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026). The 2025 row inverts the
model for the 20 firm-years of the organic frontier (E2) at or above the 95th percentile of its 2023–25 cash-basis score. On the
after-tax cash basis m0 = m + (1 − τ)σ + kg, with capital treated as not deductible (ψ = 0, Remark 1), τ = 0.18 and the
selling-and-marketing share as an upper bound on σ ; Π = R − R∞ . Its entries are interquartile ranges of the firm-level values, and
its R∗ entry is the interquartile range of observed scores. Later rows are scenario bounds under our scenario assumptions (the
