all assumptions. The intercept moves from the anchor’s quartiles toward the central and favorable full-cost margin terms of
Table 10 (58 and 68), m0 = m0A + E (m0F − m0A ), with E from Proposition 12(c) at central and fast execution (Section 7); Π
scales the 2025 range, floored at zero, by µ 1/(1−α) for demand pull µ times its 2025 level and by (1 − k)1/(1−α) for capital
intensity, with α ∈ [0.25, 0.45].


it admits 5 firm-years, of which 4 spend so heavily on selling that their upper-bound surplus is negative.
Clear Secure’s 2025 score was durable under either bound, with a surplus of at most 4 points; Palantir and
Exzeo carried the largest surpluses in 2025, 24 to 49 points; and monday.com spent so heavily on selling
that, if all of it bought growth, it scored more than 21 points below its durable level, as a firm acting on a
weight well above one would (Section 8). Its upper-bound intercept of 64 to 65 points, however, exceeds
even the central full-cost margin term, a sign that the upper bound overstates its growth spend. Even the
higher, upper-bound median intercept of 43 points is 14 points below the central full-cost margin term of
Table 10 (58) and 25 points below the favorable one (68), so automation still has room to raise the durable
scores of the best firms, but by tens of points, not hundreds.
     Pace. The intercept does not follow an assumed path. It moves from the anchor toward the full-cost
margin term at the pace that Proposition 12(c) allows: m0 (t) = m0A +Et (m0F −m0A ), where Et is the expected
share of the automation program embedded by date t. The high end of each range pairs the anchor’s upper
quartile (52 points) with the favorable full-cost term (68) and fast execution; the low end pairs the lower
quartile (36) with the central term (58) and central execution, on the view that an exceptional firm executes
at least as well as a typical one. Execution has three parts.
     • Capacity. Central execution changes the work of 10% of the firm’s employees a year, the middle of a
       range of 5 to 20% that layoff rates and single restructurings bracket. Fast execution changes 20%. For
       a program of 48 processes, decision bandwidth and security review allow a faster pace in both cases,
       so change capacity binds (Proposition 11(c)).
     • Risk. An attempt works with probability 0.54 in central execution and 0.80 in fast execution. These
       are survey rates: the share of AI proofs of concept that reach production, and the success rate of trans-
       formations that meet six conditions. Leadership changes that do not keep the program and changes of
       strategy disrupt work in flight at combined rates of 0.23 and 0.07 a year. Transitions embed 2 years
       and 1 year after they start, and successes realize 85% and 100% of their savings. Reorganizations
       freeze the program about 10% and 2% of the time.


                                                                45
    • Embedding. Together these give per-attempt embedding probabilities of pemb = 0.34 and 0.74. Each
      wave attempts as much of the program as change capacity allows, so the program embeds 3.0% of
      itself a year in expectation under central execution and 14.5% under fast execution.
Under central execution, 2.5% of the program is embedded by 2028, 10% by 2031 and 23% by 2036. Fast
execution embeds 30%, 73% and all of it, completing it in 2033. A firm still transforming also carries the
cost of the transitions in flight. If they cost two years of their savings in central execution and one year in
fast execution, the cash score is lower by about 3.9 and 3.0 points while the program runs. Under central
execution, embedded gains first exceed that dip after about 9 years. The survey rates, the capacity range and
the turnover and reorganization rates are sourced or grounded by precedent; the program size, embedding
times, realized shares, the rates at which new leaders keep a program and strategies change, and the transition
costs are scenario values, so the pace, like the ranges below, is conditional on them.
    Ranges. Under our scenario assumptions, and if all selling and marketing expense buys growth, the
maximal score of an exceptional asset-light firm would be 46 to 134 in 2028, 46 to 117 in 2031 and 46 to
122 in 2036, of which 46 to 77, 46 to 84 and 46 to 98 points would be durable; a firm still transforming
would score up to 3.9 points less. The upper ends are set by fast execution, which completes the program
within the decade, so that by 2036 the durable part reaches the favorable ceiling; the lower ends are set by
central execution, which embeds less than a quarter of the program by 2036. The upper end in 2028 requires
a doubling of pull, the upper-quartile 2025 surplus and α near 0.45, and it implies growth above 100%,
a range in which the difference between continuously compounded and annual growth rates is no longer
negligible, so it should be read as an order of magnitude. A firm that must invest half a dollar of capital per
dollar of new revenue would score 41 to 83 in 2028, 42 to 85 in 2031 and 43 to 92 in 2036 if that capital
is not deductible, and 42 to 91, 43 to 89 and 44 to 96 if it is deducted when incurred, as domestic software
development and equipment now are (Remark 1). If instead no selling and marketing expense buys growth,
every intercept is lower by the 19-point difference between the two bounds at the anchor, and the ranges
would be 40 to 156 in 2028, 35 to 123 in 2031 and 31 to 121 in 2036, of which 28 to 58, 27 to 65 and 27 to
79 points would be durable: the totals are similar, but more of each score is transient. Across both bounds
the ranges are therefore roughly 40 to 156 in 2028, 35 to 123 in 2031 and 31 to 122 in 2036, of which about
28 to 77, 27 to 84 and 27 to 98 points would be durable.
    Ownership. Ownership changes the pace more than the level. A public firm that holds to a guided dip of
one point of revenue a year can attempt only about 25% of the central program at first, and its budget loosens
only as savings accumulate. By 2036 it embeds 8% of the program instead of 23%, so its intercept rises by
2 points instead of 5. A guided dip of two points gives 16%. A sponsor’s faster decisions change nothing in
these cases, because change capacity, not decision bandwidth, binds; they matter only for programs whose
decisions are slow relative to the number of employees affected. Under slow execution (5% of employees a
year, success 0.30, half the savings realized, frequent disruption), less than 1% of the program is embedded
by 2036. The companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026) derives the dip budgets and decision
speeds of different owners. These ranges are scenario bounds under our scenario assumptions, not forecasts.
They are lower than company-reported figures would suggest for two reasons: they are on an annual cash
basis after SBC, and the fastest-growing AI firms of 2025 and 2026 are private and therefore outside the
anchor. Proposition 4 bounds how long any surplus can last.




                                                      46
7.4   Levers
The model turns the question “what does it take to approach the limit?” into conditions on parameters, each
of which can be checked against a firm’s own data. Six levers move the limit:
   1. A scarce, non-replicable input that supports a rent ρ: proprietary data with a feedback loop, distribu-
      tion, a license, or network effects. Where entry is free, without it the score falls toward G + (r − G)k.
      A data advantage is durable only if the data are renewed or the market tips: accumulating data has
      increasing returns early but diminishing returns in the long run (Farboodi and Veldkamp, 2021), while
      data-driven markets can tip to a single firm (Prüfer and Schottmüller, 2021).
   2. Demand pull λ h well above that of the typical firm, which shows up as growth that does not require
      rising selling-and-marketing intensity.
   3. Capital that earns its keep: either k near zero, or capital whose operating return exceeds the condition
      in Corollary 2, so that replacing labor with computing capacity raises rather than lowers the cash-basis
      score.
   4. Pricing that captures value as inference costs fall and as customers’ headcount, and with it their seat
      counts, shrinks, so that falling unit costs raise γ rather than being passed through to customers.
   5. A small accountability floor amin and small full-cost floors. Certification of financial statements, audit,
      model-risk validation and liability insurance remain human-accountable costs; Proposition 10 shows
      that part of the floor is an expected loss set by the diversity of the control design rather than by
      headcount. The costs of maintaining, securing and supervising AI behave in the same way.
   6. High market growth G, meaning a position where spending on labor is converting into spending on
      software.
A further factor moves the speed of approach rather than the limit: the capacity to execute. Change capacity,
decision bandwidth and security review bound how many transitions a firm can run, and failures, rollbacks,
leadership changes and reorganizations waste that capacity (Propositions 11 and 12). Corollary 3 orders
the levers a firm controls by stage: while it has share to gain, the productivity of its growth engine (lever
2) and its retention, the firm-level counterpart of market growth (lever 6), move its score most; once it is
near saturation, overhead and the accountability floor do (lever 5); and capital-heavy automation is cheapest
for slow growers (lever 3). No lever is sufficient on its own. Because any surplus above the durable score
is transient, a sustained cash-basis Rule of 100 requires the levers jointly to push the durable score R∞ =
m0 + (1 − k)G above 100.
     The empirical evidence suggests which levers bind today. Among the best performers a point of growth
has come with well under a point less margin, and the fall in the top of the score distribution between 2016–
19 and 2023–25 coincided with a fall in the number of fast growers, while the margins attainable at a given
growth rate showed no detectable change. This points to the supply of growth opportunity (levers 2 and 6),
rather than willingness to trade margin for growth, as the constraint that binds, although the intervals are
wide. The cash-basis size gradient of observed scores disappears on the organic frontier, so the data say
little about saturation (lever 2, through h), and since 2023 the upper tail of the cash-basis score has been 11
points lower for capital-intensive firms on the organic frontier (lever 3).




                                                       47
8     Discussion
8.1   Two weights on growth
The model separates two weights on growth that practice treats as one. The screening weight of Lemma 1
compares a persistent change in growth with a persistent change in margin; it is a duration, about 1.8 to
2.8 for a firm with a ten-year runway and partly transitory margins, close to the weights of two to three
that Deeter and Bondy (2024) recommend for free-cash-flow margins “if growth is efficient”. The decision
weight of Proposition 6 is the marginal value of a dollar of revenue, the marginal q of revenue in the sense of
Hayashi (1982); the worked example gives 5 to 15, and the ratio of growth spend to excess growth implies
roughly 7 to 13 (Section 5). Because cash flows of longer duration earn lower expected returns (Weber,
2018; Gormsen and Lazarus, 2023), a constant discount rate, if anything, understates the decision weight.
The screening weight tells an investor how to compare firms; the decision weight tells a manager whether a
dollar of growth spend creates value.
    The difference has a practical consequence. A firm that acts on its decision weight X spends σ ∗ =
α(X − k)∆∗ (Proposition 2), so its Rule-of-40 score exceeds the saturated level m0 + (1 − k)G by ∆∗ (1 − k −
α(X − k)), which is negative whenever α(X − k) > 1 − k. At the median spend ratio of 3.3, an asset-light
firm growing 10 points faster than its market scores about 23 points below the score it would earn with no
growth spend, even though the spending creates value, and among the best organic firm-years of 2023–25 a
quarter score at least 17 points below their durable level if all of their selling and marketing expense buys
growth (Section 7.3). A fixed threshold on the Rule of 40, whether 40, 50 or 60, therefore marks down firms
whose growth spending earns more than it costs, and is better used to screen firms than to set their spending.
Proposition 13 gives the same point in value terms: the Rule of X at the firm’s own weight is a criterion of
economic profit plus growth, while the cash Rule of 40 weights growth at 1 − k whatever it earns.

8.2   Execution sets the pace
The static frontier says where a firm can be once a transformation is complete; the attainable frontier says
how fast it gets there. In our calibration the binding constraint is the capacity to change the work of em-
ployees, not decision bandwidth or security review, and failures, rollbacks, leadership changes and reorga-
nizations act like a cut in that capacity (Proposition 12). The difference between central and fast execution,
about 23% of the program embedded by 2036 against all of it by 2033, lies in the success rate of transitions
and the stability of their sponsorship, not in technology. Two implications follow. A firm in the middle of
a program scores below its eventual durable score by the dip of the transitions in flight, about 3.0 to 3.9
points in our scenarios, so a screen that reads the dip as weakness penalizes firms that are executing. And
ownership changes the pace more than the level: a public firm that holds to a guided dip of one point of
revenue embeds 8% of the central program by 2036 instead of 23%. These statements are conditional on
scenario values for program size, embedding times and the rates at which new leaders keep programs, which
no source we found measures.

8.3   Measurement at the limit
Near the top of the distribution, accounting choices move the score by more than operating performance
does. Four mechanisms matter. First, adjusted margins exclude SBC: Palantir’s second-quarter 2026 score
was 155 on its adjusted operating margin of 62% but 140 on its GAAP operating margin of 47% (Palantir
Technologies Inc., 2026). Its annual GAAP score for fiscal 2025 was 88 (Section 5), but that comparison

                                                      48
also reflects an acceleration of growth, not measurement alone. Second, computing capacity that a firm owns
is capitalized. It enters operating income only through depreciation over the useful life management assigns,
it never enters EBITDA, and it appears in full only in free cash flow. Lengthening the assigned useful life of
servers raises operating margins without changing cash flow, and capitalizing internally developed software
shifts cost between periods in the same way; for a firm whose capitalized spending grows with revenue,
amortization charges today’s income with past, smaller vintages, so the GAAP score exceeds the cash score
permanently, by more the faster the firm grows (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026). Third, recording
revenue gross rather than net raises reported revenue relative to profit, and it raises reported growth while
the mix of gross-recorded revenue is rising. Fourth, run-rate and annualized quarterly revenue run ahead of
annual revenue in a growth spurt. Each of these can push a reported score above the ceiling in Corollary 4
without any change in economics. Growth that is bought also needs care: acquired revenue enters a reported
score without the capital paid for it, and interest on acquisition debt is not in the operating margin; Section 6
shows how to charge both. For the serial acquirer of that section, the 2025 GAAP score of 115.9 falls to
105.0 after interest and to 84.4 to 92.8 when acquisitions are charged as an annuity over a scenario life, on
figures from its F-1 filed on 8 June 2026, which are unchanged in the final prospectus; and in the second
quarter of 2026 its reported score of about 160 compares with an organic score of about 37 on the company’s
own measure of organic growth. Such a score is evidence of access to targets and capital, not of a frontier
above the ceiling, and a firm whose base shrinks must keep buying to stand still. This is why observed
scores (E1) remain the estimand for statements about reported scores, while the organic frontier (E2), which
removes acquired growth, is the estimand for statements about the frontier. We suggest computing the score
as this paper does, on annual free cash flow after SBC and after capital expenditure including capitalized
software, and, where lease-financed capacity is material, also after additions to finance-lease assets.

8.4   What to track if headcount falls
The Rule of 40 was built for firms whose main costs were people, so its implicit trade-off is between selling
and marketing expense and growth. When automation turns labor into capital, capital becomes the scarce
input, and the score has no capital term. Two firms can both score 100 while earning very different returns
on the capital that produced the score. Proposition 13 says what to track instead: economic profit, defined
as after-tax operating profit minus the cost of capital on invested capital including leases and purchase
commitments, plus growth at the net weight X − k. Two further supplements follow from the model: gross
profit per dollar of computing cost, because the compute share is the largest uncertainty in the ceiling; and the
growth-weighted score with a weight that reflects the firm’s own marginal value of revenue (Proposition 6)
rather than a fixed screening weight.

8.5   The accountability floor
Full automation does not remove the cost of being accountable. Under Section 404 of the Sarbanes–Oxley
Act, management must assess the company’s internal control over financial reporting every year, and the
auditor of an accelerated filer must attest to that assessment in an integrated audit performed under PCAOB
Auditing Standard 2201 (United States Congress, 2026b; Public Company Accounting Oversight Board,
2026). Emerging growth companies are exempt from the auditor’s attestation, and a newly public com-
pany’s first annual report need not contain management’s assessment (United States Congress, 2026b; U.S.
Securities and Exchange Commission, 2026a), so the floor of a young public firm is lower than that of a
seasoned one. Sections 302 and 906 make the principal executive and financial officers personally responsi-


                                                       49
ble for certifying the periodic reports (United States Congress, 2026a). Regulators, insurers and courts look
to identifiable people and entities. The recurring costs of this system (control testing, the integrated audit,
directors’ and officers’ insurance and the legal and compliance staff behind them) form the floor amin in
Corollary 4. Much of the floor is fixed in dollars, so as a share of revenue it falls with scale. In the core sam-
ple the median general and administrative expense, which contains most of it, is 20% of revenue for firms
with less than $1 billion of revenue in 2023–25, 12% between $1 and $10 billion and 7% above $10 billion;
Microsoft and Oracle report 2.4 to 3.6%. We therefore set amin to 1.5%, 3% and 4.6% of revenue in the
favorable, central and conservative cases of Table 10. The central value equals the tenth-percentile general
and administrative share of firms above $10 billion, so it assumes that none of that expense is automated.
    Automated controls can lower the cost of testing, but the floor need not fall with automation and may rise
with it. Proposition 10 gives the floor as a minimum over control designs of the cost of accountability, control
layers and IT general controls plus the expected loss from misstatements the design misses. When control
layers share one AI model, they share its failures, so the common-mode part of that loss does not vanish
however many layers are added, and it does not fall with scale. In the illustrative example of Remark 10,
an all-AI design costs less than an all-human one only while misstatement opportunities and the model’s
common-mode failure rate are low; with twice either, it costs more. Each AI application that enters the
scope of internal control also adds its own IT general controls. The size of the floor therefore depends on the
potential damage of a failure and on the diversity of the control design more than on headcount, and firms
that can bear accountability credibly and cheaply hold a scarce input in the sense of Proposition 8.

8.6   Limitations
The evidence covers firms that file XBRL financial statements with the SEC under US GAAP, mostly U.S.
firms. The fastest-growing AI firms of 2025 and 2026 are private and absent, so the sample likely un-
derstates the current frontier at high growth; this is one reason the 2028 to 2036 ranges come from the
calibration rather than from the sample. Industry membership uses each registrant’s current SIC code.
The cross-sectional frontier records the points firms chose, so it does not identify any firm’s own trade-off
(Proposition 7), and the data do not identify the spend elasticity α; the weight on growth that we infer from
spending treats all selling and marketing expense as growth spend. Observed scores and the organic frontier
answer different questions, and we report the model-facing findings for both. The ceiling rests on a compute
share with no measured analog and on scenario values for loss severities and the build stock; the pace rests
on scenario values for program size, embedding times and the persistence of sponsorship; and both hold
output prices fixed. The horizon ranges are conditional on the stated parameter assumptions and should be
read as scenario bounds, not forecasts.


9     Conclusion
The Rule of 40 is a line, not a frontier. Read through a model of the attainable set, it is a price at which
growth is traded for margin, and the maximal score is reached where a line at that price touches the frontier.
Each firm’s frontier is concave, with an intercept set by the margin it earns at market growth and a slope
set by the diminishing returns to buying growth. In value terms the Rule of X is a rule of economic profit,
which carries the frontier beyond software. The frontier that can be estimated across firms is drawn through
the points firms chose. In SEC-registered software firms from 2010 to 2025 it falls by about half a point of
margin per point of growth on the organic frontier, which removes acquired growth, and by a third to a half


                                                       50
of a point in observed scores. Between 2016–19 and 2023–25 the top of the distribution of reported scores
fell by 12 to 15 points as fast growers became scarcer, with no detectable change in the margins attainable
at a given growth rate, and the decline holds on the organic frontier. Two gradients in observed scores, a
cash-basis score that falls with firm size and a cash-basis slope that steepened after 2020, disappear once
acquired growth is removed; the cash-basis penalty on capital intensity survives.
     The answer to “Rule of 100 or Rule of 1000?” is neither, as a steady state. Automation raises the margin
intercept by removing operating cost, but it adds computing cost and the recurring costs of maintaining,
securing and supervising AI, and, for firms that own capacity, capital that weighs on cash-basis scores.
Saturation removes the growth surplus as markets fill, and a finite budget of share headroom limits how
long any surplus lasts. Where entry is free, competition removes the margin that automation creates unless
the firm holds a scarce input. A sustained cash-basis score above 100 therefore requires near-complete
automation, negligible capital needs and a market growing at a rate few markets sustain: about 42% a year if
compute absorbs 20% of revenue, and between 26 and 72% over the credible range of that share. Scores of
several hundred are possible only briefly, in new markets with exceptional demand pull. Capacity to execute
risky transitions limits how fast a firm gets to its ceiling: under our scenario assumptions an exceptional firm
embeds about 23% of its automation program by 2036 under central execution and all of it by 2033 under
fast execution, and its maximal score would be roughly 40 to 156 in 2028, 35 to 123 in 2031 and 31 to 122
in 2036, of which about 28 to 77, 27 to 84 and 27 to 98 points would be durable.
     For practitioners the implications are concrete. Compute the score on annual free cash flow after stock-
based compensation and capital expenditure, and charge acquired growth with what it cost. Use the Rule
of 40 to screen firms, not to set growth spending: a firm that values a dollar of revenue at its marginal
value should accept a lower Rule-of-40 score while its growth spending earns more than it costs. Read
cross-sectional comparisons as a record of choices by firms with different opportunities, not as the trade-off
any one firm faces. Supplement the score with economic profit (Proposition 13) and gross profit per dollar
of computing cost as headcount stops being the main cost. And evaluate AI strategies by the parameters
they move: the margin intercept, demand pull, capital intensity, pricing power, the accountability floor, the
full-cost floors of maintaining, securing and supervising AI, and the capacity to execute the transitions that
get the firm there. For a fast grower the first target is the productivity of the growth engine and retention; for
a mature firm it is overhead; savings in cost of revenue are worth only what pricing power lets the firm keep;
owned capacity pays only where growth is slow enough for the cost it removes to exceed growth times the
capital it adds; and control designs that rely on one model leave an accountability cost that more layers do
not remove. Where entry is free, the limit of the rule is set by what stays scarce, not by what is automated.

A      Additional Theoretical Results
This appendix collects results referred to in Section 3. Table 12 lists the notation; in particular ∆∗ (ℓ, X) and σ ∗ (ℓ, X)
denote (5) and (6) with λ h replaced by ℓ and X − k by (X − k)+ . Proofs are in Appendix B.
Remark 4 (Four simplifying assumptions).      (a) (Fixed costs.) If part of the cost floor is fixed in dollars, a = av +
     Ξ/S with Ξ > 0, then m0 = γ − av − Ξ/S rises with revenue. Propositions 1, 2 and 5 hold at each date with this
     m0 , but R∞ = γ − av − Ξ/(sM) + (X − k)G rises with revenue at a given share, and R∗X (s) rises with s at small
     shares, because at fixed M its derivative is Ξ/(s2 M) − (X − k)∆∗ /(s̄ − s). Tests of Proposition 3 that sort firms
     by revenue therefore confound share with absolute size. Proposition 4 holds with R∞ evaluated at the largest
     revenue reached on the path. A constant fixed cost Ξ does not affect the optimal policy, so Proposition 6 holds
     with m0 = γ − av and firm value M v(S/M) − Ξ/r (for r > 0): the growth weight is still v′ (s).
  (b) (Firm-specific zero-spend growth.) In subscription software, the growth a firm gets without acquisition spend is
      set largely by its net revenue retention, so it varies across firms. With g = Gi + ℓσ α , Propositions 1–3 and 5 hold
      at each date with G replaced by Gi (so R∞ = m0 + (X − k)Gi ). Share     now drifts at rate Gi − G without spend,
      and Proposition 4(a) holds with (X − k) ln(s(T )/s0 ) + (G − Gi )T on the right side. A firm whose retention
      lags its market (Gi < G) loses share without spend and can buy it back repeatedly, so its budget grows with
      time. Across firms, Proposition 7(i) can fail (Remark 7).
    (c) (Negative capital intensity and depreciation.) Prepaid subscriptions create deferred revenue, which finances
        growth: with deferred revenue equal to a fraction kdef of annual revenue, the cash capital intensity is k =
        kfix − kdef and can be negative. The closed forms of Propositions 2, 3 and 5 and the score budget hold for k < 0,
        but the frontier then rises at first (its slope at G is −k > 0): points with g − G < ∆0 := [−k/(β κ)]1/(β −1) are
        dominated, Proposition 1(c) holds only for g ≥ G + ∆0 , and Proposition 7(i) can fail; Proposition 6(d) uses
        k ≥ 0. If growth capital depreciates at rate δ , so that investment is k(g + δ )S, the results of Sections 3.2–3.5
        and Proposition 6 hold with m0 replaced by m0 − δ k; in particular ∂ R∗X /∂ k = −(g∗ + δ ), and Corollary 2 holds
        with g∗ replaced by g∗ + δ . Compute fleets depreciate at about 20% to 33% a year; with δ = 0.25, raising k by
        0.10 at 10% growth requires opex savings of about 3.5 points rather than 1.
  (d) (Network effects.) A decreasing headroom h rules out network effects, which would raise the productivity of
      spend as share grows. Monotonicity of h is used only in Proposition 3(a) and (c), in the attainability statement
      of Proposition 3(d), in the bound ℓ(s) ≤ λ of Proposition 6(d), and, through the logistic form, in Proposition 15.
      Propositions 1, 2, 5 and 7, and the score budget (Proposition 4 and Corollary 1), do not use it: the budget needs
      only share accounting and the cap s ≤ s̄.
Lemma 2 (Timing of growth capital).    (a) Equation (2) is exact in continuous time. In discrete time it holds exactly
    when capital is installed one period ahead, It = k(St+1 − St ), with g the forward growth rate.
  (b) If capital is installed contemporaneously, It = k(St − St−1 ) with g = St /St−1 − 1 > −1, then m = mop − kg/(1 +
      g), and the frontier becomes m̃(g) = m0 − kg/(1 + g) − κ(g − G)β . The two conventions differ by kg2 /(1 + g) ≥
      0, which is second order in g; at k = 0.5 and g = 0.3 the gap is about 0.035.
    (c) If α < 1/2 and k > 0, then m̃ is strictly convex on an interval (G, G + ε), and the feasible set under convention
        (b) is not convex, so the Rule-of-X problem can have several local maxima. For example, take α = 0.3, k = 1.25,
        κ = 0.5, G = 0.1, m0 = 0.5 and X = 1. Then the corner g = G is a local maximum of Xg + m̃(g) (score 0.486),
        and there is a second, interior local maximum at g ≈ 0.74 (score ≈ 0.595). Under (2) the same parameters
        give the unique optimum g = G, because X < k.
    We adopt (2) because it is exact in continuous time, matches capacity that must be in place before revenue arrives,
and keeps the feasible set convex for every α ∈ (0, 1).
Remark 5 (Binding financing cap). If σ ∗ > σ̄ , the optimum is at the cap: σ = σ̄ , g = G + ℓσ̄ α and R∗X = m0 + (X −
k)G + (X − k)ℓσ̄ α − σ̄ , because σ 7→ (X − k)ℓσ α − σ is strictly concave and increasing below σ ∗ . Where the cap
binds strictly, the envelope formulas of Proposition 5 continue to hold with (σ ∗ , ∆∗ ) replaced by (σ̄ , ℓσ̄ α ).
Remark 6 (The Rule of 40 is a pass mark, not a choice rule). Along the frontier, g 7→ g + m∗ (g) is strictly concave. The
frontier points that pass the Rule of 40 therefore form an interval, possibly empty, and if the interval is nonempty it
contains the Rule-of-1 optimum. The rule accepts a range of strategies, and a point that passes may still be dominated.
The Rule of X, by contrast, selects a single point: the tangency.

Proposition 17 (Non-identification and within-firm co-movement). Under Assumption 5:
  (iv) (Non-identification.) Let k be common, X > k, and ξ , µ ∈ R. Let there be one firm for each ℓ ∈ [ℓlo , ℓhi ],
       0 < ℓlo < ℓhi , with σ ∗ (ℓhi , X) ≤ σ̄ and m0 (ℓ) = µ + [ξ + k + α(X − k)]∆∗ (ℓ, X). Then the chosen points fill
       the segment mc = µ − kG + ξ (gc − G), gc ∈ [glo , ghi ], of slope ξ , and Ψ has slope min{ξ , 0} on (glo , ghi ). Yet
       at its chosen point every firm’s frontier has slope −X and curvature −(1 − α)(X − k)/(α∆∗i ). Moreover, for
       every α ′ ∈ (0, 1) and every X ′ > k with σ̄ ≥ (X ′ − k)(ghi − G), the same segment is generated by firms that have
       spend elasticity α ′ , maximize RX ′ , and have suitably chosen opportunities and intercepts; their frontiers then
       have slope −X ′ and curvature −(1 − α ′ )(X ′ − k)/(α ′ ∆∗i ) at their chosen points. The cross-section therefore
       identifies neither the slope nor the curvature of any firm’s frontier.
  (v) (Within-firm co-movement.) Fix a firm with X > k and slack caps. In each period it observes (m0t , ℓt ) and sets
      σt = σ ∗ (ℓt , X). It commits the cost block (a f + σt )Ste in dollars against planned revenue Ste = St−1 (1 + gte ),
      where gte = G + ∆∗ (ℓt , X) and a f ∈ [0, a] is the part of the cost floor fixed in advance. Realized growth is
      gt = gte + νt , where νt is a growth surprise (for example, a shock to λ h that arrives after spend is committed).
      The realized margin is then mt = m0t + a f − (a f + σt )(1 + gte )/(1 + gt ) − kgt . Let (m0t , ℓt , νt ) = (m̄0 , ℓ̄, 0) + εzt ,
      where the zt are identically distributed and bounded, with mean zero, and z3t is uncorrelated with (z1t , z2t ).
      Then, as ε → 0, the population slope of mt on gt converges to

                                    Cℓ − k + α(X − k) Vℓ + L Vν                    a f + σ ∗ (ℓ̄, X)
                                                      
                              W =                                    ,     L :=                      − k,                        (33)
                                               Vℓ +Vν                                   1 + ḡe

       where ḡe = G + ∆∗ (ℓ̄, X), Vℓ = ∆∗′ (ℓ̄)2 Var(z2 ), Vν = Var(z3 ), Cℓ = ∆∗′ (ℓ̄) Cov(z1 , z2 ), ∆∗′ is the derivative of
       ∆∗ (·, X), and Vℓ +Vν > 0 is assumed. With anticipated opportunity shocks alone, W = −[k + α(X − k)] < 0. In
       general, W > 0 if and only if Cℓ + L Vν > [k + α(X − k)]Vℓ .

    For example, with X = 7, α = 0.3, k = 0, G = 0.1, planned excess growth ∆∗ = 0.1 and a f = 0.3, the expansion-
path slope is −2.1 and L = (0.3 + 0.21)/1.2 = 0.425. With Cℓ = 0, a within-firm slope of zero requires growth
surprises to account for about 83% of within-firm growth variance (2.1/2.525).
Remark 7 (Segment-specific market growth). Proposition 7(i) uses the common market growth rate G: every firm can
grow at G at its highest margin, so the possibility envelope inherits the downward slope of firm frontiers. If market
growth differs across segments, Φ need not be monotone. For example, let firm 1 have (G1 , m01 , k1 ) = (0, 0.2, 0) with
ℓ1 σ̄ α ≥ 0.1, and let firm 2 have (G2 , m02 , k2 ) = (0.3, 0.5, 0). Then Φ(0.1) = m∗1 (0.1) < 0.2 < 0.5 = m∗2 (0.3) = Φ(0.3),
because firm 2 cannot grow more slowly than its market without negative spend.
Remark 8 (Mode of competition). The conclusion L → 0 in Proposition 8 requires post-entry competition that does not
punish a second entrant too severely. Under homogeneous Bertrand competition with any sunk cost F > 0, a second
entrant would earn zero and lose F. A single firm therefore enters (provided monopoly profit covers the entry cost)
and charges the monopoly price for every F > 0, and incumbency itself becomes the scarce input. Under contestability
(a fixed flow cost F̂ that is not sunk), price equals average cost, so the markup is F̂/(Px) and vanishes as F → 0, as
under Cournot. A firm that rents all of its capital has k = 0 in (24), so its competitive-limit score is XG.

Lemma 3 (Price channels).    (a) Let u be the non-fee unit cost (human service plus compute, u = uh + pc ι), let p(u)
    be the equilibrium output price, assumed differentiable, and let ϕ := d ln p/d ln u be the pass-through elasticity.
    Then γ = 1 − f − u/p and dγ/du = −(1 − ϕ)/p, so a cost reduction raises the gross margin if and only if ϕ < 1.
                                                                                            n u
    With f = 0 and full unit cost ĉ = u + rku : under Cournot with linear demand, ϕ = n+1     p < 1; under monopoly


                                                                 59
       with constant-elasticity demand and under perfect competition, p is proportional to ĉ, so ϕ = u/ĉ ≤ 1, with
       equality if and only if b = 0.
  (b) Suppose demand at constant prices grows at GQ with constant price elasticity ηD > 0, and the output price
      falls at rate δ p . Then nominal market growth is G = GQ − (1 − ηD )δ p . Price deflation therefore lowers G when
      demand is inelastic (ηD < 1) and raises it when demand is elastic (ηD > 1).
Proposition 18 (AI shifts of the frontier). Let the parameters (m0 , k, λ , G) change from ζ0 to ζ1 , with s, α and σ̄ fixed.
  (a) (Up and out.) Suppose that λ and G weakly rise, k weakly falls, and m0 − kG weakly rises. Then every point
      feasible at ζ0 is weakly dominated by a point feasible at ζ1 , and R∗X weakly rises for every X ≥ 0. The reverse
      changes shift the frontier down and in and weakly lower R∗X for every X ≥ 0.
  (b) (Rotation.) If only m0 and k change, by dm0 > 0 and dk > 0, the new frontier lies above the old one for
      g < dm0 /dk and below it for g > dm0 /dk.
Lemma 4 (Components of the full-cost floors). All quantities are annual and per dollar of revenue.
  (a) (Maintenance.) If automated builds cost cbld , need upkeep at the rate δM a year and a revalidation costing
      ρrv cbld at each model upgrade, every Tup years, then cmnt = (δM + ρrv /Tup ) cbld ; with discounting at r, 1/Tup
      becomes r/(erTup − 1).
  (b) (Security.) Let the expected incident loss without security spending be cinc , and let spending csp multiply every
                                                                                                        sp
      incident hazard by exp(−csp /ceff ), with ceff > 0. The risk cost crisk := mincsp ≥0 {csp + e−c /ceff cinc } equals cinc
      if cinc ≤ ceff and ceff [1 + ln(cinc /ceff )] otherwise. Optimal spending is ceff ln(cinc /ceff )+ ≤ cinc /e, and when
                                                                                                           sp
      it is positive it leaves the residual loss ceff , whatever the exposure. If today’s spending c0 > 0 is optimal,
      so that Lsec := ln(c0 /ceff ) > 0, and full automation multiplies the expected loss by ξexp ≥ 1, then crisk =
                            inc
         sp
      (c0 /Lsec )(1 + Lsec + ln ξexp ).
  (c) (Errors and hallucination.) Let automated work whose in-house cost is WA carry, per unit, an expected ε o
      ordinary errors and ε h confident fabrications, which review catches with probabilities d o and d h , and let each
      undetected error cost Lerr times the unit’s in-house cost. Whatever units are reviewed, the expected loss is
      at least WA ū Lerr , where ū := ε o (1 − d o ) + ε h (1 − d h ) is the review-proof error rate, and a requirement that
      undetected errors not exceed ε̄ per unit can be met if and only if ū ≤ ε̄.
  (d) (Misbehavior.) Let each unit trigger a consequential misbehavior event with probability ε m , stopped before
      harm with probability d m , and otherwise costing LmisY times the unit’s in-house cost, where Y ≥ 1 is Pareto
      with tail index pY > 0, P(Y > y) = y−pY , capped at Ȳ > 1 by least privilege, independently. The expected loss
      is WA ε m (1 − d m )Lmis E min{Y, Ȳ }, with E min{Y, Ȳ } = 1 + (1 − Ȳ 1−pY )/(pY − 1) for pY ̸= 1 and 1 + ln Ȳ for
      pY = 1. Without the cap the expectation is pY /(pY − 1) for pY > 1 and infinite for pY ≤ 1.
  (e) (Common-mode failure.) Let the automated processes lose Wcm a year while all of them are disrupted, spread
      over model providers with shares wm , and let Hcm := ∑m w2m . Common-mode events (an outage, a regression
      after a model update, a shared vulnerability) arrive at each provider independently at rate νcm and disrupt every
      process on it for the containment time Tcon , and an event that disrupts the loss rate y costs Tcon [y + y2 /(2Yfb )],
      where Yfb measures fallback capacity. The expected annual cost is νcm Tcon [Wcm + HcmWcm      2 /(2Y )], which is
                                                                                                            fb
      largest with a single provider (Hcm = 1).
Remark 9 (The full-cost floors in numbers). Table 13 evaluates Lemma 4 for the cases of Table 10. Values come from
the parameter files of the replication package and from the assumptions register, whose grounding classes are shown:
(a) sourced, (b) grounded by precedent, (c) calibrated from our own data, (d) a scenario value. Two rules set the
three cases. Factors in classes (a) to (c) take the low end, the central value and the high end of their ranges. Scenario
composites take a central value from the stated structure, halved and doubled. Fixed costs are evaluated at $1 billion
of revenue and fall as a share of revenue with scale. Two choices need comment. The conservative error rates are held
at today’s central values, because an activity at today’s worst measured rates fails the test of Lemma 4(c) and would
not be automated. The risk cost uses Lemma 4(b). In the conservative case, Lsec = 1, today’s spending is at the bound
of 1/e of the expected loss that Gordon and Loeb (2002) derive for two classes of breach-probability functions. The
companion paper (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026) sources each rate and derives the floors activity by activity.
Remark 10 (The accountability floor in numbers). The example follows the companion paper (Bangalore Sreeni-
vasa Murthy, 2026); its values are illustrative. A firm with $1 billion of revenue faces Nmm = 24 opportunities a year


                                                             60
for a misstatement costing Lmm = $10 million. Human control layers fail with probability 0.05 and cost $500,000
a year each, with a common-mode probability of 0.002; AI layers on one provider fail with probability 0.02 and
cost $50,000, with a common-mode probability of 0.005; and the AI application’s IT general controls and revalida-
tion cost $350,000 a year. By Proposition 10, the best all-human design (three layers) costs $2.01 million a year,
0.20% of revenue; the best all-AI design (three layers) $1.70 million, of which $1.20 million is the common-mode
loss Nmm Lmm pcm that no further AI layer removes; and one human layer over two AI layers $1.02 million, 0.10%.
With 48 opportunities, or an AI common-mode probability of 0.01, the best design costs about 0.11% and the all-AI
design costs more than the all-human one. Direct audit and Section 404 costs add about 0.14% at this size, for a floor
of about 0.24%. An IT-general-controls line like that of an application performing invoice matching and exceptions
(access over its identities and tool permissions, change management, a $100,000 retest at each model upgrade every
1.22 years, operations and four interfaces into in-scope systems) costs about $347,000 a year, 0.035% of revenue, and
a manual fallback for the close about $161,000 more, so the floor reaches 0.5% at about 9 in-scope AI applications, or
6 with fallbacks. code/verify_theory.py checks these values.

Remark 11 (Owning the compute). Let the compute behind cc cost TCO per dollar of revenue a year at cost, of
which servers are the share ssrv , with life Lsrv , and let a vendor charge (1 + υc )TCO. Owning it requires capital
kc = ssrv Lsrv TCO, replaced at the rate 1/Lsrv , and leaves the running cost (1 − ssrv )TCO in cc . By Corollary 4 with
ψ = 0, owning changes the ceiling of the Rule of 40 by (1 − τ)υc TCO − ssrv Lsrv TCO G, so it raises the cash ceiling
if and only if (1 − τ)υc > ssrv Lsrv G. With servers 60% of the cost of compute (Michael and Cottier, 2026) and a
5.5-year book life, ssrv Lsrv = 3.3, and at τ = 0.18 the markup must exceed 20% at 5% market growth and 60% at 15%.
Economic obsolescence faster than the book life raises the bar, and a test of value rather than of the steady-state score
charges the capital at r + δK in place of the growth rate (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026).

Lemma 5 (Expected frontier and the value of flexibility). Under Definition 4(b), with m∗ (g; ϑ ) := −∞ for g < G(ϑ ),
for every plan and date:
    (a) m̄t is concave; if every δ ϑ p changes only m0 and k, then m̄t (g) = m∗ (g; Eϑt ), the frontier at the expected
        parameters.
    (b) E R∗X (ϑt ) ≥ supg [Xg + m̄t (g)]. A firm that must fix its growth rate before the outcomes are known scores at
        most supg [Xg + m̄t (g)] in expectation, and the difference is the value of choosing growth after the outcomes are
        known.


B      Proofs
Throughout, ℓ = λ h(s) > 0, β = 1/α > 1, κ = ℓ−β , y = X − k, y+ = max{y, 0} and Cα = (1 − α)α α/(1−α) . We use
two elementary facts repeatedly.
 (F1) For c > 0 and β > 1, the function ∆ 7→ c∆β is strictly convex on [0, ∞).
 (F2) For y > 0, ℓ > 0 and α ∈ (0, 1), the function P(σ ) = yℓσ α − σ on [0, ∞) is strictly concave and has the
      unique maximizer σ ∗ = (αℓy)1/(1−α) , with P(σ ∗ ) = (1 − α)yℓ(σ ∗ )α = Cα (ℓy)1/(1−α) . Indeed, P ′′ (σ ) =
      α(α − 1)yℓσ α−2 < 0 for σ > 0, P ′ (σ ) = αyℓσ α−1 − 1 is positive near 0 and tends to −1, and P ′ (σ ∗ ) = 0
      gives σ ∗ . At σ ∗ , σ ∗ = αyℓ(σ ∗ )α , so P(σ ∗ ) = (1 − α)yℓ(σ ∗ )α . Finally, ℓ(σ ∗ )α = ℓ1/(1−α) (αy)α/(1−α) , which
      gives the last expression. For y ≤ 0, P(σ ) ≤ −σ , so the maximum is 0, attained only at σ = 0.

Proof of Lemma 2
(a) In continuous time K = kS gives K̇ = kṠ = kgS, so investment per dollar of revenue is kg. In discrete time with
It = k(St+1 − St ), It /St = k(St+1 /St − 1) = kg with g the forward growth rate.
                                                                              
     (b) With It = k(St − St−1 ), It /St = k(1 − St−1 /St ) = k 1 − 1/(1 + g) = kg/(1 + g). Substituting the frontier
spend σ = κ(g − G)β (Proposition 1(a), whose proof does not depend on the capital term) gives m̃. The difference is
kg − kg/(1 + g) = kg2 /(1 + g) ≥ 0 for g > −1; at k = 0.5 and g = 0.3 it is 0.045/1.3 ≈ 0.035.
     (c) Differentiating twice, m̃′′ (g) = 2k/(1 + g)3 − β (β − 1)κ(g − G)β −2 for g > G. If α < 1/2, then β > 2 and
the second term tends to 0 as g ↓ G, while the first tends to 2k/(1 + G)3 > 0. By continuity, m̃′′ > 0 on some interval
(G, G + ε), so m̃ is strictly convex there. The feasible set {(g, m) : g ≥ G, m ≤ m̃(g)} is then not convex: for G <

g1 < g2 < G + ε, the midpoint of (g1 , m̃(g1 )) and (g2 , m̃(g2 )) lies strictly above m̃ at the midpoint growth rate. For
the example, let f (g) = g + 0.5 − 1.25g/(1 + g) − 0.5(g − 0.1)10/3 . Then f ′ (G) = 1 − 1.25/1.12 < 0, so the corner
g = G is a local maximum with f (G) = 0.1 + 0.5 − 0.125/1.1 ≈ 0.486. Direct evaluation gives f ′ (0.5) ≈ 0.248 > 0,
 f ′ (0.7) ≈ 0.061 > 0 and f ′ (0.8) ≈ −0.111 < 0. So f first falls, then rises, then falls, and it has an interior local
maximum in (0.7, 0.8), at g ≈ 0.739 with f ≈ 0.595; verify_theory.py (check 2d) locates it on a grid of step 10−5
and confirms that it is the only interior local maximum. Under (2) with the same parameters, X = 1 < k = 1.25, so the
unique maximizer is g = G by Proposition 2(c).

Proof of Proposition 1
(a) The map σ 7→ g = G + ℓσ α is a continuous strictly increasing bijection from [0, σ̄ ] ∩ R onto I, with inverse
σ (g) = ((g − G)/ℓ)1/α = κ(g − G)β . Every feasible g is therefore produced by exactly one spend level, and the
largest margin attainable at g is m0 − σ (g) − kg = m∗ (g). Hence F = {(g, m) : g ∈ I, m ≤ m∗ (g)}. This is the
hypograph of a concave function (part (c)) over an interval, so it is convex. A point with m < m∗ (g) is dominated by
(g, m∗ (g)). Conversely, suppose (g, m∗ (g)) were dominated by a feasible (g′ , m′ ), so that g′ ≥ g and m′ ≥ m∗ (g) with
one inequality strict. If g′ = g, then m′ > m∗ (g), which is infeasible. If g′ > g, then m′ ≤ m∗ (g′ ) < m∗ (g) because m∗
is strictly decreasing (part (c)), a contradiction.
     (b) m∗ (G) = m0 − kG. The right difference quotient is [m∗ (G + ∆) − m∗ (G)]/∆ = −k − κ∆β −1 → −k as ∆ ↓ 0,
because β > 1.
     (c) For G ≤ g1 < g2 in I, m∗ (g1 ) − m∗ (g2 ) = k(g2 − g1 ) + κ[(g2 − G)β − (g1 − G)β ] > 0, because k ≥ 0, κ > 0 and
∆ 7→ ∆β is strictly increasing on [0, ∞). So m∗ is strictly decreasing on I, and for g > G, m∗′ (g) = −k −β κ(g−G)β −1 <
−k ≤ 0. Strict concavity on I follows from (F1): m∗ is the sum of an affine function and −κ(g − G)β , which is strictly
concave on [G, ∞). The second derivative −β (β − 1)κ(g − G)β −2 is negative for g > G. As g ↓ G, (g − G)β −2 tends
to 0 if β > 2, equals 1 if β = 2, and tends to ∞ if β < 2; with β = 1/α this gives the three cases, and for β = 2 the
value is −2κ.
     (d) With σ̄ = ∞, I = [G, ∞). Since β > 1, κ(g − G)β /g → ∞ as g → ∞, so m∗ (g)/g = m0 /g − k − κ(g − G)β /g →
−∞, which implies m∗ (g) → −∞. Also m∗′ (g) = −k − β κ(g − G)β −1 → −∞.

Proof of Proposition 2
(a) On the line Xg + m = c, m = c − Xg, which has slope −X. If (g, m) ∈ F with m < m∗ (g), then (g, m∗ (g)) ∈ F
has a strictly higher score, so every maximizer lies on the frontier. On the frontier the score is φ (g) := Xg + m∗ (g),
which is continuous and strictly concave on I. If σ̄ < ∞, I is compact and a maximizer exists. If σ̄ = ∞, φ (g) =
g[X + m∗ (g)/g] → −∞ by Proposition 1(d), so a maximizer exists on a compact subinterval. Strict concavity implies
that the maximizer is unique.
     (b) Write φ in terms of ∆ = g − G: φ = m0 + yG + f (∆) with f (∆) = y∆ − κ∆β . For y > 0, f ′ (∆) = y − β κ∆β −1
is positive near 0 and strictly decreasing, so the unique stationary point solves β κ(∆∗ )β −1 = y, that is,
                                    y 1/(β −1)
                            ∆∗ =
                                                               α/(1−α)
                                                   = αy ℓ1/α              = ℓ1/(1−α) (αy)α/(1−α) ,
                                    βκ

using β − 1 = (1 − α)/α and κ −1 = ℓβ . This is (5). Since the cap does not bind, ∆∗ ∈ (0, ḡ − G] and g∗ = G + ∆∗
is interior to [G, ∞). The first-order condition f ′ (∆∗ ) = 0 reads −m∗′ (g∗ ) = k + β κ(∆∗ )β −1 = k + y = X, which is
tangency. The spend producing ∆∗ is σ ∗ = κ(∆∗ )β = ∆∗ · κ(∆∗ )β −1 = ∆∗ y/β = αy∆∗ . Substituting (5), αy∆∗ =
αy ℓ1/(1−α) (αy)α/(1−α) = (αℓy)1/(1−α) , which is (6) and coincides with the maximizer in (F2), as it must. Then
 f (∆∗ ) = y∆∗ − αy∆∗ = (1 − α)y∆∗ , which gives the first form of (7); the second follows from (F2), since (1 − α)y∆∗ =
(1 − α)yℓ(σ ∗ )α . Finally m∗ (g∗ ) = m0 − kg∗ − σ ∗ = m0 − kg∗ − αy∆∗ .
      (c) If y ≤ 0, then f (∆) = y∆ − κ∆β < 0 = f (0) for every ∆ > 0, so g∗ = G is the unique maximizer, and R∗X =
m0 + yG. The argument does not involve the cap.
      (d) Let G < g < ḡ and set X(g) := −m∗′ (g) = k + β κ(g − G)β −1 > k. Then φ with weight X(g) is strictly concave
with φ ′ (g) = 0 at an interior point, so g is its unique maximizer. For any other weight X ′ ̸= X(g), φ ′ (g) = X ′ −X(g) ̸= 0
at the interior point g, so g is not a maximizer. The point g = G is a maximizer if and only if the right derivative
φ ′ (G+ ) = X − k ≤ 0, because φ is concave. If σ̄ = ∞: for X ≤ k, g∗ (X) = G; for X > k, g∗ (X) = G + ∆∗ (X) with



                                                              63
∆∗ continuous, strictly increasing in X and tending to 0 as X ↓ k. So g∗ is continuous and nondecreasing, and strictly
increasing on (k, ∞).
    (e) Π = maxσ ≥0 {yℓσ α − σ } is immediate from (F2) for y > 0 and for y ≤ 0. Since (X − k)G + (X − k)ℓσ α − σ +
m0 = Xg + m0 − σ − kg along the frontier, R∗X = m0 + yG + Π.

Proof of Remarks 5 and 6
For Remark 5, the score along the frontier as a function of spend is m0 + yG + P(σ ) with P from (F2), restricted to
[0, σ̄ ]. Since P is strictly concave and increasing on [0, σ ∗ ], if σ̄ < σ ∗ the maximum over [0, σ̄ ] is at σ̄ . When the
cap binds strictly, σ̄ < σ ∗ holds on a neighborhood of the parameters and the value is m0 + yG + yℓσ̄ α − σ̄ , whose
derivatives are those of (14) with ∆∗ replaced by ℓσ̄ α and σ ∗ by σ̄ . For Remark 6, g 7→ g + m∗ (g) is the sum of a linear
and a strictly concave function, so its upper level set {g : g + m∗ (g) ≥ 0.4} is an interval. If the interval is nonempty,
the maximum of g + m∗ (g) is at least 0.4, so the maximizer belongs to it.

Proof of Proposition 3
(a) By Proposition 2(b), (c) and (e), R∗X (s) = R∞ +Cα (λ h(s)y+ )1/(1−α) for every X ≥ 0. If y ≤ 0, this is constant. If
y > 0, the function ℓ 7→ Cα (ℓy)1/(1−α) is strictly increasing, and h(s) = 1 − s/s̄ is strictly decreasing, so R∗X is strictly
decreasing in s. By the chain rule and (F2),

                          dR∗X    Cα                         1         Π            y∆∗
                               =     (λ y)1/(1−α) hα/(1−α) · −    =−              =−        ,
                           ds    1−α                           s̄    (1 − α) h s̄    s̄ − s

using Π = (1 − α)y∆∗ and hs̄ = s̄ − s.
     (b) As s → s̄, h(s) → 0, and 1/(1 − α) > 0, so the growth surplus tends to 0 and R∗X (s) → R∞ . For X = 1 and
k = 0, R∞ = m0 + G. Since ch ≥ 0 and a ≥ amin , m0 = 1 − ch − cc − f − a ≤ 1 − cc − f − amin ≤ 1, which gives (9).
     (c) By (a), R∗X (s) is nonincreasing in s and continuous in h, so its supremum over (0, s̄) is its limit as s → 0 (h → 1),
namely R0 . If y > 0, it is strictly decreasing, so the supremum is not attained on the open interval.
     (d) For y > 0, R∗X (s) ≥ R̄ if and only if Cα (ℓy)1/(1−α) ≥ R̄ − R∞ > 0. Because x 7→ x1−α is strictly increasing on
(0, ∞), this is equivalent to ℓy ≥ [(R̄ − R∞ )/Cα ]1−α , that is, ℓ ≥ Λ(R̄). Since ℓ = λ h(s) takes every value in (0, λ ) as s
ranges over (0, s̄), some share attains R̄ if and only if λ > Λ(R̄). At ℓ = Λ(R̄), Π = R̄ − R∞ , so ∆∗ = Π/[(1 − α)y] and
σ ∗ = αy∆∗ = αΠ/(1 − α), and the margin is m∗ (g∗ ) = m0 − kg∗ − σ ∗ by Proposition 2(b).

Proof of Proposition 15

Proof of Proposition 16
Fix ∆ > 0 and a target share s1 ∈ (s0 , s̄), and consider the policy that holds excess growth at ∆ until s reaches s1 , at date
T1 = ln(s1 /s0 )/∆, using spend σ (t) = (∆/(λ h(s(t))))1/α and frontier margins. Along the path h(s(t)) ≥ h(s1 ) > 0, so
σ (t) ≤ σ̄1 := (∆/(λ h(s1 )))1/α , which is finite and, for small ∆, below any positive financing cap. The excess score is
RX − R∞ = y∆ − σ (t) ≥ y∆ − σ̄1 , which is positive for small ∆ because σ̄1 /∆ = ∆β −1 (λ h(s1 ))−β → 0. Hence

Proof of Corollary 1
If T periods have RX,t ≥ R̄, each contributes at least ln(1 + (R̄ − R∞ )/[y(1 + G)]) > 0 to the sum in (12), and the other
terms are nonnegative, so T ln(1 + (R̄ − R∞ )/[y(1 + G)]) ≤ ln(s̄/s0 ), which is (13). In the example, y = 1, R∞ = 0.8
and 1 + G = 1.2. For R̄ = 10, ln(1 + 9.2/1.2) = ln 8.667 ≈ 2.159; with s0 = 0.01, ln(s̄/s0 ) = ln 50 ≈ 3.912 and the
bound is 1.81, so T (R̄) ≤ 1; with s0 = 10−5 , ln(5 × 104 ) ≈ 10.820 and the bound is 5.01, so T (R̄) ≤ 5. For R̄ = 1.4
and s0 = 0.01, ln(1 + 0.6/1.2) = ln 1.5 ≈ 0.405 and the bound is 9.65, so T (R̄) ≤ 9.

                                                               65
Proof of Corollary 2
Let R(k) := R∗X at a fixed. The total change is ∆R∗X = da + R(k + dk) − R(k), because R∗X is linear in a with
slope −1. By Proposition 5, R is convex and differentiable with R ′ (k) = −g∗ (k). Convexity gives R ′ (k) dk ≤
R(k + dk) − R(k) ≤ R ′ (k + dk) dk, which is (15). If da > g∗ (k)dk, the lower bound is positive. If da ≤ g∗ (k + dk)dk,
the upper bound is nonpositive. To first order, ∆R∗X = da − g∗ dk + o(dk).

Proof of Corollary 3
(i) R∗X = m0 + yG + Π and R∞ = m0 + yG are both linear in a with slope −1, so removing da raises each by da exactly.
     (ii) R∗X depends on λ only through Π = Cα (λ hy)1/(1−α) , so replacing λ by λ (1 + xλ ) multiplies Π by (1 +
xλ )1/(1−α)  and changes R∗X by exactly Π[(1 + xλ )1/(1−α) − 1]. To first order this is Πxλ /(1 − α) = y∆∗ xλ , using
Π = (1 − α)y∆∗ , and y∆∗ = σ ∗ /α by (6). Cutting a non-discretionary cost line of size cL by the proportion xλ raises
R∗X by xλ cL by (i), which equals xλ σ ∗ /α when cL = σ ∗ /α; for a line of size σ ∗ the gain is xλ σ ∗ , a ratio of 1/α. R∞
does not depend on λ , and the gain tends to zero as s → s̄ because Π → 0 (Proposition 3(b)).
     (iii) With g = Gi + ℓσ α , the proof of Proposition 2 applies with G replaced by Gi (Remark 4(b)), so R∗X = m0 +
yGi + Π and R∞ = m0 + yGi , both with derivative y in Gi .
     (iv) By Lemma 3(a), dγ = −(1 − ϕ)du/p to first order, which is positive for du < 0 and ϕ < 1, and ∂ R∗X /∂ γ = 1.
Under free entry without a scarce input, Proposition 8(b) gives Ln∗ → 0 as F → 0 for every u, and the limiting score
XG + (r − G)ku /ĉ with ĉ = u + rku depends on u only through ku /ĉ, and not at all if ku = 0.
     (v) This is Corollary 2; the first-order hurdle g∗ dk is increasing in g∗ , and g∗ ≥ G.

where the left side may be −∞. Because v̂ is bounded and r > G, the last term tends to 0, so the limit superior of the
left side is at most v̂(s0 ). Taking the supremum over policies gives v ≤ v̂. Now suppose the feedback generates a path
st from s0 . The map s 7→ σ̂ (s) = [αℓ(s)(v̂′ (s) − k)+ ]1/(1−α) is continuous, so σt := σ̂ (st ) is continuous and locally
bounded, hence admissible, and its induced share path is st (the share equation has a unique solution for a given spend
path). By (F2), σ̂ (s) attains the maximum in (17), so the inequalities above hold with equality and (34) becomes an
equality. Letting T → ∞, the policy attains v̂(s0 ). Hence v̂(s0 ) ≤ v(s0 ), so v̂(s0 ) = v(s0 ) and the policy is optimal. If
this holds from every s0 , then v̂ = v.
     (c) On the frontier, g = G + ∆ and m = m0 − σ − k(G + ∆), so RX (g, m) − XG = X∆ + m0 − σ − kG − k∆ =
m0 − σ − kG + (X − k)∆, which is the maximand with X = v̂′ (s). Maximizing it over σ is therefore maximizing RX
over the frontier at the current share, and by Proposition 2(a) also over the feasible set. Its maximum is R∗X (s) − XG
by Proposition 2(e), for every X when σ̄ = ∞. Under the conditions of (b), v̂ = v, so X dyn (s) = v′ (s) = ∂V /∂ S by (a),
and (17) reads (r − G)v(s)/s = R∗X dyn (s) (s) − G X dyn (s).
      (d) The root. For υ ≥ 0 let Πλ (υ) := Cα [λ (υ − k)+ ]1/(1−α) and f (υ) := (r − G)υ − (m0 − kG) − Πλ (υ), so that
(19) is f (v0 ) = 0. By the proof of Proposition 5, Πλ is continuously differentiable with Π′λ (υ) = ∆∗ (λ , υ). Since k ≥ 0,
f (0) = −(m0 − kG) < 0, so the smallest root v0 is positive and f < 0 on [0, v0 ). Hence f ′ (v0 ) ≥ 0, and simplicity gives

Proof of Remark 7
Firm 1 is feasible at g = 0.1 (given ℓ1 σ̄ α ≥ 0.1) with m∗1 (0.1) = 0.2 − κ1 (0.1)β < 0.2. Firm 2 is not feasible at
g = 0.1 < G2 , because its growth is at least G2 . Hence Φ(0.1) = m∗1 (0.1) < 0.2. At g = 0.3, firm 1 attains at most
m∗1 (0.3) < 0.2 (if feasible), and firm 2 attains m∗2 (0.3) = 0.5, so Φ(0.3) = 0.5 > Φ(0.1).

Proof of Proposition 8
(a) Fix n firms. At date t, let x be a firm’s output and Q− the others’ output. Its flow profit, including the user cost of
capital rku per unit, is (A − B(x + Q− )e−Gt − ĉ)x. Owning capital gives the same present value as renting it: for output
x(t) = x0 eGt , the owner invests ku x0 at entry and  k ẋ = k Gx eGt thereafter, with present value ku x0 + ku Gx0 /(r − G) =
                                                   R ∞ u−rt u 0Gt
rku x0 /(r − G), which equals the present value 0 e rku x0 e dt of rental payments. We therefore work with the rental
form. The best response is x = [(A − ĉ)eGt /B − Q− ]/2, and the symmetric equilibrium is xn (t) = (A − ĉ)eGt /[B(n + 1)],
with price Pn = A − Bnxn e−Gt = (A + nĉ)/(n + 1), constantR over time. Flow profit is (Pn − ĉ)xn = πn eGt with πn =
(A − ĉ)2 /[B(n + 1)2 ], and an entrant’s net present value is 0∞ e−rt πn eGt dt − F = πn /(r − G) − F. The Lerner index
is (Pn − ĉ)/Pn = [(A − ĉ)/(n + 1)]/[(A + nĉ)/(n + 1)] = (A − ĉ)/(A + nĉ). For a firm that owns its capital, revenue is
Pn x, variable cost is ux, and the capital stock is ku x, so kn = ku x/(Pn x) = ku /Pn . The operating margin is (Pn − u)/Pn =
(Pn − ĉ)/Pn + rku /Pn = Ln + rkn . Investment is ku ẋ = ku Gx, so the FCF margin is mop − Gkn = (r − G)kn + Ln . Since
revenue grows at G, the score is XG + (r − G)kn + Ln .
     (b) An entrant enters if and only if πn /(r − G) ≥ F, that is, (A − ĉ)2 /[B(n + 1)2 ] ≥ F̂, or n + 1 ≤ NF . Because πn
is decreasing in n, free entry gives the largest n with n + 1 ≤ NF , namely n∗ = ⌊NF ⌋ − 1 ≥ 1. Positivity of Ln∗ holds
because A > ĉ. For the upper bound, A + n∗ ĉ > ĉ + n∗ ĉ = ĉ⌊NF ⌋ > ĉ(NF − 1) > 0, because A > ĉ > 0 and ⌊NF ⌋ >
NF − 1 ≥ 1; hence Ln∗ = (A − ĉ)/(A + n∗ ĉ) < (A − ĉ)/[ĉ(NF − 1)]. As F → 0, F̂ → 0, so NF → ∞, n∗ → ∞, Ln∗ → 0
and Pn∗ → ĉ; hence kn∗ → ku /ĉ and the score tends to XG + (r − G)ku /ĉ. For X = 1, G + (r − G)k̂ = G(1 − k̂) + rk̂.
     (c) With the input price w per unit, the full unit cost is ĉ + w, and the Cournot quantities from (a) give total
output Qn (w) = n(A − ĉ − w)eGt /[B(n + 1)] whenever w < A − ĉ. The input market clears with w > 0 if and only if
Qn (0) > ZeGt , that is, n(A− ĉ)/[B(n+1)] > Z. Since n/(n+1) ≥ 1/2 and Z < (A− ĉ)/(2B), this holds for every n ≥ 1.
Solving Qn (w) = ZeGt gives wn = A − ĉ − BZ(n + 1)/n > 0, and the price is P = A − BZ, independent of n. Each firm
produces x = ZeGt /n and earns flow profit net of all costs (including the input at price wn ) (P − ĉ − wn )x = [BZ/n] ·
ZeGt /n = BZ 2 eGt /n2 ; the net Lerner index is Lnnet = (P − ĉ − wn )/P = BZ/(nP). A firm that owns its input requirement
pays nothing for the input, so its operating margin is (P − u)/P = rku /P + wn /P + (P − ĉ − wn )/P = rk + ρn + Lnnet ,
with k = ku /P and ρn = wn /P. A firm that buys the input has mop = (P − u − wnq             )/P = rk + Lnnet . An entrant buys
the input, so entry is profitable if and only if BZ 2 /[n2 (r − G)] ≥ F, that is, n ≤ Z B/F̂. Free entry therefore gives
        q
 ∗
n = ⌊Z B/F̂⌋, which is at least 1 under the stated assumption and tends to infinity as F → 0. Then wn∗ → A− ĉ−BZ,
ρn∗ → (A − BZ − ĉ)/(A − BZ) = 1 − ĉ/(A − BZ) = ρ, and Lnnet     ∗ → 0. FCF margins subtract Gk as in (a), which gives
(25).

Proof of Remark 8
Under homogeneous Bertrand competition with identical full unit cost ĉ, two or more active firms price at ĉ and earn
zero flow profit, so the net value of entry for a second firm is −F < 0. Hence at most one firm enters, and a single
entrant charges the monopoly price, which it does provided monopoly profit covers F. Under contestability with a
flow fixed cost F̂ that is not sunk, any price above average cost ĉ + F̂/x invites hit-and-run entry, so in equilibrium


                                                              69
P = ĉ + F̂/x and L = (P − ĉ)/P = F̂/(Px) → 0 as F → 0. A firm that rents its capital holds no capital stock, so k = 0
and the score in Proposition 8(a) is XG + Ln , with limit XG.

Proof of Lemma 3
(a) γ = 1 − f − u/p(u). Differentiating, dγ/du = −[p − up′ ]/p2 = −(1/p)[1 − up′ /p] = −(1 − ϕ)/p. A reduction in
u raises γ if and only if dγ/du < 0, that is, ϕ < 1. Cournot with linear demand: from the proof of Proposition 8(a),
p = (A + n(u + rku ))/(n + 1), so p′ = n/(n + 1) and ϕ = n+1    n
                                                                   u/p < 1. Monopoly with constant elasticity ηD > 1:
       ηD
p = ηD −1 (u + rku ), so ϕ = u/(u + rku ). Perfect competition: p = u + rku , so ϕ = u/(u + rku ). In both of the last two
cases ϕ ≤ 1, with equality if and only if rku = 0, that is, b = 0 (given r > 0).
     (b) Nominal market size is Mt = pt Qt with Qt = Q0 eGQ t pt−ηD and pt = p0 e−δ p t . Then ln Mt = const + GQt − (1 −
ηD )δ pt, so G = d ln M/dt = GQ − (1 − ηD )δ p . The sign claims are immediate.

Proof of Proposition 9
By Proposition 5, R∗X is continuously differentiable in (m0 , G, k, λ ), so the chain rule gives dR∗X /dζ = m′0 + (X −
k)G′ − g∗ k′ + (X − k)(∆∗ /λ )λ ′ , with m′0 = γ ′ − a′ . If the parameters are continuously differentiable, so is ζ 7→ R∗X , and
a positive (negative) derivative at ζ persists on a neighborhood, on which R∗X is strictly increasing (decreasing). The
expression for γ ′ follows from Lemma 3(a) and the chain rule.

Proof of Proposition 18
(a) Denote old and new parameters by subscripts 0 and 1, with λ1 ≥ λ0 , G1 ≥ G0 , k1 ≤ k0 and m01 −k1 G1 ≥ m00 −k0 G0 .
Since h(s) is unchanged, ℓ1 ≥ ℓ0 , so κ1 ≤ κ0 and the new growth bound satisfies ḡ1 − G1 ≥ ḡ0 − G0 . Take a feasible
old point (g, m), so m ≤ m∗0 (g) with g ∈ [G0 , ḡ0 ]. Case 1: g ≥ G1 . Then g − G1 ≤ g − G0 ≤ ḡ0 − G0 ≤ ḡ1 − G1 , so
g is feasible under the new parameters. Write m∗j (g) = (m0 j − k j G j ) − k j (g − G j ) − κ j (g − G j )β for j = 0, 1. Then
m∗1 (g) − m∗0 (g) is the sum of three nonnegative terms: (m01 − k1 G1 ) − (m00 − k0 G0 ) ≥ 0; k0 (g − G0 ) − k1 (g − G1 ) ≥ 0,
because k0 ≥ k1 ≥ 0 and g − G0 ≥ g − G1 ≥ 0; and κ0 (g − G0 )β − κ1 (g − G1 )β ≥ 0, for the same reason. Hence
(g, m∗1 (g)) is new-feasible and dominates or equals (g, m). Case 2: g < G1 . Then the new point (G1 , m∗1 (G1 )) has
G1 > g and m∗1 (G1 ) = m01 − k1 G1 ≥ m00 − k0 G0 = m∗0 (G0 ) ≥ m∗0 (g) ≥ m, using Proposition 1(c). So (g, m) is strictly
dominated. In both cases every old feasible point is weakly dominated by a new feasible point. Since X ≥ 0, a
dominating point has a weakly higher score, so R∗X,1 ≥ R∗X,0 . Reversing the roles of the two parameter vectors proves
the reverse statement.
     (b) The difference between the new and old frontier at g is dm0 − dk g, which is positive for g < dm0 /dk and
negative for g > dm0 /dk.

Proof of Remark 1
Taxable profit per dollar of revenue is m0 − σ − (deductions for capital), and with linear taxes and full loss offset the
after-tax FCF margin is (1 − τ)(m0 − σ ) − kg + ψτkg = (1 − τ)(m0 − σ ) − (1 − ψτ)kg, where ψτkg is the present
value of the tax shield on new capital. Let σ̃ = (1 − τ)σ be after-tax spend. Then g = G + ℓσ α = G + ℓ(1 − τ)−α σ̃ α ,
so the growth technology in terms of σ̃ has demand pull λ̃ = (1 − τ)−α λ , and σ̃ ∈ [0, (1 − τ)σ̄ ]. The margin is
m̃0 − σ̃ − k̃g with m̃0 = (1 − τ)m0 and k̃ = (1 − ψτ)k. This is Assumptions 2–4 with the substituted parameters, so
every result derived from those assumptions applies. Explicitly, the taxed frontier is m̃0 − k̃g − (λ̃ h)−β (g − G)β , and
(λ̃ h)−β = (1 − τ)κ. For α = 0.3, (1 − τ)−α equals 1.050 at τ = 0.15 and 1.073 at τ = 0.21. For (27), a dollar
deducted ratably over T years from the middle of the first tax year yields a half-year deduction of 1/(2T ) in the first
year, 1/T in each of the next T − 1 years and 1/(2T ) in the year after; discounting each year’s deduction to the first,
                          T −1

ψ5 = 0.796 and ψ15 = 0.532.

Proof of Corollary 4
By Remark 1 and (8), the saturated score is m̃0 + (X − k̃)G = (1 − τ)(1 − ch − cc − f − a) + [X − (1 − ψτ)k]G. Under
Assumption 4 the replacement capital spending δK kS is part of the non-discretionary cost, and under full automation


                                                              70
a also carries the floors of Definition 3, so a ≥ amin + cfull + δK k. Because 1 − τ > 0, the saturated score is strictly
decreasing in ch and in a, so it is maximized over ch ≥ 0 and a ≥ amin + cfull + δK k exactly at ch = 0 and a =
amin + cfull + δK k, which gives Rmax and the equality condition. With X = 1, ψ = 0 and δK = 0, Rmax reduces to (29);
at k = 0 it is at least R̄ if and only if G ≥ R̄−(1−τ)(1−cc − f −amin −cfull ). Setting ψ = 1 adds τkG, and a positive δK
subtracts (1−τ)δK k. The statement about R∗X (s)−R∞ is Proposition 3(a)–(b) applied to the substituted parameters, and
the integral bound is Proposition 4(a), whose proof uses only m ≤ m̃0 − k̃g and ṡ/s = g−G. The entries of Tables 10 and
13 are direct evaluations; for example, the central margin term is 0.82 × (1 − 0.20 − 0.025 − 0.03 − 0.0409) = 0.5774,
so Rmax = 1 with k = 0 requires G ≈ 0.423. With cfull = 0 and amin = 0.06 in the conservative case the margin terms
are those of the earlier form of the corollary, 0.6928, 0.6109, 0.5214 and 0.4469, which require G ≈ 0.307, 0.389,
0.479 and 0.553.

Proof of Proposition 10
(a) A misstatement goes undetected if and only if every layer of the design fails. For a type j with n j ≥ 1 layers, all
of them fail either through the common mode, with probability pcm     j , or, if it does not occur, each independently, with

probability p j , so all fail with probability p j + (1 − p j )p j . Types fail independently, so Pmm is the product of
these factors over the types used. For one type the factor is strictly decreasing in n j , because p j < 1 and pcm
                                                                                                                 j < 1, and
it tends to pcm                           cm
             j . Each factor is at least p j , which gives the lower bound. (b) The annual cost of a design is the cost
of accountability, the lines of the in-scope AI applications, the cost of the layers and the expected loss Nmm Lmm Pmm ;
dividing by revenue and minimizing over designs gives (28). An all-AI design on one provider uses one type, so
Pmm = pcm            cm n j       cm
          j + (1 − p j )p j > p j ; it has at least one in-scope application and nonnegative layer costs, which gives the

Proof of Proposition 11
Two facts are used throughout. First, Zn is closed under taking subsets: removing processes from a wave keeps it
within capacity because uses are nonnegative. Second, Zn ⊆ Zn′ for n ≤ n′ , because empty waves are allowed.
     (a) If t ≤ t ′ , then n(t) ≤ n(t ′ ), so Zn(t) ⊆ Zn(t ′ ) and At ⊆ At ′ . Raising a capacity or lowering a use keeps every
wave within capacity, so Zn weakly grows; raising Temb weakly lowers n(t). A union over a larger family of sets
is larger. On each F (ϑZ ) the linear score RX attains its maximum R∗X (ϑZ ) (Proposition 2(a) and Remark 5), and
the family is finite, so the supremum over At is maxZ ∈Zn(t) R∗X (ϑZ ), and set inclusion gives its monotonicity. For
t < Temb , n(t) = 0, Z0 = {0}   / and At = F (ϑ0 ). Finally, Zn is contained in the set of all subsets of P.
     (b) By the first fact, Zn contains every subset of P if and only if P ∈ Zn , that is, if and only if n ≥ n̄. Since
n(t) ≥ n̄ if and only if t ≥ Temb + (n̄ − 1)Tpar , At = A∞ from that date on. If every process fits a wave on its own, one
process per wave gives a split into N waves; if the total uses fit in one wave, n̄ = 1, and n(Temb ) = 1.
     (c) Summing the wave constraints over the at most n(t) waves of a split of Z gives ∑ p∈Z cip ≤ n(t)Γi . With
identical uses, a wave can hold j processes if and only if jci ≤ Γi for every i, that is, j ≤ ncap , so |Z | ≤ n(t)ncap .
Embedding all N processes requires n(t) ≥ ⌈N/ncap ⌉, that is, t ≥ Temb + (⌈N/ncap ⌉ − 1)Tpar . Because Temb ≥ Tpar and
ncap ≤ Γi /ci , this date is at least ⌈N/ncap ⌉Tpar ≥ NTpar /ncap ≥ Nci Tpar /Γi for every i.
     (d) By Proposition 2(b), (c) and (e), R∗X = m0 + (X − k)G + Π, where Π depends on λ h and X − k but not on m0
or G. With k and λ fixed, R∗X (ϑZ ) − R∗X (ϑ0 ) = ∑ p∈Z ∆R p . For Z ∈ Zn(t) , the indicator vector of Z is feasible for
the linear program that defines Vi (n(t)Γi ), by (c), so R∗X (ϑZ ) − R∗X (ϑ0 ) ≤ Vi (n(t)Γi ); maximizing over Z gives the
bound. The value of a linear maximization as a function of its right-hand side is concave and piecewise linear, and it
is nondecreasing here because the feasible set grows with C. An optimal solution uses no process with ∆R p ≤ 0, takes
every process with cip = 0 and ∆R p > 0, and otherwise fills capacity in decreasing order of ∆R p /cip : if x j > 0 and xl < 1
with ∆Rl /cil > ∆R j /cij , moving εcil units of capacity from j to l raises the objective by εcil (∆Rl /cil − ∆R j /cij ) > 0.

Proof of Proposition 12
Let B := {ϑ0 + ∑ p x p δ ϑ p : x ∈ [0, 1]N }. It is compact, ϑt ∈ B always, and R∗X is continuously differentiable on B
(Proposition 5), hence bounded, so every expectation below exists. Plans may randomize. Two observations are used.
(i) Reverting a process removes its increment, and the firm may revert at any time. (ii) Freezes slow the program’s
clock, so for every plan the law of ϑt is the law, in the model without freezes, of ϑtR .
     (a) The plan that never attempts anything gives ϑt = ϑ0 . For the upper bound, fix a plan and a realization, and
let Et be the set of processes embedded by t. By (ii), each p ∈ Et has a successful attempt that started in a wave w
with (w − 1)Tpar + Temb ≤ tR , that is, w ≤ n(tR ). The successful attempts of each wave are a subset of that wave’s
attempts, which are within capacity, so Et ∈ Zn(tR ) . Now ϑt = ϑ0 + ∑ p∈Et b p δ ϑ p . Raising the shares b p to one, one at
a time, moves ϑ along the directions δ ϑ p inside B, and along each such segment R∗X has derivative ∇R∗X · δ ϑ p ≥ 0 for
X-improving processes. Hence R∗X (ϑt ) ≤ R∗X (ϑEt ) ≤ RattX (tR ). Taking expectations and the supremum over plans gives
the bound.
     (b) Time. Given a plan for the horizon t and a later date t ′ , follow the plan until t, make no attempt after t, and
revert each process at the moment it would embed after t, by (i). Then ϑt ′ = ϑt , so R̄X (t ′ ) ≥ E R∗X (ϑt ) for every plan,
and R̄X (t ′ ) ≥ R̄X (t).
     Capacities and uses. The set of plans grows when a capacity rises or a use falls.
     Success. Let ϖ p′ > ϖ p , and take any plan of the model with ϖ p . In the model with ϖ p′ , run the same plan, except
that whenever an attempt of p works, an independent coin that comes up heads with probability ϖ p /ϖ p′ is drawn; on
tails, p is reverted when the outcome is revealed, and the plan is told that the attempt failed. Each attempt of p then
works with probability ϖ p , independently of everything else, so the joint law of the plan’s information and of ϑt is the
same as in the model with ϖ p . Hence the value with ϖ p′ is at least the value with ϖ p .
     Disruption. Let νD′ > νD , and take any plan of the model with νD′ . In the model with νD , superpose on the flight
of every attempt an independent Poisson process of rate νD′ − νD , revert the process at its first point, and report that
point to the plan as a disruption. The first point of the superposed processes arrives at rate νD′ , so the joint law is that



                                                              72
of the model with νD′ , and the value with νD is at least the value with νD′ . The parameters ν L , ν V and pkeep enter only
through νD (and, for a sponsorship reset, through ϖ p , which the previous step covers).
      Freezes. By (ii), R̄X (t) equals the value at tR of the model without freezes, which is nondecreasing in its time
argument, and tR falls as ν R Tfrz rises.
      Embedding time. Let Temb  ′   > Temb and let every process be X-improving. Take any plan of the model with Temb            ′ .

In the model with Temb , run it with the same waves, and add to the real disruption process, which covers [s, s + Temb ]
for an attempt started at s, an independent Poisson process of rate νD on (s + Temb , s + Temb      ′ ], reverting the process at

its first point. The combined disruption process is Poisson with rate νD over the whole longer flight, so the plan’s
information has the law of the model with Temb      ′ . At every date, every process that is embedded in that model (started
                  ′
at s with s + Temb ≤ t, working and not disrupted) is also embedded in the model with Temb , with the same share; the
additional processes embedded only in the faster model have increments b p δ ϑ p with b p ∈ [0, 1]. Adding them moves
ϑt along improving directions inside B, so R∗X does not fall, and the value with Temb is at least the value with Temb       ′ .
                                                                           ′                   ′
      Realized shares. Let every process be X-improving, and let b p have distribution Fp ≤ Fp , where Fp is the distribu-
tion of b p . Take any plan of the model with Fp . When an attempt of p works in the model with Fp′ , draw an independent
uniform U, let Ũ := Fp′ (b′p −) + U[Fp′ (b′p ) − Fp′ (b′p −)], which is uniform on [0, 1] with Fp′−1 (Ũ) = b′p almost surely,
and report b̃ p := Fp−1 (Ũ) to the plan. Then b̃ p has distribution Fp , and b̃ p ≤ b′p because Fp ≥ Fp′ implies Fp−1 ≤ Fp′−1 .
The plan’s actions have the law of the model with Fp , and the realized parameters exceed that model’s in every share.
Since R∗X is nondecreasing in each share on B, the value with Fp′ is at least the value with Fp .
      (c) Let S be the set of attempts that start in waves 1, . . . , n(tR ). As in (a), every process embedded
by t has a successful, undisrupted attempt in S , and distinct processes have distinct attempts, so |Et | ≤
∑ι∈S 1{ι works and is not disrupted}. The decision to make an attempt depends only on information available when
it starts, and its outcome is independent of that information and works and survives with probability pemb . Sum-
ming over the at most ncap attempts of each of the n(tR ) waves, E|Et | ≤ pemb E|S | ≤ pemb ncap n(tR ), and |Et | ≤ N.
For equality, let the plan attempt, in each wave, ncap processes that are neither embedded nor in flight, and never
revert a process voluntarily. At the start of wave w ≤ n(tR ) at most ncap (w − 1) ≤ N − ncap processes are embed-
ded or in flight when N ≥ ncap n(tR ), so the wave can be filled; a process is attempted again only after a failure or a
disruption, so successes belong to distinct processes, and E|Et | = pemb ncap n(tR ). For the score, Proposition 11(d)
gives R∗X (ϑt ) = R∗X (ϑ0 ) + ∆R ∑ p∈Et b p . Also ∑ p∈Et b p ≤ ∑ι∈S bι 1{ι works and is not disrupted}, where bι is the
share drawn on the success of attempt ι, independently of everything known at its start. This sum has expectation
b̄ pemb E|S | ≤ b̄ pemb ncap n(tR ), and ∑ p∈Et b p ≤ |Et | ≤ N. The bound holds even for plans that revert processes with
low realized shares and try again.
      (d) Let the plan attempt, in each wave and in a fixed cyclic order, as many processes that are neither embedded
nor in flight as capacity allows, which is at least one. Every process that is not embedded is then attempted infinitely
often until it embeds, and each attempt embeds it with probability pemb      p > 0, independently, so the number of attempts
it needs is geometric and almost surely finite. After the last process embeds, ϑt = ϑ∞ := ϑ0 + ∑ p b p δ ϑ p , with b p
the share of its successful attempt. Hence R∗X (ϑt ) → R∗X (ϑ∞ ) almost surely, and bounded convergence gives the limit
of expectations. If every process is X-improving, R∗X (ϑ∞ ) ≤ R∗X (ϑP ) as in (a); in the setting of (c), E R∗X (ϑ∞ ) =
R∗X (ϑ0 ) + N b̄ ∆R = R∗X (ϑP ) − (1 − b̄)N∆R.
      For the last claim, let Z ⋆ maximize R∗X (ϑZ ) over all subsets, so that R∗X (ϑZ ⋆ ) = Ratt                              i
