Proof of Lemma 5
(a) For each realization, m∗ (·; ϑt ) is concave on [G(ϑt ), ∞) (Proposition 1(c)) and equal to −∞ to the left of it, so it is
concave on the real line as an extended-valued function. It is bounded above on B, so its expectation is well defined
and, as an average of concave functions, concave. If only m0 and k vary, G is fixed and m∗ (g; ϑ ) = m0 −kg−κ(g−G)β
is affine in (m0 , k) for each g, so E m∗ (g; ϑt ) = m∗ (g; Eϑt ). (b) For every g and realization, Xg + m∗ (g; ϑt ) ≤ R∗X (ϑt )
by Proposition 2(a). Taking expectations gives Xg + m̄t (g) ≤ E R∗X (ϑt ) for every g, and a firm that fixes g in advance
and then earns the frontier margin at g scores Xg + m̄t (g) in expectation.




                                                                73
Proof of Remark 2
(i) The attainable set is a finite union of sets on each of which RX attains its maximum, so maximizers exist. Let (g, m)
maximize RX and (g′ , m′ ) maximize RX ′ on it, with X < X ′ . Then Xg + m ≥ Xg′ + m′ and X ′ g′ + m′ ≥ X ′ g + m; adding
gives (X ′ − X)(g′ − g) ≥ 0, so g′ ≥ g. If g′ > g, m′ ≥ m and X > 0, then Xg′ + m′ > Xg + m, which contradicts the
optimality of (g, m); so m′ < m. (ii) Every split into waves within capacity of a subset of P ′ is also one of a subset
of P, so the families, and with them the attainable and static sets, are nested. (iii) A tighter dip budget lowers the dip
capacity, and by hypothesis slower decisions lower the decision capacity; Propositions 11(a) and 12(b) give the effect
on At and R̄X (t). The static set A∞ does not depend on capacities.

Proof of Proposition 13
(a) Substituting the frontier (4) into e = m + k(g − r) gives e∗ (g) = m0 − rk − κ(g − G)β . Its derivative −β κ(g −
G)β −1 tends to zero as g ↓ G and is negative for g > G, and its second derivative is that of m∗ , so it is concave by
Proposition 1(c). For k > 0, (m∗ + kg)/k = [m0 − κ(g − G)β ]/k. For each g the maps m 7→ m + k(g − r) and m 7→
(m + kg)/k are strictly increasing, so the largest feasible m at g, produced by the spend κ(g − G)β (Proposition 1(a)),
gives the largest feasible economic margin and ROIC at g. Both frontiers are strictly decreasing for g > G, so every
point on them is undominated.
     (b) (X − k)g + m + k(g − r) + rk = Xg + m, and k[(m + kg)/k + (X/k − 1)g] = m + kg + Xg − kg = Xg + m. Since
qK > 1 if and only if X > k, the last claim is Proposition 2(b)    and (c).
     (c) With revenue Segt and a constant margin, V = 0∞ e−rt mSegt dt = mS/(r − g) for g < r. Substituting m =
                                                             R

e−k(g−r) gives V /S = k +e/(r −g), and dividing by k and substituting e = k(ROIC−r) gives V /(kS) = 1+(ROIC−
r)/(r − g) = (ROIC − g)/(r − g). The FCF yield is mS/V = r − g. The set V /S = v is the line e = (v − k)(r − g),
which passes through (r, 0) with slope −(v − k). By Proposition 6(d), v0 = m◦ /(r − g◦ ), and (g◦ , m◦ ) maximizes Rv0
on the frontier with ℓ = λ ; if v0 > k the optimum is interior and −m∗′ (g◦ ) = v0 (Proposition 2(b)). Hence −e∗′ (g◦ ) =
−m∗′ (g◦ ) − k = v0 − k and e∗ (g◦ ) = m◦ + k(g◦ − r) = (v0 − k)(r − g◦ ).
     (d) With k = 0, e = m by definition. With X = 1, (b) gives R1 = (1 − k)g + e + rk, whose derivative in g at fixed
(e, k) is 1−k and in k at fixed (g, e) is r −g. In the example, (r −g)×1.5 = −0.075 and (1−1.5)×0.01 = −0.005.

Proof of Remark 11
Bought as a service, the compute adds (1 + υc )TCO to cc . Owned, it adds (1 − ssrv )TCO to cc , the replacement
capital spending kc /Lsrv = ssrv TCO to a, and kc = ssrv Lsrv TCO to k. By Corollary 4 with X = 1 and ψ = 0, owning
minus buying changes the ceiling by (1 − τ)[(1 + υc ) − (1 − ssrv ) − ssrv ]TCO − kc G = (1 − τ)υc TCO − ssrv Lsrv TCO G.
With ssrv Lsrv = 0.6 × 5.5 = 3.3 and τ = 0.18, the threshold markup 3.3G/0.82 is 0.201 at G = 0.05 and 0.604 at
G = 0.15.

Proof of Remark 4
(a) At a given date S is given, so m0 = γ − av − Ξ/S is a constant in the static problem and Propositions 1, 2 and 5
apply as stated. R∞ = γ − av − Ξ/(sM) + (X − k)G is increasing in S = sM. At fixed M, R∗X (s) = R∞ (s) + Π(s), and

R∗X is increasing at small s. Along a path with largest revenue Smax , mt ≤ m0 (St ) − σt − kgt ≤ m0 (Smax ) − σt − kgt , so
the proof of Proposition 4 applies with m0 (Smax ). For Proposition 6,     the FCF flow is (γ − av − σ − kg)S − Ξ, so for

                                                                         R

policy; the optimal policy and ∂V /∂ S are therefore those of Proposition 6 with m0 = γ − av .
     (b) With g = Gi + ℓσ α , the static problem at a date is that of Section 3 with G replaced by Gi , so the static
propositions hold with this substitution. Along any path, RX ≤ Xg + m0 − kg = m0 + (X − k)Gi + (X             − k)(g − Gi ),
and g ≥ Gi , so (RX − R∞ )+ ≤ (X − k)(g − Gi ) with R∞ = m0 + (X − k)Gi . Since ṡ/s = g − G, 0T (g − Gi ) dt =

ln(s(T )/s0 ) + (G − Gi )T .
     (c) For k < 0, the proofs of Proposition 2(a),(b),(e), Proposition 3, Proposition 5 and Proposition 4 do not use the
sign of k (for X ≥ 0 > k, y > 0, so part (b) of Proposition 2 applies). The frontier satisfies m∗′ (g) = −k −β κ(g−G)β −1 ,
which is positive for g − G < ∆0 and negative beyond, so m∗ is strictly increasing on [G, G + ∆0 ] and every point with
g < G + ∆0 is dominated by (G + ∆0 , m∗ (G + ∆0 )); strict concavity is unaffected. A single firm with k < 0 already

makes Φ = m∗ increasing on [G, G + ∆0 ], so Proposition 7(i) can fail. The proof of Proposition 6(d) uses k ≥ 0
in f (0) < 0 and in mt ≥ m◦ . With depreciation, investment is K̇ + δ K = k(g + δ )S, so m = (m0 − δ k) − σ − kg,
which is (2) with intercept m0 − δ k. Then R∗X (k) = R(k) − δ k with R convex as in the proof of Corollary 2, so
∂ R∗X /∂ k = −(g∗ + δ ) and the bracket (15) holds with g∗ replaced by g∗ + δ . At g∗ = 0.10, δ = 0.25 and dk = 0.10
the hurdle is 0.035; at g∗ = 0.50 it is 0.075.
     (d) The proofs of Propositions 1, 2, 5, 7 and 17 take ℓ as given at a point and never use h′ . The proof of Proposi-
tion 4 uses the functional form only to show s(t) < s̄, which the cap s ≤ s̄ provides directly. Monotonicity of h enters
the proof of Proposition 3(a) (sign of dR∗X /ds) and (c) (the supremum is the limit at s → 0), the attainability statement
in (d) (the range of λ h is (0, λ )), the bound ℓ(s) ≤ λ in the proof of Proposition 6(d), and the logistic solution in the
proof of Proposition 15.

D     Data Construction
Source and retrieval. Firm data come from the SEC’s XBRL companyfacts interface (U.S. Securities and Ex-
change Commission, 2026b), which returns every fact a registrant has filed, with its period, value, accession number,
form and filing date. The universe is the 3,641 registrants listed under SIC codes 7370 to 7374 in the EDGAR company
index, which includes inactive registrants. We retrieved companyfacts for the 662 of them that ever report at least
$50 million of revenue, keeping facts from annual reports (forms 10-K, 10-K/A, 10-KT, 10-KT/A, 20-F, 20-F/A and



                                                                 75
40-F) for 35 US-GAAP elements: 240,732 facts in all. Retrieval took place on 28 and 29 October 2026, through a
browser on the author’s computer.

As-first-reported construction. For each firm and fiscal year ending on date E, the original filing is the earliest-
filed annual report containing an annual revenue fact (340 to 392 days) ending on E. Revenue is the largest of the
revenue elements listed below reported for E in that filing, and growth uses the largest revenue element reported in the
same filing for the prior fiscal year (ending 345 to 385 days before E). Every other item is taken from the same filing,
and from the earliest later filing only if the original does not report it. Instants (goodwill, property and equipment) are
taken at E and, for goodwill, at the prior year-end in the same filing.

Elements.
    • Revenue: Revenues; RevenueFromContractWithCustomerExcludingAssessedTax;
      RevenueFromContractWithCustomerIncludingAssessedTax; SalesRevenueNet;
      SalesRevenueServicesNet.
    • Operating cash flow: NetCashProvidedByUsedInOperatingActivitiesContinuingOperations if
      reported, otherwise NetCashProvidedByUsedInOperatingActivities.
    • Capital expenditure: first of PaymentsToAcquirePropertyPlantAndEquipment,
      PaymentsToAcquireProductiveAssets, PaymentsForCapitalImprovements,
      PaymentsToAcquireOtherPropertyPlantAndEquipment; missing if none is reported. Capitalized
      software: PaymentsForSoftware plus PaymentsToDevelopSoftware. Lease-financed capacity:
      RightOfUseAssetObtainedInExchangeForFinanceLeaseLiability.
    • Stock-based compensation: ShareBasedCompensation; otherwise
      AllocatedShareBasedCompensationExpense.
    • Selling and marketing: SellingAndMarketingExpense. Used for Table 8 and for statistics quoted in
      Sections 5 to 8: GeneralAndAdministrativeExpense; ResearchAndDevelopmentExpense;
      GrossProfit, otherwise revenue minus CostOfRevenue or CostOfGoodsAndServicesSold.
    • Acquisitions: PaymentsToAcquireBusinessesNetOfCashAcquired or
      PaymentsToAcquireBusinessesGross; change in Goodwill;
      BusinessAcquisitionsProFormaRevenue.
    • Discontinued operations: IncomeLossFromDiscontinuedOperationsNetOfTax and its
      attributable-to-reporting-entity variant.

Cleaning. One firm-year with revenue more than ten times both neighboring years is dropped as a scale error.
Registrants that report revenue identical to within $0.1 million to another registrant’s in two or more fiscal years
are treated as duplicates, and the registrant with fewer firm-years is dropped (seven registrants); for three verified
predecessor–successor pairs that overlap in one year, the predecessor’s overlapping year is dropped.

Filters. The analysis sample requires fiscal years 2010 to 2025, prior-year revenue of at least $100 million (nominal)
and growth in (−0.9, 5), and sets margins outside [−3, 1] to missing. Quantile and fixed-effects regressions drop
growth above 150%.

Known limitations. SIC codes are each registrant’s current code. Foreign private issuers reporting under IFRS are
absent, and dollar growth of those reporting under US GAAP includes currency effects. Selling-and-marketing expense
includes amortization of capitalized contract costs and is reported separately in 90% of core firm-years. Operating-
lease capacity is not counted as capital. The $100 million threshold is nominal, so the median size of included firms
rises over time. Company-defined adjusted measures are not available in XBRL; our adjusted margin adds SBC to
GAAP operating income.

Additional tables. Table 14 reports the robustness checks of Section 5, and Table 15 the sensitivity of the edge
statistic that we do not use.

least g. Because the observed frontier falls by less than one point of margin per point of growth, the maximum sits at the edge of
the growth support, so the statistic is governed by a handful of firm-years and falls as the minimum cell size rises.


Reproducibility. The replication package contains the browser script used for retrieval (retrieval_browser.js)
and an equivalent Python script (fetch_companyfacts.py); the extracted facts and the list of registrants; the panel-
construction script (build_panel_v2.py) and the resulting panels; the estimation scripts (estimate_frontier.py,
estimate_revised.py, decompose_gap.py); the calibration script (calibrate_horizons.py); the script for
statistics quoted only in the text (paper_stats.py); the symbolic and numerical verification of the theory
(verify_theory.py); and the script for the theory figures (frontier_figures.py). Running the scripts in that
order regenerates every table and figure. Facts filed after our retrieval date will make a new retrieval differ slightly
from the distributed extract.


E      Human–AI Research Protocol
This appendix documents how the paper was produced. arXiv’s policy is that generative AI tools cannot be authors,
that authors must report significant use of such tools, and that authors take full responsibility for all content however it
was generated (arXiv, 2023, 2026). We go further than a disclosure statement because the collaboration was structured,
and its structure is part of what a reader needs to judge the work. The appendix records which agents contributed to
this paper’s content and checks, the review rounds, the decisions the author made and the errors that were caught, with
the safeguard that caught each. Its sources are the project’s agent log and errors log, which are part of the replication
package.

E.1     Agents and roles
The human author posed the research questions, set the scope, made every decision listed in Table 17, reviewed the
manuscript and is responsible for it. All AI work was done by agents built on Anthropic’s Claude models inside
one research session configured for the model identifier claude-opus-5-5; subagents inherited it. A lead agent
coordinated the work and was the only agent that communicated with the author. Where independence mattered, an
agent was given a distinct role and no access to the others’ outputs. The project later grew into three papers: this one,
a companion paper on the economics of AI transformation (Bangalore Sreenivasa Murthy, 2026), and a paper on the
software engine. Table 16 lists the agents whose work entered this paper; agents that worked only on the companion
paper or the engine are omitted.



                                                                 78
E.2    Sequence and review rounds
Question and council. The project began with a question the author put to the lead agent: taking the Rule of 40 and
its proposed successors, and assuming everything can be automated with AI, what is the theoretical limit of the score,
how does it evolve over two, five and ten years, and what conditions are needed to approach it. The author asked
for a council of agents. The lead agent gathered current evidence and drafted an answer; the three council members
critiqued it without seeing each other’s critiques. Their critiques changed the answer in four ways that survive in this
paper: growth and margin are linked through capital intensity (Proposition 5); very high scores can occur at large scale
during market transitions, not only at small bases; the measurement basis moves scores by more than operations do
(Section 8); and accountability costs scale with the damage a failure can cause rather than with headcount.
     First draft. The author then asked for the efficient frontier and for a paper suitable for arXiv. The lead agent
checked arXiv’s policies on AI authorship, endorsement and position papers before asking the author four scoping
questions, on the evidence base, authorship, affiliation and the documentation of the AI role. Data retrieval required
the author’s participation: the agents ran in a cloud environment that could not reach the SEC’s servers, so the author
granted the lead agent access to a folder and to the SEC’s websites in a browser on the author’s own computer (Ap-
pendix D). The first draft found a flat cross-sectional frontier; the lead agent returned the result to the theory agent,
which added Proposition 7 to show how a flat frontier of chosen points can coexist with a trade-off for every firm.
     Referee round. The referee panel and the audit agent reviewed the complete first draft, and all three referees
recommended major revision. Their reports changed the data (the panel was rebuilt as first reported), the statistics
(sample-size-invariant statistics replaced an edge statistic), a finding (the flat frontier reversed), the identification
(α became a calibration range), the theory (the dynamic weight of Proposition 6 and the firm-by-firm inversion of
Section 7) and several claims; Table 18 lists the errors. A second audit of the revised draft returned 33 findings (4
errors, 13 inconsistencies, 5 overclaims, 6 unsupported claims, 5 style issues), of which 29 were resolved at once and
the rest after a re-audit.
     Extension and integration. The author then asked for a model of AI transformation itself, which became the
companion paper, and asked that every constraint found there also act on the frontier. The integration theorist charged
the full costs of running AI against the ceiling, which moved the central growth threshold for a ceiling of 100 from
39% to 42%, added the attainable and expected frontiers and the generalization to economic profit, and rebuilt the
calibration, which moved the horizon ranges. The assumptions auditor’s register then required every headline number
that rests on a scenario value to be stated as conditional on it. The author decided to split the project into three
papers and to release this one first; the editor-author agent made it self-contained, replacing every dependence on the
companion paper either by a compact statement with a proof (Lemma 4, Proposition 10, equation (27)) or by a citation
where the argument does not depend on the result.
     Choice of estimand. After the robustness results were known, the primary sample for the model-facing results
was changed from all firm-years to the organic frontier, to align the estimand with the theory, whose growth is bought
with expensed spend within one market, and with the calibration, which already used an organic sample (Section 4.4).
The author asked what rigor requires; the lead agent set the two estimands. The conclusions under both samples are
reported. Three conclusions of the earlier draft changed: the GAAP upper-tail slope is steeper on the organic frontier
(−0.59 against −0.46), and the cash-basis size gradient and the steepening of the cash-basis slope after 2020 do not
survive once acquired growth is removed. The calibration anchor was then switched to the organic frontier, with
observed scores kept as the comparison; the median growth surplus of the top tail at the upper bound on growth spend
fell from 7 to −1 points, so the claim that the best scores were partly transient now holds only at the lower bound.

E.5    Errors caught and the safeguards that caught them
Table 18 lists the errors that affected this paper’s content, grouped by the safeguard that caught them. Errors in work
that entered only the companion paper or the engine are recorded in those papers.
     Two errors were not caught by a safeguard but by reversing course openly. The first draft’s flat cross-sectional
frontier did not survive the rebuilt data, and the paper reports the reversal. And the primary sample for the model-facing
results was changed after the robustness results were known, as described above; both sets of results are reported.

E.6    Safeguards
Five safeguards address the known failure modes of AI-generated research. Fabricated citations were addressed by
dedicated verification passes with a recorded outcome for every reference. Errors in derivations were addressed by
a script that checks each closed form, first-order condition and envelope derivative symbolically and checks shape
properties numerically on random parameter draws (verify_theory.py, 168 checks), and by a referee who re-
derived the results independently. Errors in data handling were addressed by spot checks of the panel against filed
figures for named firms, by reporting several margin definitions, samples and estimands, and by a replication package.
Errors in quoted numbers were addressed by generating every number in the abstract, introduction, horizons section
and conclusion from the analysis outputs (paper1_macros.py). Confirmation bias was addressed by independent
critics who did not see one another’s work, by an assumptions register that classifies every parameter by its grounding,
and by reporting results that contradicted earlier drafts.

E.7    What the protocol does not establish
Independent agents built on the same underlying model may share blind spots, so their agreement is weaker evidence
than agreement among human experts would be. Several parameters remain scenario values, and the review panels on
this version are recorded above when they are complete. The author’s review is the final check, and responsibility for
any remaining error is the author’s.
